For generations, the Dashwood family had made their home in the rolling green countryside of Sussex, where their sprawling Norland Park estate sat at the heart of thousands of acres of ancient oak woods, wild meadow, and well-tended pasture. Their name was synonymous with quiet, generous respectability across the whole surrounding shire, and neighbors for miles around spoke of the Dashwoods’ hospitality as something to be cherished.

The last lifelong sole owner of Norland had been a quiet bachelor who lived well into his eighties, his only constant companion and housekeeper for most of his long life his unmarried younger sister. When she died ten years before him, the old house felt hollow and empty, so he invited his nephew, Mr. Henry Dashwood — the legal heir to the Norland estate, and the man he had long intended to leave it to — to move his whole family in. Suddenly the quiet old walls echoed with the sound of children’s laughter, the soft clatter of teacups at social hours, and the warm, easy cheer of a full household. The old gentleman’s days grew soft and comfortable, and his affection for every member of his nephew’s family deepened year by year.

Mr. Henry Dashwood had one son from his first marriage, a steady, respectable young man named John, who had already been very well provided for by his birth mother’s large fortune: half of it became his the moment he turned twenty-one, and soon after he added even more to his wealth by marrying a woman of equal fortune. For him, inheriting Norland was no life-changing windfall — but for his three half-sisters, the daughters Henry had with his current beloved wife, it was the whole promise of future security. Their mother had no fortune of her own, and Henry only held a life interest in the remaining half of his first wife’s money, which would pass entirely to John upon his death, leaving him with just seven thousand pounds that he could actually leave to his widow and three girls.

When the old gentleman died and his will was read, it brought nearly as much disappointment as it did relief. He had not been ungrateful enough to leave the estate away from Henry entirely — but he had tied it up so tightly, to pass straight from Henry to John, and then to John’s own four-year-old little son, that Henry had no power at all to carve out any share of the land or its valuable timber to secure a future for his wife and daughters. The whole estate was locked away for that small boy, who had won his great-uncle’s late affection over the occasional summer visits: the tot’s stammered mispronunciations, stubborn determination to get his own way, silly little tricks, and endless noisy high spirits had somehow come to outweigh years of steady, loving care from Henry Dashwood, his wife, and their three daughters. The old man had not meant to be cruel, though — he left each of the three Dashwood girls a single thousand pounds, a small token of his fondness.

Henry Dashwood’s disappointment was sharp at first, but he was a naturally cheerful, sanguine man. He told himself he had decades of life ahead of him, and if he managed the large, productive estate frugally, he could easily set aside a large sum of money over the years to make his wife and daughters perfectly comfortable. But fortune never gave him the chance: he survived his uncle by barely twelve months. When he died, all that was left for his widow and three girls was ten thousand pounds total, counting the small legacies the old gentleman had left them.

The moment his illness turned dangerous, Henry sent for his son John, and with all the weak, urgent strength he had left, he begged him to take care of his stepmother and his three half-sisters, to do everything he could to make their lives easy and happy. John Dashwood was not a cruel man, exactly — just cold-hearted, a little selfish, the sort of man who was well-respected in public because he never failed to fulfill his most basic social duties. At his father’s deathbed, he was truly moved, and he promised to look after the three girls as thoroughly as he could.

As soon as his father was buried, though, John’s narrow-minded, equally selfish wife packed up their little boy, all their servants, and moved into Norland Park without so much as a single note of warning sent ahead to the grieving widow. Legally, the house was theirs the moment Henry drew his last breath, but the indelicacy of the act stung deeply. Mrs. Dashwood, the widow, had a keen, romantic sense of honor, and the unapologetic disregard for her comfort filled her with such disgust that she nearly packed her bags and left Norland forever that very day — only the gentle, steady pleading of her eldest nineteen-year-old daughter convinced her to stay, for the sake of her three girls.

That eldest daughter, Elinor, was the steadying force of the whole household. She had a sharp, clear judgment, and the rare gift of being able to govern her own strong, affectionate feelings, a skill neither her mother nor her middle sister possessed. Time and time again, she would step in to stop her mother’s impulsive, passion-fueled decisions that would have led the whole family into ruin.

Marianne, the middle daughter, was just as clever and warm-hearted as Elinor, but every feeling she held was unmoderated, untamed: her joys were so bright they could make the whole room glow, her sorrows so sharp they could overwhelm her completely. Generous, soft, endlessly romantic, she was everything but prudent — and she and her mother were so alike in their sensibilities that they fed each other’s grief after Henry’s death, deliberately lingering on every sad, precious memory, refusing to accept any small comfort that might soften the sharp edge of their loss. Elinor grieved just as deeply as they did, but she forced herself to push through the pain: she met her rude sister-in-law with polite civility, mediated calmly with her brother, and worked tirelessly to keep the peace for the whole family.

The youngest sister, Margaret, was thirteen, good-humored and sweet-natured, but she had absorbed every bit of Marianne’s romantic whimsy without inheriting half her common sense, lost most days in the soft, vivid daydreams that made up the world inside her head.

Mrs. John Dashwood had fully settled into her role as mistress of Norland Park, her silk gown swishing past hallways that for years had belonged to her husband’s stepmother and half-sisters, who were now no more than tolerated guests in the home they had once loved as their own. Fanny treated them, for the most part, with a cool, unruffled civility that never strayed into warmth; John, for his part, showed them as much kindness as he was capable of feeling for anyone who was not himself, his adored wife, or their chubby, golden-haired little son Harry. He even urged them, with a show of earnest concern, to treat Norland as their own home for as long as they pleased — and since Mrs. Dashwood could not bear the thought of rushing away from the place where every tree, every window seat, every rose bush in the garden held memories of her late husband and years of unshadowed joy, she accepted the invitation.

It was exactly the kind of soft, lingering space her heart craved. In times of happiness, no temper could be brighter than hers, no one could hold that bright, sanguine expectation of coming joy that feels like happiness all on its own. But in sorrow, she was equally swept away by the power of her own affections, as far past all consolation now as she had once been past every possible hint of bitterness when her days were good.

Fanny Dashwood did not remotely approve of the promise her husband had made his father on his deathbed: to gift his three half-sisters three thousand pounds from the family fortune. To take that vast sum away from their dear little Harry would be to impoverish him beyond all repair, she insisted, curling herself close to John by their private parlour fire, little Harry playing with a wooden soldier at her feet. She begged him to reconsider. How could he live with himself to rob his own only child of so much wealth? What possible claim could the Miss Dashwoods — related to him only by half blood, which Fanny dismissed as barely counting as relationship at all — have on such extravagant generosity? Everyone knew children of different marriages never felt any real affection for one another, why on earth would he ruin himself, and their precious little boy, by throwing away their money on girls who were barely more than strangers?

“It was my father’s last request to me,” John protested. “That I look after his widow and daughters, make their situation easier.”
“He had no idea what he was saying, I’d wager,” Fanny sniffed. “Nine times out of ten he was light-headed from fever at the time. If he’d been in his right mind, he never would have dreamed of begging you to give away half of Harry’s inheritance.”
“He never named a specific sum, my dear Fanny,” he said. “He only told me, in general terms, to assist them, to make their lives more comfortable than he could. I daresay it would have been wiser if he’d left the whole matter to my own discretion. He could never have thought I would neglect them. But he asked me for the promise, and I could not refuse it — not then, at least. The promise is made, and I must keep it. I must do something for them when they leave Norland and move to a new home nearby.”

“Very well, do something,” Fanny said smoothly. “But that something does not need to be three thousand pounds. Think: once that money is gone, it never comes back. Your sisters will marry, and it will vanish into the hands of strangers forever. If only it could be returned to our poor little Harry, one day —”
“Why, to be sure,” John said, his face growing grave. “That would make all the difference. The day may come when Harry regrets that so large a sum was given away. If he has a large family of his own one day, that money would be a great comfort to him.”
“Exactly so!”
“Then perhaps it would be better for everyone if the sum was cut in half. Five hundred pounds apiece would be a wonderful addition to their fortunes!”
“Oh, that is beyond generous!” Fanny cried, patting his arm. “What brother on earth would do half as much even for full blood sisters — and these are only half related! You have such a noble, generous spirit, my love.”
“I would never want to do anything unkind or mean,” he said. “One would rather do too much than too little on such occasions. No one can say I did not do right by them. Even they can hardly expect more.”
“There is no telling what they might expect,” Fanny said, “but we need not care about their expectations. The question is what you can afford to give.”
“Quite right. I think I can easily afford five hundred pounds a piece. Even without a penny from me, each of them will come into three thousand pounds when their mother passes. That is a perfectly respectable, comfortable fortune for any young woman.”
“Of course it is! All told, the four of them will have ten thousand pounds between them. If they marry, they will do perfectly well for themselves. If they never marry, they can live very comfortably together on the interest of that sum. They want for nothing more.”
“True enough,” John said, nodding slowly. “Perhaps it would be wiser, then, to do something for their mother while she lives, rather than give the girls lump sums. An annuity, I mean — a hundred pounds a year. That would make all four of their lives far easier.”

Fanny hesitated a moment before she answered. “That is better than giving away fifteen hundred pounds all at once, to be sure. But what if Mrs. Dashwood lives another fifteen years? We would be trapped paying it all that time, completely swindled!”
“Fifteen years, my dear Fanny? She is hardly forty, but her health cannot be worth half that purchase.”
“Have you not noticed? People always live forever when there is an annuity to be paid them,” Fanny insisted. “An annuity is a terrible burden: it comes round every single year, over and over, and there is no getting rid of it. You do not know what you are agreeing to. I know all the trouble they cause — my father left my mother paying three annuities to superannuated old servants, and it was dreadful. Twice a year she had to trek across the county to deliver the money, there was endless hassle when one of them was rumoured dead only to turn up very much alive a month later. My mother was sick of the whole business. She said her fortune was never her own, not with those constant, unescapable demands on it. It is so unfair — if it were not for those annuities, all that money would be hers to spend as she pleased. It has given me such a horror of annuities, I would never tie myself down to paying one, not for anything in the world.”
“It is very unpleasant, to be sure,” John agreed. “To have those constant drains on your income, to be forced to set aside the same sum every rent day, it robs you of your independence entirely, just as your mother says.”
“Exactly! And you get no thanks for it, either. They think it is their right, they never feel grateful. If I were you, every penny I gave them would be at my own full discretion. I would never bind myself to pay them a yearly sum. Some years we may be hard pressed to spare a hundred, or even fifty, pounds from our own household expenses.”

“I believe you are right, my love,” John said, convinced. “An annuity is not the way to go. Occasional gifts of money will help them far more than a fixed allowance — if they knew a hundred pounds a year was coming in, they would just start spending more, living above their means, and end up no richer at the end of the twelve months. A fifty pound gift now and then will keep them from ever being short of money, and that will be more than enough to fulfill my promise to my father.”
“Of course it will!” Fanny said, smiling. “I am utterly convinced your father never meant for you to give them any actual money at all. The assistance he had in mind was only the kind of help that is naturally expected of you: help them hunt out a nice small cottage nearby, lend a hand moving their furniture over, send over extra hare or game from the estate larder when it is in season, a basket of apples from our orchard, that sort of thing. I will wager my life he never meant anything more than that — it would be absurd and unreasonable if he did. Think, my dear John! Between the interest of their seven thousand pounds, plus the thousand pounds each girl already has of her own, their income comes out to fifty pounds a year per girl, and of course they will pay their mother for their board out of that. All told, they will have five hundred pounds a year between four of them. On earth, what could four quiet gentlewomen possibly need more than that? They will live so cheaply! No carriage, no horses, barely two servants, no parties or company to entertain. Their housekeeping costs will be next to nothing. You cannot imagine how comfortably they will live! Five hundred a year? I cannot see how they will ever spend half of it. The idea of giving them more is completely ridiculous. They will be far better off than we are, in many ways — they will probably end up being able to give us gifts, not the other way round.”

“Upon my word,” John said, fully persuaded now, “I believe you are perfectly right. My father could never have meant anything more than that by his request. I see that now. I will keep my promise perfectly, by offering them that small acts of kindness and support you described. When my mother moves to her new cottage, I will gladly help her settle in as much as I can. A few small pieces of leftover furniture, that will be all they could possibly need.”
“Of course,” Fanny agreed. “And there is one more thing to consider: when your father and mother first moved to Norland, they sold all the furniture from their old home at Stanhill, but they kept all their china, silver plate, and linen, and all of that is left to your mother in the will. Her new cottage will be almost fully furnished the day she steps through the door.”
“That is a very important point, a very fine legacy indeed! Though some of that silver plate would have made a lovely addition to our own, here at Norland.”
“Yes, and their breakfast china set is twice as fine as the one we use in this house. Far too grand, in my opinion, for any small cottage they could ever afford to rent. But there it is — your father thought of no one but them. And I will say this: you owe him no particular gratitude, no obligation to follow his wishes blindly. We all know if he could have had his way, he would have left every last penny he owned to them, and left our poor Harry with nothing at all.”

That argument was impossible to push back against. It gave John the last little bit of firm resolve he had been missing, and he finally decided that it would be utterly unnecessary, if not outright ungentlemanly and indecorous, to do anything more for his father’s widow and his half-sisters than the small, neighbourly little favours his wife had outlined.

For months after her husband’s passing, Mrs. Dashwood lingered at Norland Park—though not out of any fondness for the sharp new edge of Fanny’s household, or any reluctance to leave once the first ragged, searing grief of seeing every well-loved path, every old apple orchard, every window where she’d watched sunsets with her husband softened into something she could bear. When her spirits began to lift, and she found she could think of something other than wrapping herself in melancholic memories to feed her sorrow, she grew restless to move, chasing every lead on a small, cozy home within a few miles of Norland. She could not bear the thought of fleeing the land that had been the heart of her marriage, the only home her daughters had known for years. But no house seemed to strike the perfect balance between the warm, easy comfort she craved, and the modest prudence her eldest daughter Elinor insisted on; Elinor’s steady, level head turned down half the places her mother loved, pointing out they were far too large for their limited income.

Mr. Dashwood had made his wife swear he would not be forgotten on his deathbed, telling her of the solemn, earnest promise he’d wrung from their son John to care for his stepmother and half-sisters. That promise had been the last comfort of his dying hours, and Mrs. Dashwood trusted John’s sincerity no less than her husband had. She thought of the seven thousand pounds he’d pledged to set aside for the girls with quiet satisfaction, certain she herself could live in perfect ease on a far smaller sum—but the money would give her daughters the security they deserved. She even scolded herself for ever doubting John’s generosity, for thinking him too selfish to care for his half-sisters; his gentle attentions, his constant little gestures of care for them all, had convinced her that their welfare was dear to his heart, and for a long while she never once questioned he would keep his word.

The quiet contempt she’d felt for her daughter-in-law Fanny from their first meeting only deepened over their six months living under the same roof. It would have been impossible to stay in the same house a day longer, even for the sake of politeness, if a new, soft, sweet circumstance had not arisen that made Mrs. Dashwood glad her girls were still at Norland: Elinor had grown deeply attached to Fanny’s younger brother, Edward Ferrars.

Edward was a gentle, charming young gentleman who had come to stay at Norland not long after Fanny moved in as its new mistress, and he’d spent nearly every day there ever since. Some mothers would have chased the pair apart at once, for all Edward owned in his own right was a tiny, trifling legacy—every other part of his fortune depended entirely on the whims of his very wealthy, very headstrong mother. Other mothers would have pushed the match relentlessly, for he was the eldest son of one of the richest men in the county. But Mrs. Dashwood cared nothing for either consideration. It was enough for her that Edward was kind, that he looked at Elinor as if she outshone every other person in the room, and that Elinor returned his quiet fondness tenfold. She thought it a cruel wrong that difference in fortune should ever keep two people apart whose hearts and temperaments fit so perfectly together, and she could not fathom how anyone who knew Elinor could fail to recognize her quiet, shining merit.

Edward was not the sort of young man to win over strangers at first glance. He was not strikingly handsome, and his manners were so shy that new acquaintances often mistook him for dull. Too diffident to show off his own strengths, he had to be drawn out of his shell before the warmth of his open, affectionate heart shone through. His mind was sharp, honed by years of solid, careful education—but he had neither the ambition nor the disposition to live up to the grand plans his mother and sister had laid out for him. They ached to see him rise to fame, to make a grand splash in high society, though they could never quite agree on what sort of fame that ought to be. His mother wanted to nudge him into politics, get him a seat in Parliament, or tie him to some great, powerful lord of the day. Fanny wanted that too—but until such a grand position could be secured, she would have been perfectly happy just to see him driving a fancy, grand barouche through the streets of London. Edward wanted none of those things. All his heart desired was a quiet home, the comfort of his family, and the soft peace of private life. Fortunately for his family’s ambitions, he had a younger brother Robert who was far more eager for glitter, status, and fine carriages.

Edward had been staying at Norland for weeks before he caught Mrs. Dashwood’s notice; in those early months of grief, she was too wrapped up in her own sorrow to pay much mind to the new guest. She only saw that he was quiet, unobtrusive, and she liked him for it—he never pestered her with ill-timed small talk that would have cut through the fragile quiet she needed to grieve. It was a passing remark Elinor made one day about how very different Edward was from his sister Fanny that made Mrs. Dashwood sit up and take full notice of him. The contrast was all the recommendation he needed.
“To say he is not like Fanny is more than enough,” she said, smiling. “That implies every amiable quality in the world. I love him already.”
“I think you will grow very fond of him, once you know him better,” Elinor said softly.
“Fond of him?” her mother laughed. “I do not feel mere approval. I already hold him in my heart as someone I love.”
“You may at least esteem him,” Elinor teased.
“I have never in my life been able to separate esteem from love,” her mother replied.

After that, Mrs. Dashwood made a point of getting to know Edward. Her warm, easy, open manner melted away his shyness in no time, and she soon saw every bit of his merit. The quiet certainty that he loved Elinor no doubt helped her see his goodness all the faster, but she was truly convinced of his worth: even that quiet, reserved demeanor that once felt odd, against her usual preference for lively, sparking young men, felt perfectly charming once she knew his heart was warm and his temper was gentle. The moment she saw the first soft signs of his love for Elinor in the way he looked at her, the way he hovered near her side at dinner, she was certain their attachment was serious, and began to daydream of their fast-approaching wedding.

“In just a few months, my dear Marianne,” she told her second daughter one afternoon, “Elinor will almost certainly be settled for life. We will miss her dearly—but she will be so very happy.”
“Oh Mama, how will we ever get on without her?” Marianne cried.
“My love, it will hardly feel like a separation at all,” Mrs. Dashwood said, stroking her hair. “We will live only a few miles apart, and we will see each other every single day. You will gain a brother, a true, affectionate brother. I have the highest possible opinion of Edward’s heart.” She paused, noticing Marianne’s pensive, grave face. “You look so serious, my dear. Do you disapprove of your sister’s choice?”

Marianne hesitated. “Edward is very amiable. I love him tenderly. But there is something missing. He is not the kind of young man I would expect to win Elinor’s whole heart. His bearing is not striking, he has none of that easy grace that ought to shine in the man she loves. His eyes have none of that spark, that fire, that announces a soul full of virtue and deep, bright intelligence. And besides, I fear he has no real taste for the things that matter most to us. Music hardly seems to interest him at all, and though he praises Elinor’s drawings over and over, his admiration is not that of a connoisseur who can see every stroke of her skill. It is obvious, even when he sits and watches her draw for hours, that he knows nothing of art at all. He admires the drawings only because he loves her, not because he feels their beauty. For me, those two things must go hand in hand. I could never be happy with a man whose tastes did not match mine in every single way. He must feel all my feelings. The same books, the same music, the same wild rapture at a sunset must charm us both. Oh Mama—how flat, how tame his voice was when he read poetry to us last night! I felt so sorry for Elinor, I could hardly sit still in my chair. To hear those beautiful lines of Cowper that have so often made my own heart beat wild with feeling, spoken with such calm, such dreadful, unfeeling indifference!”

“He would have done far better with simple, elegant prose,” Mrs. Dashwood said, laughing gently. “I thought that at the time, but you insisted on handing him Cowper.”
“No, Mama—if a man cannot feel Cowper, what is there to say? We must allow for difference of taste, I suppose. Elinor does not feel things as deeply as I do, so she can overlook that flatness and be happy. But if I loved a man, hearing him read my favorite poetry with no passion at all would break my heart. The more I see of the world, the more I am convinced I will never meet a man I can truly love. I require so much! He must have all Edward’s kindness and goodness, and his bearing and spirit must drape that goodness in every possible grace and fire.”

“Remember, my love, you are not quite seventeen yet,” Mrs. Dashwood said, pulling her close. “It is far too early to despair of that happiness. Why should you be less fortunate than your own mother? In only one thing, my Marianne, I hope your destiny will be different from mine: I hope you get to keep the man you love for far longer than I did.”

Elinor was turning the corner of a half-finished landscape sketch in the drawing room when Marianne sighed, pressing her shoulder to the windowsill overlooking Norland’s wooded park. “What a cruel, terrible pity it is, Elinor,” she said, earnest as ever, “that Edward should have no taste for drawing at all.”

“No taste for drawing?” Elinor repeated, a faint, warm flush rising high on her cheeks that she tried to hide by adjusting the sketch in her lap. “Whatever makes you think that? He does not draw himself, it is true, but he lights up at the sight of other people’s work, and I assure you his natural taste is anything but lacking—he simply never had the chance to nurture it. If he had ever been taught properly, I believe he would have drawn beautifully. He doubts his own judgment so much he hates to speak his mind on any piece of art, but he has an innate, simple, right sense of what is good that almost never leads him wrong.”

Marianne held her tongue, afraid to offend, but she could not reconcile the quiet, polite appreciation Elinor described with the rapturous, soul-shaking delight she believed was the only true mark of taste. She smiled privately at what she thought was her sister’s gentle fond blind spot, even as she honored Elinor for loving Edward so well she would defend his smallest virtues.

“I hope you do not think his general taste is lacking, Marianne,” Elinor went on, teasing softly. “I think I can safely say you do not, for you are always perfectly warm and kind to him. If you truly thought him dull or unfeeling, I know you would never suffer to be civil to him for a minute.”

Marianne fumbled for words. She would never wound her sister for the world, but she could not lie and say she saw in Edward all the tiny, fiery preferences of art and poetry that lit up her own soul. At last she said, “Do not be cross with me, Elinor, if my praise for him does not match every bit of your high opinion. I have not spent the hours you have, picking through his quiet little likes and dislikes, but I think he is the kindest, most sensible man I have ever met. There is no one more worthy of being loved.”

Elinor laughed, soft and relieved. “His dearest friends could not ask for warmer praise than that. I have no right to ask for more.” Marianne was delighted to see her sister so easily pleased.

“Of his sense and his goodness no one who has spoken to him at length could ever doubt,” Elinor continued. “His sharp understanding and steady principles are only hidden by that shyness of his that keeps him quiet half the time. You see enough of him to know how solid and good he is, but you have been so busy doting on mother these past months, I have spent far more time in his company. We have talked for hours about books, about poetry, about all the small things that make a mind feel alive. His imagination is bright, his eye for the world is sharp, his taste is delicate and pure. The more you know him, the better he gets—his manners, his whole face, unfurl the longer you are with him. At first glance he is not striking, not at all, but once you notice how kind and bright his eyes are, how sweet his smile is? I think he is very nearly handsome now. Do you not see it, Marianne?”

“I will think him handsome soon enough,” Marianne promised. “When you marry him, and he is my brother, I will not see a single flaw in his face any more than I see a flaw in his heart now.”

Elinor started at the words, suddenly quiet. She had let her warmth for Edward carry her away, and now she realized Marianne and their mother had already woven a whole future for the two of them, without a single solid promise between them. She knew their natures: what they guessed one minute, they believed the next, and what they hoped for, they expected as a certainty. “I will not pretend I do not think very highly of him,” she said gently. “That I esteem him, that I like him very much.”

Marianne burst out in indignation at once. “Esteem him? Like him? Cold-hearted Elinor! Worse than cold-hearted! You are ashamed to say you love him, and if you use those tame, frigid words one more time I will walk straight out of this room!”

Elinor could not help laughing. “Forgive me,” she said. “I did not mean to hurt you by speaking so quietly of my own feelings. Believe they are far stronger than I have said—strong enough to match his goodness, and the quiet, cautious hope that he might care for me too. But do not go further than that. I am not at all sure of his regard. Some days it feels thin, uncertain, and until I know for certain how he feels, I will not let myself pretend our attachment is something more than it is. There is far more to worry over than just whether he likes me. He is not independent, not at all. We do not know Mrs. Ferrars, his mother, but from Fanny’s little offhand remarks about her, we know she is sharp, greedy, and determined both her sons marry women of great fortune and high rank. Edward knows perfectly well there would be every kind of obstacle in his path if he tried to marry a girl with no money, no connections, like us.”

Marianne stared, stunned, to realize she and her mother’s romantic imaginings had raced so far ahead of the hard, unforgiving truth.

“And you are not secretly engaged to him?” she said. “But it will be, soon, I know it. At least this wait has two good sides: I will not lose you as a sister quite so soon, and Edward will have all the time in the world to nurture that taste for drawing you know he has hidden away. If your love could spur him to learn to paint, how perfectly delightful that would be for the rest of your lives!”

Elinor had spoken her whole, honest truth at last. She knew her regard for Edward was not as secure as Marianne and her mother had thought. Some days he was so quiet, so heavy-spirited, that it felt like more than simple doubt of her affection weighing on him—like the knowledge of his mother’s unforgiving rules was already dragging him down. She could not rest easy about their future, not when he was trapped in a home that would never let him be happy on his own terms. Sometimes, for a few sharp, painful minutes, she feared his affection was nothing more than friendship.

But that quiet fondness had not escaped Edward’s sister Fanny. She seized the first chance to corner Mrs. Dashwood in the breakfast room, dropping hint after hint about her brother’s vast expected inheritance, about Mrs. Ferrars’s unshakable resolve that both her sons make brilliant, wealthy marriages, about how any silly, penniless girl who tried to “trap” Edward would be ruined by the Ferrars family for good. Mrs. Dashwood’s temper flamed hot, she snapped a sharp, scathing reply to Fanny, and stormed out of the room vowing she would not let her beloved Elinor be insulted under this roof for one more week.

It was at that exact moment the post arrived, carrying a letter perfectly timed to her anger. It was from Sir John Middleton, a distant relative of hers, a wealthy, good-natured gentleman who owned a large estate in Devonshire. He offered her a small, cozy cottage on his land, at a rent so low it was barely worth naming, and swore he would make every alteration and repair she could possibly want to make it comfortable. He begged her to come visit his own home, Barton Park, to see for herself if Barton Cottage, a mile down the lane in the same parish, would suit her. The letter was brimming with genuine kindness, exactly the thing a woman who had been stung by the cold cruelty of her own in-laws needed most.

Before that morning, Devonshire, hundreds of miles away from their beloved Norland, would have felt like an unbearable exile. Now it was the best news she could have received. To leave Norland was no longer a grief to mourn—it was a blessing, far better than staying under Fanny Dashwood’s roof, far better than watching the home she had loved for so many years belong to a woman who hated her. She wrote back to Sir John at once, accepting his offer with deep gratitude, then ran to show her daughters the letter to win their approval.

Elinor had long thought it would be far wiser for them to move somewhere far from Norland, rather than stay close by all their old acquaintances and be constantly reminded of what they had lost. She had no cause to argue. The cottage was small, the rent absurdly gentle, and though the thought of leaving Norland, leaving Edward, tugged sharp at her heart, she did not say a word to dissuade her mother from sending the acceptance.

No sooner had Mrs. Dashwood sealed and dispatched her reply accepting Sir John Middleton’s offer of Barton Cottage, than she could not hold back her bright, relieved news any longer. She announced to her stepson John Dashwood and his wife, alongside quiet Edward Ferrars who was staying with them, that she had secured a home at last, and would not be a burden on their household a single day longer than it took to pack their belongings and move.
The pair stared back in unmasked surprise. Mrs. John Dashwood (Fanny, as everyone who knew her well named her) said not a single word, her lips pressing thin, but her husband made a show of civil courtesy, saying he hoped they would not settle too far from Norland Park for easy visits. Mrs. Dashwood smiled, brimming with quiet satisfaction, and answered that they were bound for Devonshire.
Edward turned toward her in that instant so fast his half-full teacup nearly sloshed over its rim. His face was soft with unmistakeable, startled concern, no explanation needed for Elinor, who watched him from across the room, to understand he was thinking of the miles that would stretch between them. “Devonshire!” he repeated, his voice quiet and warm with dismay. “Are you truly going there? So very far from here? What part of the shire do you mean?”
She explained the location: the small cottage stood four miles north of Exeter, tucked into the rolling green Devonshire hills. “It is only a little place,” she went on, “but there is more than enough room for those I love. We can add an extra two rooms easily enough, and any friend who is willing to travel so far to see us will never want for a warm welcome, or a bed, or a place at our table.”
She finished with a kind invitation for Mr. and Mrs. John Dashwood to come stay at Barton as soon as they were settled, and then turned to Edward with an even softer, more affectionate invitation that left no room for protest. Though her recent sharp conversation with Fanny had convinced her to leave Norland as quickly as possible, it had not changed her resolve a single bit: she would never do anything to separate Elinor and Edward, and this open, pointed invitation to Fanny’s own brother was her quiet way of showing Fanny that her petty disapproval of the match meant nothing at all.
John Dashwood reiterated over and over how terribly sorry he was that they had chosen a home so far away, that he would not be able to lend a hand moving all their furniture. He truly did feel quietly, conveniently vexed: the small, token act of help he had limited himself to promising his father on his deathbed was now perfectly, comfortably impossible to fulfill.
All their belongings were loaded onto a barge to be sent west by water: stacks of soft household linen they had used for years, the family silver, well-loved chipped china, shelves of treasured books, and the handsome new pianoforte Marianne had saved nearly a year of her pin money to buy. Fanny watched every crate carted off with a dramatic little sigh, deeply resentful that Mrs. Dashwood, with her tiny, modest income compared to their own, should be allowed to keep such fine, expensive things for herself and her girls.
Mrs. Dashwood had taken the cottage on a full twelve-month lease, it came fully furnished, and they could move in the very day they arrived. No disagreements over the terms delayed them, and with Mrs. Dashwood’s characteristic speed when it came to things she cared deeply about, every last arrangement was sorted in no time. The horses left to her by her late husband had been sold not long after his death, and when a fair offer came in for their old family carriage, Elinor gently persuaded her mother to sell that too, even though Mrs. Dashwood would have kept it for the comfort of her girls if she had followed her own heart. Elinor’s good sense also trimmed their household staff down to just three servants: two maids and one man, all long-time trusted members of the old Norland household they had known for years.
The man and one of the maids were sent ahead straight to Devonshire to air the cottage linens, light the fires, and get the place ready for their arrival. Mrs. Dashwood did not know Lady Middleton at all, and much preferred to walk straight into her own home as its mistress than impose as a guest at grand Barton Park first. She trusted Sir John’s glowing descriptions of the cottage completely, and felt no need to see it before she stepped over its threshold as its owner.
Her eagerness to leave Norland never faded, not even a little, especially when she saw how openly delighted Fanny was at the prospect of their departure — a delight Fanny only bothered to hide behind a cold, half-hearted suggestion that they could stay a few more weeks if they wished.
This, of course, would have been the perfect time for John Dashwood to make good on the promise he had made to his dying father, to give his stepmother and half-sisters a little sum of money to make their new life comfortable. But Mrs. Dashwood soon gave up all hope of that kindness, and realized from every word he said that he never intended to do more than let them live in his house for free for six months. He talked constantly of how expensive running a large estate was, how many endless demands there were on his purse from every neighbor and acquaintance who expected parties and gifts and favors, until he sounded more like a man on the edge of ruin than a wealthy heir to a grand property, who never intended to give away a single penny of his own money.
Only a few short weeks after Sir John Middleton’s first letter arrived at Norland, every last arrangement was finalised, and Mrs. Dashwood and her three daughters were ready to begin their long journey west.
They shed so many tears over their final goodbyes to the place they had loved so deeply for so many years. On the very last evening they would ever spend at Norland, Marianne wandered alone through the grounds, trailing her fingers over the bark of every old familiar oak tree, her cheeks streaked with quiet sobs. “Dear, dear Norland!” she whispered to the quiet house behind her. “When will I ever stop grieving to leave you? When will I ever learn to call any other place home? Oh, happy house, if only you knew how my heart aches to look at you from this spot, the very last spot I may ever stand to see you from! And all of you, old well-loved trees — you will stay just the same. No leaf will fall early because we are gone, no branch will stop swaying in the wind even when we cannot see you anymore. You will go on, never noticing the joy or the sorrow you gave us, never caring that the people who walked and sang and read beneath your shade have gone away. But who will be left to love you now?”

The first stretch of their journey away from Norland unfolded under a heavy, cloud of quiet grief that made every mile drag slow and bitter. Elinor twisted a frayed linen handkerchief her mother had given her in her hands, Marianne’s cheek was streaked with silent tears as she stared unseeing at the passing hedgerows, and even Margaret, the youngest, was too heavy-hearted to tease her sisters. But as the road wound deeper into Devonshire, their new home crept into the edges of their attention, softening their dejection little by little. When Barton Valley first came into view, the sun broke through the thin late-summer cloud cover to gild the rolling green pastures, the ancient oak woods heavy with leaves, and the silver thread of a stream snaking through the fields, and the whole family breathed out at once. This was no cold, unwelcoming place.
A mile of winding lane later, their coach pulled to a stop before their new home. A tiny lush green courtyard made up the whole of the cottage’s front grounds, and a neat little wicket gate, painted soft cream, swung open to admit them. Barton Cottage was small, compact, and cozy enough to wrap around a person like a well-loved shawl, though it fell short of the dreamy ideal of a rustic cottage many might picture: its roof was neat and tiled, its window shutters were plain dark wood rather than the fashionable green, and no wild honeysuckle climbed its plaster walls, no matter how much they wished it would. A narrow front passage ran straight through the center of the house to the garden out back, with two sitting rooms each no more than sixteen feet square flanking the front door. Beyond those lay the kitchen and scullery, and the narrow staircase that led up to four small bedchambers and two garret rooms tucked under the eaves. The house was barely a dozen years old, and in perfect, solid repair.
Next to the grand, sprawling halls of Norland, it was impossibly tiny, impossibly humble — and for a heartbeat after they stepped over the threshold, the memory of their old home pricked hard enough to bring fresh tears. But the sound of their longtime servant’s warm, relieved laugh as she set down a tray of spiced cider dried those tears quickly. Each of them resolved, for the sake of the others, to make a happy home here. It was early September, the air sweet with ripening blackberries and clover, and the golden late summer sun flooded every room with warm light on their first day, giving them a gentle first impression that would make the cottage feel beloved long after the weather turned cold.
The location could hardly have been better. High, soft hills rose directly behind the cottage, rolling out to either side: some open, windswept downlands bright with gorse, others thick with trees and planted wheat fields. The village of Barton clung to the slope of one of the nearest hills, its little white church spire peeking over the rooftops to make a lovely view from every front window. From their front doorstep, the valley stretched wide for miles, rolling out into wilder, more distant country beyond.
Mrs. Dashwood, for all her soft heart, was already glowing with eager plans. Though her old life at Norland had made her used to far more grand furnishings, the prospect of decorating and improving their little home was pure joy to her, and she had enough saved to add every small touch of elegance the rooms currently lacked. “It’s true, it’s far too small for our little family,” she said, laughing as she ran a hand over the mantel in the front parlor, “but we’ll make it perfectly comfortable for now. It’s too late in the year for building work, but come spring, if I’ve set a little extra coin aside — and I’m sure I will — we can make all sorts of changes. These parlors are far too small for the dinner parties and musical evenings I hope to host here. We can knock out the old front passage, merge half of it into one parlor, add a bright new drawing room at the back, and a pretty new bedchamber and garret above it, and it will be the coziest little home in all Devonshire. I do wish the stairs were a little wider, of course, but we can’t have everything, and I daresay that’s an easy fix. We’ll map out all our plans once we see how the year’s savings add up.”
And for the weeks before that dream could ever be realized, on an income of only five hundred pounds a year for a woman who had never been good at pinching pennies, they were all perfectly happy to settle in to the house as it was. They spent the rest of the day unpacking, tucking their favorite books onto the small shelves, arranging their small personal treasures on the mantels to make the place feel like theirs. Marianne had her beloved pianoforte unpacked and pushed right up to the front window where the afternoon sun fell soft on the keys, and Elinor pinned all her carefully rendered watercolor sketches of Norland’s woods and wildflowers to the sitting room walls, so a little piece of their old home lived here too.
They were mid-breakfast the next morning when the front door was thrown open without ceremony, and their landlord stepped in to welcome them. Sir John Middleton was a handsome, rosy-cheeked man of around forty, all broad smiles and easy warmth. He had visited their old family home of Stanhill many years before, though the Dashwood girls had been far too small to remember him. His face was nothing but good humor, and his manners were every bit as friendly as the warm welcome letter he had sent them before they arrived. Their arrival seemed to give him genuine joy, and their comfort was his top priority. He talked at length of how much he hoped they would be the closest of neighbors, and pressed them so hard to dine at his home at Barton Park every single night until they were fully settled that even his over-eager persistence felt far more kind than rude.
His kindness did not end at words. Barely an hour after he left, a huge wicker basket arrived from the park, stuffed full of fresh garden peas, ripe plums, crisp apples, and sun-warmed strawberries. Before the sun set that same day, a second delivery arrived, full of game he had shot that morning. He insisted on carrying all their outgoing letters to the village post for them, and refused to let them turn down his daily newspaper, which he promised to send over every evening without fail.
He had also brought a very polite message from his wife, Lady Middleton, who said she would call on them the very first moment she was sure her visit would not be an inconvenience. Mrs. Dashwood sent back an equally warm invitation, and the very next day, Lady Middleton arrived at the cottage. They had all been curious to meet the woman whose goodwill would shape so much of their new life, and her tall, elegant figure, handsome face, and graceful manners met every bit of their hopes at first. She was no more than twenty-six or seven, dressed in a fine silk gown, with perfect polished poise. But that poise edged into cold reserve: for all her good breeding, she had none of her husband’s frank, warm spirit, and very little to say beyond the most generic, polite platitudes.
Conversation would have run dry very quickly, if she had not brought along her eldest child, Harry, a curly golden-haired little boy of six years old, who hung shyly behind his mother’s skirts with his face pressed to her leg. The ladies had no shortage of things to talk about then: they cooed over his beauty, asked his age, laughed at the silly rhymes Sir John insisted he knew by heart, and spent ten pleasant minutes playfully debating whether the boy favored his jolly, round-faced father or his slender, elegant mother, every one of them disagreeing on the point and pretending to be shocked by the others’ opinions.
By the end of the visit, Sir John would not leave without extracting a firm promise from the whole Dashwood family to come dine at Barton Park the very next day, and not a single one of them had the heart to say no.

Barton Park stood scarcely half a mile from the Dashwoods’ cottage. The ladies had often wandered near its grounds while walking the valley paths, but a broad, jutting shoulder of hill hid the great stone house entirely from view of their home. It was a large, handsome property, and the Middletons ran their household with equal parts unbridled hospitality and polished elegance — the former tailored perfectly to Sir John’s tastes, the latter to his lady’s. Their doors were seldom without guests, and they kept up a more brisk, varied social schedule than any other family in the whole neighbourhood.

This constant bustle was essential to both their happiness, for different as their temperaments and public manners were, they were perfectly matched in their total lack of cultivated talent or refined taste, which left them with little to fill their days outside the company of others. Sir John was a sportsman through and through: he lived for the hunt and the shoot, while Lady Middleton lived only to indulge her four rambunctious children. Those were their sole true pleasures, with Lady Middleton having the advantage of spoiling her darlings twelve months of the year, while Sir John’s favourite pastimes only occupied half the calendar. Still, endless social engagements at home and abroad more than made up for any gaps in their natural wit or formal education: they kept Sir John’s good spirits high, and gave Lady Middleton endless opportunities to show off her graceful, polished breeding.

Lady Middleton prided herself above all on the elegance of her dining table and every small domestic arrangement, and that quiet vanity was the source of her greatest joy at any of their parties. Sir John’s delight in company, though, was far more unfeigned: he loved cramming more young people into his rooms than his house could reasonably hold, and the louder and more lighthearted they were, the happier he was. He was a beloved figure to every young person in the neighbourhood, hosting unending summer picnics out on the downs piled high with cold ham and spiced chicken, and enough private winter balls to satisfy even the most dance-obsessed fifteen-year-old with an insatiable appetite for fun.

The arrival of a new family in the area was always a cause for great excitement for him, and he was utterly charmed by the new occupants of his Barton cottage. The Miss Dashwoods were young, pretty, and entirely unpretentious — that was more than enough to win his full approval, for he held that a pretty girl who was free of affectation had as captivating a mind as she did a face. His naturally warm, generous disposition made him eager to be kind to a family who had fallen so far from the comfort of their old home, and he got double the pleasure from having them as tenants: the genuine satisfaction of doing a good turn for his unfortunate cousins, plus the quiet delight of a sportsman who’d secured a fine new addition to his manor, with no competitive fellow huntsman to intrude on his favourite grounds.

Sir John met the Dashwoods at the very threshold of Barton Park, welcoming them with unfeigned, hearty sincerity. As he escorted them to the drawing room, he repeated to the young ladies the same regret he’d expressed the day before: he was gutted he hadn’t managed to round up a passel of smart, lively young bachelors to meet them. The only other gentleman staying in the house, besides himself, was an old, dear friend who was far past the first flush of youth and not nearly as playful as he wished he was. He begged them to forgive the smallness of their party, and swore it would never happen again. He’d called at half a dozen neighbouring houses that very morning trying to coax more guests over, but it was a bright full moon, it seemed, and every single family in the shire had a prior engagement. Luckily, Lady Middleton’s mother had arrived at Barton barely an hour before their own party, and she was such a cheerful, agreeable soul that he was sure the young ladies wouldn’t find the evening half so dull as they might fear.

Mrs. Dashwood and her daughters were perfectly happy to meet just two new strangers, and wanted no larger crowd at all. Mrs. Jennings, Lady Middleton’s mother, was a plump, merry, good-humoured elderly woman who talked a mile a minute, laughed freely, and was delightfully unrefined. She was full of silly jokes and raillery, and before dinner was even over she’d teased the girls no end about sweethearts and suitors, asking if they’d left their hearts tucked away somewhere back in Sussex, and pretending to spot blushes rising on their cheeks whether they coloured or not. Marianne, vexed on her sister’s behalf, kept glancing over at Elinor to see how she bore the silly jabs, and that earnest, concerned gaze of hers stung Elinor far more than Mrs. Jennings’ trivial, commonplace teasing ever could.

Colonel Brandon, Sir John’s resident friend, seemed no better suited to be his companion in manner than Lady Middleton was to be his wife, or Mrs. Jennings to be Lady Middleton’s own mother. He was quiet, reserved, and grave. His looks were not striking, but they were far from unpleasant, even to Marianne and Margaret, who at once labelled him an absolute confirmed old bachelor for being well past thirty-five. His face was thoughtful and sensible, and every one of his manners was marked by perfect gentlemanly grace.

There was little in the group that the Dashwoods would have naturally chosen as companions, but the cold, flat insipidity of Lady Middleton was so deeply unappealing that, by comparison, Colonel Brandon’s quiet gravity, even Sir John and Mrs. Jennings’ loud, unruly merriment, felt almost interesting. Lady Middleton seemed to rouse to genuine enjoyment only when her four noisy children burst into the drawing room after dinner, yanking at her sleeves, tearing at the hem of her gown, and putting an end to every other topic of conversation save the endless, lively anecdotes of their own antics.

Later that evening, when it was discovered Marianne was a gifted musician, they pressed her to play. The piano was unlocked, and everyone made a show of settling in to be charmed. Marianne, who sang beautifully, readily agreed to play through most of the scores Lady Middleton had brought with her when she married into the family — scores that had sat untouched on the piano stand for years, no doubt, for her ladyship had celebrated her wedding day by giving up music entirely, even though her mother swore she had once played exquisitely, and she herself claimed to be very fond of the art.

Marianne’s performance was met with loud, over-the-top applause. Sir John cheered raucously after every song, then chatted just as loudly to his guests through the next one. Lady Middleton would frequently scold him for rudeness, wondering how anyone could tear their attention away from music for even a moment — then turn right around and ask Marianne to replay the exact song she had just finished singing. Of all the party, only Colonel Brandon listened to her without any exaggerated, performative rapture. He gave her the only compliment that mattered: his full, unbroken attention. Marianne found herself feeling a genuine respect for him in that moment, a respect the rest of the party had fully forfeited with their shameless lack of taste. His love of music was not the wild, ecstatic, all-consuming passion that Marianne believed was the only true form of appreciation for the art, but it was still estimable, especially set against the total, horrible insensibility of everyone else in the room. She was reasonable enough to acknowledge that a man of five and thirty had long outlived the sharp, acute sensitivities and exquisite capacity for joy that belonged only to the very young, and she was perfectly willing to extend to Colonel Brandon every kindness and allowance that his advanced years, and his quiet courtesy, deserved.

Mrs. Jennings was a cheerful, widowed matron who’d inherited a generous, steady income from her late husband. She’d already seen both of her two daughters marry comfortably and settle into happy homes of their own, leaving her with no greater pastime than playing matchmaker for every young person in her wide circle of acquaintance. She threw herself into this little mission with relentless, warm zeal, never letting a chance to nudge two seemingly compatible hearts together slip by. She had a rare knack for spotting quiet crushes before the people involved had even named them to themselves, and many a shy young lady had blushed bright under her teasing, flattered to have her secret soft spot for some young gentleman called out.

It took barely an evening after her arrival at Barton for Mrs. Jennings to pronounce, with total certainty, that Colonel Brandon was head over heels in love with Marianne Dashwood. She’d first suspected it their very first night gathered round the fire, when the quiet colonel had sat utterly transfixed, hanging on every note Marianne sang for them all. Then when the Middletons came over to dine at the Dashwoods’ small cottage, her suspicion hardened into fact: he’d listened to Marianne sing just as intently again. It was undeniable. This was a perfect match, in her eyes — he was wealthy, she was bright and beautiful, what more could anyone ask for? She’d been eager to see the steady, thoughtful Colonel Brandon happily married ever since her connection to Sir John first introduced them, and she was always hunting for a kind, good husband for every pretty girl she crossed paths with.

This new pairing gave her endless material for gentle jokes, too. She teased the colonel mercilessly up at the Middleton park, and ribbed Marianne just as playfully down at the cottage. The colonel, for his part, barely noticed the raillery when it was only aimed at him. But Marianne was completely confused by it at first, and once she finally grasped exactly what Mrs. Jennings was implying, she hardly knew whether to laugh at how ridiculous it was or bristle at the rudeness of the suggestion. It felt to her like a cruel little jab at the colonel’s advanced years, the sad, lonely state of a confirmed old bachelor.

Mrs. Dashwood, who didn’t think a man only five years younger than herself was nearly as ancient as her romantic, seventeen-year-old daughter imagined, hurried to assure her that Mrs. Jennings had no wish to mock the colonel’s age on purpose.
“Mama, you can argue she’s not ill-intentioned if you like,” Marianne protested, “but you can’t possibly say the accusation isn’t absurd! Colonel Brandon is certainly younger than Mrs. Jennings, but he’s old enough to be my father. Even if he ever had it in him to fall in love, that feeling would have faded out of him decades ago. It’s so ridiculous! If old age and bad health can’t protect a man from this silly, teasing matchmaking, what on earth can?”
“Bad health?” Elinor cut in, amused. “You can find his age much more of a gap between you two than it is for Mama, that’s fair, but you can’t possibly pretend he can’t walk perfectly well, can you?”
“Didn’t you hear him complaining of rheumatism?” Marianne said. “That’s the most common ailment of people in their declining years!”
“My dearest girl,” Mrs. Dashwood laughed, “at this rate you must live in constant fear of me wasting away! It must feel like a total miracle to you that I’ve made it all the way to the advanced age of forty.”
“Mama, that’s not fair,” Marianne said. “I know full well he’s not old enough that anyone’s worried he’ll drop dead any day. He could easily live another twenty years. But thirty-five has no business getting married at all, far as I’m concerned.”
“Fair,” Elinor nodded. “Thirty-five and seventeen are probably not a great match, I’ll grant you that. But suppose there was a twenty-seven year old woman who was still unmarried? I hardly think Colonel Brandon being thirty-five would be any kind of barrier to making her a good husband.”
Marianne paused for a beat, her brow furrowing as she thought it over. “A woman of twenty-seven can never hope to feel or inspire that fiery, true kind of love ever again. If her home is miserable, or she has almost no fortune of her own, I suppose she might force herself to marry, essentially acting as a nurse just for the security and provision that being a wife gives her. That kind of marriage makes sense, as a convenient arrangement, everyone else would be happy with it. But to me? That’s not a real marriage at all. It’s just a commercial trade, each person trying to get what they want at the other’s expense.”
“I know I’ll never convince you a twenty-seven year old could love a thirty-five year old enough to want him as a companion,” Elinor sighed, half smiling. “But do you really have to doom Colonel Brandon and any hypothetical wife to be cooped up in a sickroom forever, just because he happened to mention a little dull ache in his shoulder yesterday, on that freezing, drizzling day?”
“But he talked about flannel waistcoats!” Marianne protested. “Flannel waistcoats only ever mean aches, cramps, rheumatism, every kind of ailment that plagues the old and feeble!”
“If he had a raging fever, I’ll bet you wouldn’t find him half so silly,” Elinor teased, getting up to leave the room. “Admit it, Marianne, there’s something so interesting and romantic to you about a flushed cheek, hollow eye, and a quick, racing pulse when someone’s ill.”

As soon as the door clicked shut behind Elinor, Marianne turned to her mother, her face tight with quiet anxiety. “Mama, I have a horrible worry I can’t keep from you. I’m sure Edward Ferrars is unwell. We’ve been here almost a fortnight, and he still hasn’t come to visit. Nothing but genuine sickness could keep him away this long, what else could possibly be detaining him back at Norland?”
“Had you been expecting him to arrive this soon?” Mrs. Dashwood said, surprised. “I certainly wasn’t. If anything, I’d been a little uneasy thinking back on how unexcited he seemed every time I invited him to come stay at Barton, before we left Norland. Does Elinor expect him already, then?”
“I’ve never mentioned it to her, but of course she must be waiting for him,” Marianne said.
“I think you’re wrong, love,” her mother said. “Yesterday I was talking to her about ordering a new grate for the spare bedroom, and she said there was no rush at all, that it wasn’t likely the room would be needed for a long while yet.”
Marianne stared, confused. “How strange! What on earth does that mean? The way they’ve behaved around each other this whole time has been so impossible to understand! Their last goodbyes were so cold, so collected. They barely spoke to each other the last evening we were at Norland. When Edward said farewell, he wished all of us well exactly the same way, like he was just a kind older brother, no special softness for Elinor at all. Twice I left them completely alone together that last morning, and both times he followed me right out of the room! And Elinor? She didn’t even cry when we said goodbye to Edward, the way I did. Even now she never breaks, never looks sad, never avoids company, never seems restless or unhappy around other people. What is going on?”

The Dashwood family had now settled fully into Barton Cottage, and their life there unfolded with a gentle, steady comfort they had not dared hope for after leaving Norland. The little white house, its sloping cottage garden, and all the winding lanes and wild hedgerows that surrounded them had grown familiar, and the small daily rituals that had once made half the joy of their old home—sketching wildflowers by the stream, poring over poetry beside the fire, tending to potted herbs on the windowsill—were resumed here with far more lightness than they had ever known at Norland after their father’s death. For the first fortnight after their arrival, Sir John Middleton called on them every single day; unused to seeing anyone keep themselves usefully occupied at home, he could not hide his open amazement at finding the three Dashwood girls and their mother always busy at some project, never idling away the hours.

They had very few other visitors outside of the Barton Park household. Though Sir John begged them repeatedly to mix more widely in the neighbourhood, swearing his carriage was always at their disposal for any call they wished to make, Mrs. Dashwood’s independent spirit outweighed any desire to force her daughters into uninviting social obligation. She was resolute that they would not call on any family further than a pleasant walk away, and there were very few such households within that short distance. On one of their very first rambles, the girls had wandered a mile and a half down the narrow, winding Allenham valley that branched off from Barton’s, and stumbled on an ancient, stately stone manor house whose gabled roofs and climbing old roses reminded them so strongly of Norland that they instantly longed to know more of its inhabitants. Upon enquiry, though, they learned it belonged to an elderly lady of excellent character who was sadly too infirm to ever leave her home or receive visitors.

The whole surrounding countryside was laced with beautiful walking paths. The high chalk downs rose almost directly from the cottage windows, their summits promising crisp, clean air and sweeping views of the valley below, the perfect escape when heavy mud in the lower lanes made their usual walks impassable. One showery morning, after two full days of steady, unrelenting rain that had left them cooped up indoors, Marianne and Margaret decided to head for one of those downs at once, lured by a fleeting patch of bright sunshine that peeked through the clouds. The weather was not fair enough to tempt Elinor and their mother away from their sketchbook and open book beside the parlour fire, even when Marianne insisted the day would clear entirely, every threatening cloud blown far off the hills. The two girls set off on their own, gaily climbing the path up the down, rejoicing over their good judgment every time a patch of bright blue sky peeked through the overcast. When the sharp, invigorating southwesterly gale hit their faces high on the slope, they laughed aloud, pitying Elinor and their mother for missing such pure, sparkling joy.

“Is there any happiness in all the world equal to this?” Marianne cried, turning to Margaret with glowing cheeks. “We will stay up here and walk at least two whole hours, I swear.”

Margaret agreed, and they pushed on against the wind, laughing as they leaned into its force for nearly twenty more minutes, before the clouds suddenly gathered thick and dark over their heads, and a driving, stinging rain lashed straight into their faces. Chagrined and caught completely unawares, they had no choice but to turn back, for there was not a single cottage or hedge thick enough to shelter them anywhere nearby, their only refuge the warm parlour of their own home. The quickest way down was the steep direct slope that led straight to their garden gate, and they began to run as fast as they could.

Marianne pulled ahead at first, but her foot slipped on the wet grass, and she tumbled hard to the ground. Margaret could not stop her own momentum in time to help, and she careened all the way down the hill to the bottom, arriving safe and breathless at the garden gate.

A gentleman carrying a shotgun, two fluffy pointers trotting and playing at his heels, had been making his way up the hill only a few yards from Marianne when she fell. He set his gun down instantly and ran to her side. Marianne had already lifted herself from the ground, but her ankle had twisted badly in the fall, and she could barely put any weight on it to stand. The gentleman offered his assistance at once, and seeing that her modesty made her hesitate to accept help she could not do without, he swept her gently up into his arms without further preamble and carried her all the way down the steep slope. He walked straight through the open garden gate that Margaret had left ajar, and carried her right into the cottage, not setting her down until he had seated her soft and safe in an armchair by the parlour fire.

Elinor and Mrs. Dashwood jumped to their feet in complete amazement at their entrance. Both stared at the stranger, their faces alight with equal parts wonder and quiet, unplanned admiration, drawn immediately to his handsome, bright face. He apologized gently for bursting in uninvited, explaining what had happened to Marianne with a frank, graceful ease that made his already striking looks even more charming, warmed by his soft voice and kind expression. Even if he had been old, plain, and coarse, Mrs. Dashwood would have been endlessly grateful to him for tending to her injured child; but his youth, his easy elegance, the warmth of his smile, made his small act of kindness feel like something far more precious.

She thanked him over and over, and with her usual gentle sweetness invited him to sit and rest a while. He declined, though, noting that he was soaked through with rain and covered in mud from the hill. When Mrs. Dashwood asked for his name, he told her it was Willoughby, that he was currently staying at Allenham Court, and that he hoped he might have the honour of calling on them the next day to enquire after Marianne’s sprained ankle. They granted his request at once, and he departed, stepping back out into the full force of the heavy pouring rain.

His manly beauty and uncommon grace instantly became the whole family’s topic of conversation. They laughed gently at Marianne’s tumble, and the story of her gallant rescuer felt all the more delightful for how handsome and charming he was. Marianne herself had barely gotten a clear look at him: the hot, crimson flush that had flooded her face the moment he lifted her off the ground had made her too flustered to meet his eyes for almost the whole walk into the house. But she had seen more than enough to agree with all their praise, lauding him with all the passionate warmth that was her usual way. His face and his bearing were exactly the kind of hero she had always daydreamed of finding in her favourite novels, and that quick, unhesitating eagerness to help her, no tedious formalities to get in the way, made his kindness feel all the more romantic. Every small detail of him felt interesting: his good name, his home at the old Allenham manor they had so admired, and Marianne soon declared that a hunting jacket was the most becoming garment any man could possibly wear. Her imagination ran wild, her thoughts soft and bright, and she barely noticed the throbbing ache in her sprained ankle at all.

Sir John called on them as soon as the sky cleared enough the next day for him to venture out. When they told him of Marianne’s accident, they eagerly asked if he knew any young man named Willoughby who was staying at Allenham.
“Willoughby!” Sir John cried, delighted. “What, he’s in the neighbourhood? That’s capital news! I’ll ride over to his place tomorrow and invite him to dinner here at the park on Thursday.”
“So you do know him, then?” Mrs. Dashwood said.
“Know him? Of course I do! He comes down to Allenham every single year without fail.”
“And what sort of young man is he?”
“The best fellow alive, I assure you. A crack shot, and there’s not a bolder rider in all of England.”
“Is that all you can say for him?” Marianne exclaimed, indignant. “What of his manners, when you know him better? What of his pursuits, his talents, his wit and genius?”

Sir John looked rather puzzled. “Upon my soul, I can’t say I’ve paid much mind to all of that. But he’s pleasant, good-humoured, and he’s got the sweetest little black bitch of a pointer I’ve ever seen. Was she out with him today?”
Marianne could no more tell him the colour of Mr. Willoughby’s pointer than he could describe the finer shades of his character to her.
“Wait, who exactly is he?” Elinor asked. “Where does he come from? Does he own property at Allenham?”

On that point, Sir John could give them far clearer answers. He told them Mr. Willoughby had no property of his own in Devonshire, that he was only staying at Allenham Court as a guest of the elderly lady who owned it, who was his relation, and whose entire estate he was heir to.
“Aye, aye, he’s a very good catch, let me tell you, Miss Dashwood,” he added, grinning. “He’s got a nice little private estate of his own in Somersetshire too. If I were you, I wouldn’t let your younger sister steal him away, not after tumbling down hills and getting carried off like that. Marianne can’t expect to have every nice young man all to herself, you know. Poor Colonel Brandon will be jealous, if she’s not careful.”
“I don’t think Mr. Willoughby will have any trouble from either of my daughters doing what you call ‘catching him,’” Mrs. Dashwood said with a good-humoured smile. “That’s not the sort of behaviour we raised our girls for. Men are perfectly safe with us, no matter how rich they are. I’m very glad to hear he’s a respectable young man, and that we’ll be happy to make his acquaintance.”
“He’s the best sort of fellow alive, I swear,” Sir John repeated. “Last Christmas, at the little hop we had here at the park, he danced from eight o’clock at night straight till four in the morning, and never once sat down.”
“Did he truly?” Marianne cried, her eyes sparkling. “And did he dance with elegance, with spirit?”
“He did! And he was up again by eight the next morning to go riding out to the hunt.”
“That’s exactly the kind of young man he ought to be,” Marianne said, breathless with delight. “Whatever a man chooses to pursue, he should throw his whole heart into it, no care for how tired he might get.”
“Aye, aye, I see how this will go,” Sir John laughed. “You’ll be setting your cap at him now, and never give poor old Brandon a second thought.”
“That is a phrase I detest above all others, Sir John,” Marianne said, colouring warmly. “I hate every silly, commonplace saying people use to try to sound clever, and ‘setting one’s cap at a man’ or ‘making a conquest’ are the worst of them all. They’re crude, they’re unkind, and even if they ever were clever, it’s been decades since they had any wit left to them.”

Sir John did not quite follow her rebuke, but he laughed heartily anyway, as if he did. “Well, you’ll make plenty of conquests either way, I don’t doubt. Poor Brandon’s already completely smitten with you, and he’s well worth setting your cap at, even if you have to tumble down a few more hills and sprain a few more ankles to get his attention.”

Margaret had dubbed the stranger who’d carried an injured Marianne half a mile through the lashing autumn storm back to Barton Cottage “Marianne’s preserver,” a name far more warmhearted than precise. He called on them at dawn the very next day to check on her welfare, and Mrs. Dashwood greeted him with far more than formal politeness: her welcome brimmed with the unreserved kindness sparked by both Sir John’s glowing accounts of him and her own deep gratitude. Every moment of that first visit only confirmed for Willoughby that the family accident had introduced him to was full of sharp good sense, gentle elegance, fierce mutual affection, and the soft, unforced comfort of a home where love ruled over formality. He had not needed a second meeting to be convinced of the sisters’ personal charm, either.

Elinor Dashwood had a soft, clear complexion, neat regular features, and a quiet, graceful figure that drew admiring eyes wherever she went. But Marianne was still far handsomer. Her frame, less strictly symmetrical than her sister’s, was far more striking for its extra height, and her face was so bright and lovely that calling her simply “a beautiful girl” did far less violence to the truth than most empty compliments. Her skin was warm and sun-kissed, but so translucent that her whole complexion glowed; every line of her face was soft and pleasing, her smile sweet and infectious, and in her large dark eyes burned a fire, a spirit, a bright eagerness that no one could look upon without delight.

At first, Marianne held that light back from Willoughby, flustered and embarrassed remembering how he’d found her fallen helpless on the hillside, her ankle twisted too badly to walk. But that shyness faded fast. When her nerves settled, and she saw that beneath his perfect gentlemanly manners he was unreserved, lively, and quick to laugh—most of all, when he confessed he was passionately, wildly fond of both music and dancing—she gave him a look of such unreserved approval that he spent every remaining minute of his visit talking almost entirely to her.

It took no more than a mention of any favorite shared amusement to get her talking. She could not stay silent on subjects she loved, and there was no shyness, no performative reserve in the things she said. They quickly realized their tastes in music and dancing were perfectly aligned, rooted in identical judgments of what made each art form feel alive. Emboldened to dig deeper into his opinions, she began to ask him about books, bringing out her most beloved authors and gushing over their best lines with such rapturous delight that any man of twenty-five would have to be impossibly dull not to immediately embrace the beauty of those works, even if he had never cared for them before. Their tastes were shockingly, perfectly alike. They idolized the same books, the same exact passages; and on the rare occasion a tiny difference of opinion popped up, it vanished the second Marianne laid out her argument and those bright dark eyes lit up to make her case. Willoughby agreed with every one of her judgments, caught every spark of her enthusiasm, and long before his visit ended, they talked to each other like old, long-separated friends.
