“Well, Marianne,” Elinor said the second he walked out the door, “I’d say you’ve had a very productive morning. You’ve already established his opinion on nearly every important topic under the sun. You know exactly what he thinks of Cowper and Scott, you’re certain he appreciates their beauty exactly as he ought, and you’ve got full assurance he doesn’t admire Pope more than is reasonable. But how on earth is your acquaintance going to last long at this pace? You’ll burn through every favorite topic before you know it. One more meeting and you’ll have covered his thoughts on picturesque scenery and second marriages, and then there will be nothing at all left to talk about.”

“Elinor!” Marianne cried, half-indignant, half-playful. “Is that fair? Is that just? Are my ideas really so shallow? I see what you mean, though. I was too at ease, too happy, too frank. I broke every silly little rule of decorum everyone else clings to. I was open and sincere when I should have been reserved, lifeless, dull, and deceitful. If I’d only talked of the weather and the roads, and only spoken once every ten minutes, you’d never have teased me for this.”

“My love,” their mother cut in gently, “you mustn’t be cross with Elinor. She was only jesting. I would scold her myself if she ever tried to smother the joy of your new friendship with this dear man.” Marianne’s irritation melted away instantly.

For his part, Willoughby gave every possible sign that he adored their company, and was determined to grow closer to them. He came by every single day. At first his excuse was checking on Marianne’s sprained ankle, but their welcome grew warmer and warmer with every visit, until the pretense was entirely unnecessary even before Marianne had fully recovered. She was stuck confined to the cottage for days, but no confinement had ever felt less tedious. Willoughby was sharp, quick-witted, vividly imaginative, full of life, and open and affectionate to everyone he met. He was perfectly made to win Marianne’s heart: on top of all that, he was strikingly handsome, and carried a natural fiery warmth that her own bright spirit only stoked higher, endearing him to her more than anything else ever could.

His company gradually became her greatest joy. They read poetry aloud, talked for hours on end, sang duets together; his musical talent was considerable, and he read every line of prose or verse with the full sensitivity and fire that poor Edward, to Elinor’s quiet regret, had never possessed. To Mrs. Dashwood, he was as faultless as he was to Marianne. Elinor could find nothing to criticize in him but one small flaw, a trait he shared with Marianne that made them all the more compatible: he spoke far too freely what he thought on every occasion, with no care for who might be listening, or what social niceties might dictate. He judged other people quickly and shared those judgments openly, set aside general politeness to give his full, undivided attention to anyone he cared for, and brushed off the trivial formalities of high society far too easily. Elinor could not approve of that lack of caution, no matter how fervently both Willoughby and Marianne tried to defend it.

Marianne soon realized her dark, desperate thought at sixteen and a half—that she would never meet a man who lived up to her idea of perfect goodness and passion—had been foolish and wrong. Willoughby was everything she had ever daydreamed of in that unhappy hour, and every brighter moment since: the kind of man who could win her whole heart, and his every action made it clear his affection was just as earnest, and his spirit just as strong. Her mother, who had not once let her mind drift to the thought of their marriage even when she learned Willoughby was set to inherit a large fortune, found herself hoping and expecting it before the week was out, secretly congratulating herself that she might soon have two such wonderful sons-in-law: Edward for Elinor, and Willoughby for Marianne.

Colonel Brandon’s quiet fondness for Marianne, which his friends had spotted long before, only became noticeable to Elinor when everyone else stopped paying attention to it entirely. Sir John, Lady Middleton, and Mrs. Jennings were all far too busy fawning over Willoughby, the far more fortunate rival, to notice. The silly teasing they had once aimed at the Colonel as a joke faded away now that his feelings were real, and would have only felt cruel directed at a man so clearly soft on the girl he’d grown to admire. Elinor was forced, against her will, to admit that the fondness Mrs. Jennings had once joked about for her own amusement was very real, and that even though Marianne and Willoughby were perfectly matched in their fiery, vivid tempers, the Colonel’s quiet, steady opposite nature had not stopped him from falling for her. She saw it with quiet concern. What could a quiet thirty-five-year-old gentleman hope to win, against a bright, lively twenty-five-year-old who matched Marianne’s every spark? She could not even wish for the Colonel to succeed, so she heartily wished he would stop caring for her sister altogether. She liked him, though: even with his quiet gravity and reserve, she found him a deeply interesting man. His manners were serious but gentle, and his reserve seemed to come from some old, lingering grief, not a naturally sullen temper. Sir John had dropped hints of old heartbreaks and disappointments long ago, that confirmed her suspicion he was a deeply unfortunate man, and she regarded him with genuine respect and compassion.

She might have pitied and esteemed him all the more because Willoughby and Marianne treated him like a trivial afterthought. Prejudiced against him for being neither young nor lively, they seemed determined to dismiss all his good qualities out of hand.

“Brandon is exactly the sort of man,” Willoughby said one day, when they were talking of him together, “that everyone says is perfectly nice, but nobody actually cares about. Everyone is happy to see him walk in a room, but no one ever remembers to actually talk to him.”

“That is exactly what I think of him!” Marianne cried at once.

“Don’t brag about that, though,” Elinor said. “You’re both being deeply unfair. He is highly regarded by everyone at the park, and I never see him without making a point of having a proper conversation with him.”

“That he’s your personal favorite is certainly a point in his favor,” Willoughby replied. “But as for everyone else’s approval? That’s an insult in itself. What man who could earn anyone else’s genuine interest would ever put up with the indignity of being called ‘nice’ by Lady Middleton and Mrs. Jennings?”

“But maybe your disdain and Marianne’s dismissal makes up for their praise,” Elinor shot back. “If their approval is barely an insult, your unfair judgment is far kinder. They are no more unthinking in their praise than you two are prejudiced and unjust.”

“You’re being sharp with me just to defend your little protégé,” Willoughby laughed.

“My protégé, as you call him, is a man of good sense—and good sense will always appeal to me. Yes, Marianne, even in a man between thirty and forty. He has traveled the world, been abroad, read widely, and has a thoughtful, sharp mind. He’s given me so much useful information on all kinds of subjects, and he always answers my questions with perfect good humor and politeness.”

“That means,” Marianne scoffed, “he told you the East Indies are very hot, and the mosquitoes are very annoying.”

“He would have told me that if I’d asked him,” Elinor said, “but I happened to already know those things.”

“Maybe his observations stretch even further,” Willoughby teased. “He might have mentioned nabobs, gold mohrs, and palanquins, too.”

“I daresay his experiences stretch far further than you are willing to fairly admit. But why do you dislike him so much?”

“I don’t dislike him, not really,” Willoughby said. “I think he’s a very respectable man, who everyone says hello to and nobody remembers. He’s got more money than he can spend, more time than he knows what to do with, and two new coats every year.”

“Add to that,” Marianne put in, “that he has no genius, no taste, no spirit. His mind has no sparkle, his feelings no fire, and his voice no warmth or expression.”

“You both judge his whole character all at once, based entirely on what you imagine about him, so my praise of him must sound very dull by comparison,” Elinor said. “All I can say is that he’s a sensible man, well-bred, well-informed, gentle in his manners, and I believe he has a very kind, good heart.”

“Miss Dashwood,” Willoughby protested, “you’re being unkind to me now. You’re trying to argue me into liking him against my will, and it won’t work. You’ll find I’m as stubborn as you are clever. I have three unanswerable, unchanging reasons to dislike Colonel Brandon: he told me it would rain the day I wanted perfect sunshine, he criticized the way I hung my curricle, and I cannot convince him to buy my beautiful brown mare. If it makes you happy, I will admit every other part of his character is completely irreproachable. But in return for that painful concession, you cannot deny me the right to dislike him just as much as I ever did.”

Never once, when the Dashwood women first settled into their cozy Devonshire cottage, had they imagined their days would fill so quickly with social calls, outings, and near-constant invitations that left barely a spare hour for quiet reading or sketching. Yet that was exactly the life they soon found themselves leading.
The moment Marianne was fully recovered from her fever, all the amusements Sir John Middleton had spent weeks plotting came rushing to life. Small private dances began at Barton Park, and boating parties were arranged and set out as often as the drizzly, unpredictable October weather would allow. Willoughby was included in every single gathering of the sort. The easy, unstudied familiarity of these casual events only pulled him and the Dashwood sisters closer—most of all Marianne. He had endless chances to witness her wit, her passion for poetry and wild walks, the light that lit up her face at the sight of a sunset over the Devonshire hills, and every day he made his open, unashamed admiration clearer. Marianne, in turn, showed him every corner of her heart without reserve.
Elinor was not surprised by how fast the two fell for each other. She only wished they did not wear their hearts so completely on their sleeves, and once or twice she gently suggested Marianne show a little more discretion for the sake of local gossip. But Marianne hated all concealment when no shame could come of being honest. To hold back feelings that were kind, bright, and true seemed to her not just a waste of effort, but a surrender to stuffy, meaningless social rules that had no right to dictate how a person loved. Willoughby felt exactly the same, and their behavior at every turn made their shared beliefs impossible to miss.
When Willoughby was in the room, Marianne saw no one else. Every word he spoke was clever, every thing he did was perfect. If their evenings at the Park ended with card games, he would intentionally play badly, cheat the rest of the table out of points, just to make sure Marianne walked away with the best hand. If the night’s amusement was dancing, they were partners for half the set, and when they were forced to split apart for the required two country dances, they still hovered shoulder to shoulder on the edge of the floor, barely speaking a single word to anyone else. Their neighbors teased them relentlessly for it, but the ridicule did not embarrass them, did not even ruffle them. Mrs. Dashwood, for her part, shared all their warm, eager feelings, and had no urge at all to scold them for their open affection. To her, it was simply the natural, beautiful outcome of two young, ardent hearts finding each other.
This was the season of pure, uncomplicated happiness for Marianne. Her heart belonged entirely to Willoughby, and the fond attachment she had once carried for their old home at Norland, which she had thought nothing could soften, grew lighter and lighter as every walk, every shared poem, every quiet laugh with Willoughby made their little Devonshire cottage feel more like home than Norland ever had.
Elinor’s happiness was far less steady. Her heart was not at peace, and her enjoyment of all these parties was never untainted. None of her new acquaintances could make up for what she had left behind in Sussex, could make her think of Norland, and the quiet secret love she had left there, with any less regret. Neither Lady Middleton nor the garrulous Mrs. Jennings could give her the thoughtful, equal conversation she missed—though Mrs. Jennings never stopped talking, and had taken a great liking to Elinor from their very first meeting, she had already repeated the full story of her late husband’s final illness three or four times over. If Elinor’s memory was sharper, she would have known every last detail of Mr. Jennings’s last words to his wife before he drew his final breath. Lady Middleton was only more pleasant than her mother in that she spoke far less. Elinor quickly saw that her quiet reserve was not a mark of good sense, just a total lack of anything interesting to say. She treated her husband and her mother exactly the same way she treated the Dashwoods, so there was no hope of building any sort of warm friendship with her. She never had a new thing to say from one day to the next, her flat, unchanging good humor never shifted, and though she never protested her husband’s constant parties as long as everything was done in proper style and her two eldest children were close by, she never appeared to enjoy them any more than she would have enjoyed sitting silent at home. She added nothing to the group’s conversation, and people would often forget she was in the room entirely until she snapped to scold her two rambunctious boys for running off with the cake knives.
Of all their new acquaintances, only Colonel Brandon could give Elinor the feeling of having found a friend worthy of her respect, a companion who made the long evenings feel worth her time. Willoughby was out of the question as far as Elinor was concerned, of course: all his attention was reserved for Marianne, and a man far less charming than him would still have been beloved by the whole group, wrapped up as he was in his own happy love. The Colonel, by contrast, had no hope of Marianne’s regard, and found quiet, gentle comfort in talking to Elinor, even as he watched Marianne dote on another man. Elinor’s sympathy for him only grew when she began to suspect he already knew the sharp, lingering pain of a love that had not worked out.
Her suspicion was confirmed one evening at the Park, when they sat together by mutual consent on the sidelines while everyone else danced. His eyes were fixed on Marianne, spinning light as a feather in Willoughby’s arms, and after a long quiet minute, he spoke with a faint, sad smile.
“Your sister, I understand, does not approve of second attachments.”
“No,” Elinor replied. “All her opinions are thoroughly romantic.”
“Or rather, I think she believes such attachments are not even possible to exist at all.”
“I think she does,” Elinor agreed, half amused. “Though I cannot imagine how she holds that view without stopping to think of our own father, who married twice. In a few years, her opinions will settle into the sensible, reasonable views that come with living in the world, and then no one will have any trouble understanding her beliefs, not even herself.”
“That may well be true,” the Colonel said softly. “And yet there is something so very amiable in the untested prejudices of a young mind, that one can’t help but grieve to see them give way to the cold, common opinions most people carry.”
“I cannot agree with you there,” Elinor said. “There are real inconveniences to feelings as uncompromising as Marianne’s, that all the charm of youthful enthusiasm and inexperience can never make up for. Her whole system of beliefs tends to throw all common propriety aside, and I only hope that getting to know the world better will be the greatest good that ever comes to her.”
After a short pause, the Colonel spoke again, his voice low.
“Does your sister draw any line in her objections to second attachments? Or is it equally wrong for everyone? Do people who had their first love torn away from them, whether the fault was their beloved’s, or the cruelty of circumstance, owe it to themselves to never care for anyone ever again for the rest of their lives?”
“Upon my word, I don’t know the fine details of her rules,” Elinor said. “I only know I have never once heard her say that a second attachment of any kind could be pardonable.”
“That cannot stand,” the Colonel said, his voice tight with a emotion he did not usually show. “But a full change, a total uprooting of all a person’s most cherished feelings—no, no, do not wish for that for your sister. When those soft romantic idealisms of youth are forced to break, all too often they are replaced by opinions that are far too common, and far too dangerous. I speak from experience. I once knew a woman who had the exact same temperament, the exact same way of thinking as your sister. She judged the world the same way, but then a forced change, a whole string of terrible misfortunes—”
He cut himself off abruptly, his face going pale, like he had said far more than he ever intended to. That small, accidental slip was all Elinor needed to put the pieces together. The memory of that unknown woman, and the grief tied to her, was clearly still a tender, raw wound the Colonel had never shared with anyone. Elinor did not press him for details. But if Marianne had been sitting in her place, she would have had the whole tragic, sweeping story of a doomed, forbidden love plotted out in her imagination in minutes, every secret and every heartbreak filled in, and never once doubted it was all perfectly true.

The crisp golden light of an autumn Barton morning filtered through hawthorn hedgerows as Elinor and Marianne took their usual post-breakfast walk, when Marianne burst into a torrent of delighted speech that left Elinor blinking in disbelief at her sister’s uncharacteristic, giddy recklessness.

Willoughby had given her a horse, Marianne announced, cheeks flushed bright with joy—one he’d bred himself on his family estate in Somersetshire, perfectly sized and tempered for a lady to ride. She’d accepted it at once, no hesitation at all, chattering on about the wind in their hair as they galloped across the local downs, the way Elinor could ride it half the time, no strings attached. It had never crossed her mind that their small household had never planned to keep a horse: that accepting the gift would mean purchasing a second mount for a servant to tend it, hiring that very servant, even building a new stable or shed to house them both, all costs their mother’s already tight budget, stretched thin after leaving Norland Park, could barely bear.

Marianne brushed off every concern Elinor raised like it was a bothersome gnat. The extra servant would cost almost nothing, their mother would never object to making her happy, the park could easily lend a horse for the groom, even a rickety old shed at the edge of the cottage grounds would do for stabling. When Elinor gently pointed out that accepting such an extravagant, personal gift from a man she had known only a handful of weeks was far from proper, Marianne flared up at once.
“You are wrong, Elinor,” she said, her voice warm and fierce with conviction. “Time means nothing when two souls fit. I have not known Willoughby long in days, but I know him better than I know anyone in the whole world save you and mama. Seven years could not make some people understand each other, and seven days is more than enough for us. I would think it far more improper to take such a gift from our brother John, who we have lived with for decades but barely know at all, than from Willoughby, whose character I have studied and loved every minute of these past weeks.”

Elinor knew better than to push her on the point when her temper was so roused—opposition would only make Marianne dig her heels in deeper. Instead, she appealed to her sister’s soft, loving heart for their mother, painting a gentle picture of the quiet sacrifices Mrs. Dashwood would have to make to cover those extra, unplanned costs, and Marianne softened immediately. She promised she would not pressure their mother to agree to the gift, that she would tell Willoughby the next time she saw him she had to turn it down.

She kept her word. Later that very day, when Willoughby called at the cottage, Elinor overheard her murmuring her disappointment to him in a low voice, just out of earshot of the rest of the family, as she explained all the practical reasons the lovely horse could not be theirs. Willoughby’s sorrow was plain on his face, and after pressing her gently to change her mind and realizing he could not, he leaned in close, his voice soft only for her to hear.
“Marianne, the horse is still yours, even if you cannot take her now. I will keep her, name her Queen Mab, and care for her every day until you are ready to claim her. When you leave Barton to move into the home we will share, she will be waiting for you.”

Elinor’s breath caught. In the warmth of his tone, the easy use of her sister’s first name, the quiet, unspoken promise woven through every word, she saw it plain as day: they were already secretly engaged. The intimacy between them was no casual flirtation, no fleeting fancy. She could not even find it in herself to be surprised that two such open, unguarded people had hidden the news from their own family, letting them stumble across the truth by accident.

Little Margaret confirmed her suspicion the very next day, pulling her eldest sister aside with a face so full of important secrecy she could barely contain it. “Elinor! I know a huge secret. Marianne and Mr. Willoughby are going to be married very soon, I am sure of it.”
Elinor laughed softly, fond of her little sister’s overactive imagination. “You’ve said that almost every day since they met on Highchurch Down, Margaret. You swore a fortnight ago Marianne wore his miniature around her neck, and it turned out to be nothing but a portrait of our great-uncle.”
“But this is real!” Margaret insisted, bouncing on her toes. “He has a lock of her hair. I saw him cut it off last night, after you and mama stepped out of the parlour. They were whispering, and he begged her for something, then picked up her little embroidery scissors and snipped off a whole long curl of the dark hair that was tumbling down her back. He kissed it, folded it in a scrap of white paper, and tucked it right into his pocketbook.”
Elinor could not find it in herself to doubt her. Every part of the story lined up perfectly with all the small, tender moments she had watched unfold between the two of them.

Margaret’s sharp little eye for secrets nearly got Elinor in trouble a few evenings later, when the whole party was gathered at the Middleton’s park. Mrs. Jennings, forever nosy about romantic attachments, turned to the youngest Miss Dashwood and badgered her to name the quiet young man she was certain Elinor was secretly fond of. Margaret looked straight at Elinor, and grinned. “I can’t tell, can I Elinor?”
The whole room burst into laughter. Elinor forced a smile, but her chest felt tight. She knew exactly who Margaret was thinking of—Edward Ferrars, the quiet, kind man she had grown to love back at Norland, the very last person she wanted to become the butt of Mrs. Jennings’ endless, teasing jokes.
Marianne, trying to defend her, only made it worse, flushing bright red and snapping at Margaret. “Whatever silly ideas you have in your head, you have no right to go repeating them to everyone!”
“I didn’t make up any ideas!” Margaret protested. “You’re the one who told me about him in the first place!”
The company grew even more curious, Mrs. Jennings practically leaning out of her seat to get the name out of the girl. “Come on, Miss Margaret, tell us all! What is the gentleman’s name?”
“I won’t say his whole name,” Margaret said, proud of her discretion. “But it starts with an F. And he’s not a curate, and he’s not even a clergyman at all.”
Marianne was about to snap again, when Lady Middleton, who detested all unrefined joking of that sort, drawled out that it was raining very heavily outside. Colonel Brandon, ever quick to notice and ease other people’s discomfort, immediately picked up the subject, chatting at length about the forecast and the state of the garden paths, while Willoughby crossed the room to fling open the piano lid and drag Marianne over to play a duet with him. The awkward topic was dropped, and the moment passed—but Elinor’s heart was still racing long after everyone had moved on to other things.

That same evening, the whole group made plans for an outing the next day, to visit the grand, famous Allenham Court, sprawling twelve miles from Barton, that belonged to Colonel Brandon’s brother-in-law. The owner was travelling abroad, and would not allow visitors to tour the grounds without Brandon’s personal permission. The estate was famed for its rolling parkland and vast, glassy lake, and Sir John, who organised a trip there at least twice every summer, swore it was the most beautiful sight in all Devonshire. They would take open carriages, bring cold picnic feasts, and spend the whole day sailing on the lake and wandering the woods.
Only a few of the group thought it a poor idea, given that it had rained every single day for a fortnight. Elinor, who had already caught a lingering scratchy cold, gently persuaded her mother, who had started to come down with a chill herself, to stay home and rest rather than risk making herself ill out on the wet roads.

Elinor had braced herself for their planned Whitwell excursion for days: she’d stowed a spare wool shawl and change of stockings in her bag, fully expecting to return soaked through, footsore, and giddy from a day out on the wild rolling countryside. What she never anticipated was that they would not leave Barton Park at all.

By ten o’clock that morning, the whole merry party had assembled for breakfast at the Park. The previous night’s heavy rain had all but blown itself out; clouds drifted thin across pale blue sky, and bursts of golden sunlight broke through every few minutes to gild the wet lawns. Spirits ran high. Everyone chattered over buttered crumpets and spiced cider, all agreeing they would endure any mud, any chill, any inconvenience to make the day perfect.

Then the post was brought in. One letter, addressed in a sharp unfamiliar hand, landed beside Colonel Brandon. He picked it up, glanced at the seal, and the colour drained from his face entirely. Without a word, he pushed back his chair and left the breakfast parlour in such a hurry the door swung shut behind him with a soft bang.
“Now what’s got into Brandon?” Sir John exclaimed, tapping his knife against the edge of his plate. No one could guess. “I do hope no harm has befallen anyone,” Lady Middleton said smoothly, lifting her teacup. “It must be a very extraordinary matter to pull Colonel Brandon away from my breakfast table so abruptly.”

Five minutes later, he returned, his jaw tight, his usual gentle smile forced.
“No bad news, I hope, Colonel?” Mrs. Jennings called out the second he crossed the threshold.
“None at all, ma’am, I thank you.”
“Was it from Avignon? I do hope your sister’s health hasn’t worsened?”
“No, ma’am. It came from London, only a letter of business.”
“Business, my foot,” Mrs. Jennings hooted, leaning forward. “A plain business letter wouldn’t make you turn that pale. Come now, Colonel, no secrets from old friends.”
“Mamma, do mind your manners,” Lady Middleton chided.
“Maybe it’s news your cousin Fanny’s finally married off?” Mrs. Jennings went on, ignoring her daughter entirely.
“Indeed it is not.”
“Well then, I know exactly who wrote it,” she grinned, winking. “I hope the dear girl is quite well?”
Colonel Brandon flushed faintly. “Who do you mean, ma’am?”
“Oh, you know perfectly well.” He turned to Lady Middleton, his tone rueful. “I am very sorry to say this, but the business in that letter demands I leave for London at once. I cannot delay a single day.”

“London?” Mrs. Jennings squawked. “What on earth could possibly need you in town in the middle of the country summer?”
“It grieves me greatly to leave such a pleasant gathering,” he went on, “but what is worse, I fear my personal presence is required to secure our admittance to Whitwell. The steward will not unlock the grounds for anyone else.”

A collective groan rippled across the table. Marianne leaned forward at once, eyes bright and eager. “If you write a note directly to the housekeeper, would that not be enough?”
He shook his head.
“Then we shall still go!” Sir John boomed, slamming his fist lightly on the table. “We are almost there already, no need to call the whole thing off. You can leave for London tomorrow, Brandon, no harm done.”
“I wish it were that easy. I have not a single hour to spare.”
Willoughby leaned back in his chair, amused. “If you would only delay your journey six hours, wait for us to return from Whitwell, you would not be behind at all.”
“I cannot.”

Elinor caught the low murmur Willoughby whispered directly in Marianne’s ear, his tone teasing: “Some folks simply cannot bear a day of unplanned fun. I’d wager fifty guineas that letter he received was written in his own hand, just to get out of the trip.”
Marianne’s eyes crinkled with a secret smile as she whispered back, “I have no doubt of it at all.”

Sir John spent another ten minutes arguing with him to change his mind, listing off all the people who had traveled specially for the excursion: the two Miss Careys from Newton, the three Miss Dashwoods walking all the way up from the cottage, Willoughby rising two hours earlier than usual just to make the dawn start. But Colonel Brandon would not budge.
“Then when will you be back?” Lady Middleton asked, softening. “We shall just reschedule the Whitwell trip for when you return, of course.”
“You are very kind. But I cannot possibly say when my business will allow me to come back. I cannot promise a date at all.”
“Oh, he’ll be back by the end of the week, or I shall ride to London and drag him home myself!” Sir John laughed.
“Quite right,” Mrs. Jennings cackled. “Then we might finally find out what this mysterious business of his is.”
“I do not pry into other men’s secrets,” Sir John said, “but I half suspect it is something he is too embarrassed to admit to.”

Colonel Brandon’s horses were announced at that moment. “You are not riding all the way to London, are you?” Sir John asked, surprised.
“Only as far as Honiton. I shall take a post-chaise from there. I truly cannot delay.”
He went around the table to take his leave of everyone. When he reached Elinor, he paused. “I hope I shall have the chance to see you and your sisters in London this winter?”
“I am afraid that is very unlikely,” Elinor said gently.
“Then I must say goodbye for far longer than I would wish.” To Marianne, he gave only a quiet, reserved bow, no words at all, before he followed Sir John out of the room.

No sooner had the door shut than all the polite restraint vanished. Everyone complained at once, bemoaning how cruel it was to have their perfect day spoiled.
“But I know exactly what his secret business is!” Mrs. Jennings announced, triumphant.
“Who is it, ma’am?” half the table chorused.
“Miss Williams, of course! She is a very near relation of the Colonel’s—so near I daren’t say exactly how, for fear of shocking you young ladies,” she lowered her voice to Elinor’s ear. “His natural daughter, my dear. The spitting image of him, too. Mark my words, he will leave her every penny he owns.”

When Sir John trundled back into the parlour a few minutes later, he threw his support behind a new plan: they would not waste the rest of the day. Since Whitwell was out of reach, they would take carriages and drive all across the surrounding countryside, make their own fun.
The first carriage brought round was Willoughby’s bright, flashy curricle. Marianne did not hesitate a second before hopping up onto the seat beside him, her face lit up brighter than the sun. He snapped the reins, and the pair tore off down the park drive at a gallop, vanishing down the lanes long before the rest of the party had even climbed into their own carriages. They were gone for hours, only returning long after everyone else had finished their slow drive across the downs. Both were pink-cheeked, glowing, and refused to say anything more than that they had stuck to the quiet little back lanes, far away from other travelers.

That evening, a dance was planned, and more guests arrived for dinner, until nearly twenty people sat around the long table. Willoughby took his usual place between Elinor and Marianne, while Mrs. Jennings sat on Elinor’s right. They had barely begun their first course before Mrs. Jennings leaned across the back of Elinor’s chair, her voice loud enough for both Marianne and Willoughby to hear, cackling. “I found you two out, no matter how you tried to hide it. I know exactly where you spent your morning.”
Marianne blushed scarlet. “Wherever do you mean?”
“Did you think I would not notice you took the curricle out?” Willoughby laughed.
“Oh, I knew that, you impudent thing,” Mrs. Jennings said, grinning. “I sent my maid to ask your groom, and he told me every last detail. You drove out to Allenham, did you not? Spent hours wandering the gardens and walking all through the great house? I do hope you liked it, Miss Marianne. It is a lovely big place, though it was dreadfully in need of new furniture when I visited six years ago. When I come to call on you there someday, I do hope you will have fitted it all out new.”

Marianne turned away, flustered beyond words, while Mrs. Jennings laughed so hard her wig tilted sideways. Elinor could scarcely believe it. It seemed utterly improper that Willoughby would bring Marianne to Allenham, to wander through Mrs. Smith’s private home, when Marianne had never even been introduced to the old lady. No sooner had they left the dining parlour than she pulled Marianne aside to question her. She was even more shocked to find every word Mrs. Jennings had said was entirely true.
“Why on earth would you think we should not go?” Marianne said, half cross that Elinor would doubt her. “You have said a hundred times you wished to see the inside of Allenham yourself.”
“I did, but I would never go traipsing through it while the owner was inside, with no chaperone but Mr. Willoughby!”
“Willoughby is the only person who has a right to show that house off. And we were in an open curricle, there was no room for any other guest. I never had a happier morning in all my life.”
“Just because it was pleasant does not mean it was proper, Marianne.”
“Nonsense! If there had been anything wrong with what we did, I would have felt it at once. You always know when you are acting incorrectly, and I could never have been that happy if I had done something wrong.”
“But the gossip from Mrs. Jennings—does that not make you wonder if you were a little unwise?”
“If every silly remark Mrs. Jennings makes counts as proof of impropriety, we would all be sinning every minute of the day. I care no more for her censure than I do for her praise. We only walked through the grounds and the empty rooms of the house. It will be Willoughby’s home one day, after all—”
“Even if it was to be your home one day, Marianne, that does not make it right to wander through it uninvited now.”

Marianne blushed deep, but her smile was so bright, so full of quiet delight, that Elinor did not have the heart to scold her further. After ten minutes of quiet, dreamy thought, Marianne came back to Elinor, gushing, “It really was so lovely, Elinor. There is that little corner sitting room upstairs, you know? Windows on two whole sides. One looks over the bowling green, past the hanging woods down to the valley, the other looks straight out at the church, the village, and all those steep, wild hills we love so much. The furniture is terribly shabby right now, but Willoughby says a mere two hundred pounds would turn it into the nicest summer parlour in all of England.”
If the rest of the party had not been chattering and calling them over to the dancing room, Marianne would have happily described every single room in the great house, down to the smallest cupboard, and Elinor would have sat and listened, knowing full well her sister was already mapping out the rest of her life there, hand in hand with Willoughby.

Colonel Brandon’s abrupt, unannounced departure from Barton Park had left Mrs. Jennings chattering and puzzling for three full days, her curiosity stoked so bright it rivaled the hearth fire in her parlor. There was no greater delight for the warm, gossiping old widow than parsing the comings and goings of every person in her acquaintance, and she barely paused her speculation long enough to butter her breakfast scones. She was certain no ordinary errand could have pulled the quiet, steady colonel away so fast, and ran through every possible misfortune that might have befallen him one by one, determined she would not miss the truth.
“It must be something terribly sad, that I am sure of,” she’d exclaim over her tea. “I saw it written all over his poor face. The Delaford estate never brought in more than two thousand a year, and his brother left all its accounts a tangled mess—he must have been sent for over money troubles, what else could it be? I’d give every last penny of my pin money to know the truth of it! Or wait—maybe it’s about that Miss Williams he’s always so tight-lipped about? He looked so guilty when I mentioned her the other day, didn’t he? She’s sure to be sick in town, that girl’s always been delicate as a lily. Though, no, he’s far too prudent to still be in debt over the estate… maybe his sister in Avignon has taken a turn for the worse, that would explain him riding off at such a mad dash, no time to even say proper goodbyes. Well, whatever it is, I wish him clear of all his troubles soon, and a sweet, good wife to keep him company into the bargain!”
Every new passing thought shifted her theory, and every new theory felt just as plausible as the last. Elinor, for her part, was genuinely fond of the colonel and did hope he was well, but she could not summon nearly the degree of endless, breathless speculation Mrs. Jennings pressed on her. For one thing, the colonel’s sudden trip hardly struck her as such a shocking, earth-shaking event—but far more of her wonder was occupied elsewhere, fixed on the strange, stubborn silence her sister and Willoughby maintained. They carried themselves so openly, so tenderly to each other, every glance, every lingering hand, every private laugh a declaration of their attachment, yet not a word of an engagement had crossed their lips to Mrs. Dashwood or to her. Elinor could easily guess marriage was not immediately within their power: Sir John had estimated Willoughby’s estate brought in only six or seven hundred a year, and he lived far too freely for that sum, often joking about his own empty pockets. But this total secrecy, hiding a bond that was obvious to every soul in the whole parish, made no sense at all. It was so contrary to the open, unguarded nature both Marianne and Willoughby wore so easily that sometimes a tiny, unspoken doubt crept into Elinor’s chest, and she dared not ask her sister the question burning on her tongue.
Willoughby’s devotion to the whole Dashwood household could not have been more plain. To Marianne, he gave every bit of tender, adoring attention a smitten lover could muster, and to the rest of the family he was as warm and attentive as a beloved son or brother. Barton Cottage had become more of a home to him than Allenham ever was: he spent far more hours of his days there than at his own relative’s estate, and if there was no planned dinner at the Park to draw them away, every morning walk he set out on would end at the cottage threshold, where he would sit at Marianne’s side for the rest of the afternoon while his shaggy, favorite pointer napped contentedly at her feet.
One evening, a full week after Colonel Brandon had left the county, he seemed softer, more open than ever, brimming with affection for every little thing in the small, cozy cottage. When Mrs. Dashwood casually mentioned she had planned to make small improvements to the house come spring, fixing wobbly sills and patching cracked plaster, he protested with such vehemence it made them all laugh.
“What!” he cried. “Improve this dear, perfect cottage? No, I will never consent to it. Not a single stone added to its walls, not an extra inch to its rooms, if you care even a little for my feelings.”
“Do not fret,” Elinor said, smiling. “No such work will ever happen. My mother will never have enough spare money to afford it.”
“Thank heaven for that,” he said, brightening. “May she always be too poor to waste money ruining a place that already holds all the happiness in the world.”
“Thank you, Willoughby,” Mrs. Dashwood laughed. “But I promise you, I would never sacrifice the attachment of you, or any person I love, for any trivial improvement. You may be sure that whatever small sum I have left over in the spring, I would far rather tuck it away unused than change this place in a way that pains you. But are you truly so fond of it that you cannot see a single flaw?”
“I am,” he said, earnest and soft. “To me, it is faultless. More than that, I think this is the only sort of house where true happiness can be found. If I were rich tomorrow, I would tear Combe House down to its foundations, and rebuild it exactly, exactly like this little cottage.”
“Even with its dark, narrow staircase and the kitchen that smokes something terrible whenever we roast a joint?” Elinor teased.
“Even that,” he agreed, eager as ever. “Not a single quirk, not a single convenience or inconvenience altered by even a hair. Then, and only then, could I be as happy at my own home as I have been here at Barton.”
“I have no doubt,” Elinor replied, “that even with better rooms and a wider staircase, you will one day find your own house just as faultless as you find this one.”
“There are circumstances that would make it very dear to me,” he said, his eyes drifting to Marianne. “But this place will always hold a claim on my heart that no other house can ever match.”
Mrs. Dashwood looked over to Marianne, whose luminous dark eyes were fixed unwaveringly on Willoughby, every line of her soft face declaring she understood every word he said perfectly.
“Do you know,” Willoughby added, “this time last year, when I was staying at Allenham, I used to ride past this empty cottage every week and wish so badly that some good, happy family would move in. I admired its little view over the valley so much, I grieved that it stood empty. I never thought then that the very first news Mrs. Smith would tell me when I came back to the country was that Barton Cottage was let! I felt such a rush of joy the moment I heard it, the only explanation can be that some part of me already knew all the happiness I would find here, didn’t it, Marianne?”
He spoke the last line in a low, soft voice only for her to hear, then turned back to the rest of the family. “And now you would spoil that perfect simplicity with your imaginary improvements! This very parlor, where we first met, where we have spent so many lovely hours together—you would turn it into a common entrance hall, when it holds more warmth and comfort than the grandest, largest drawing room in the whole world.”
Mrs. Dashwood promised him again no renovations of the sort would ever be made.
“You are the kindest woman alive,” he said, warmly. “That promise puts my whole heart at ease. Extend it just a little further, and make me perfectly happy: tell me not only that your house will stay just as it is, but that you and Elinor and Marianne will never change, that you will always welcome me here with the same kindness that has made every part of this cottage feel like mine.”
They promised him that easily, and Willoughby spent the rest of the evening glowing with open, unhidden joy and affection. As he pulled on his coat to leave later that night, Mrs. Dashwood called after him, “We shall hope to see you for dinner tomorrow, won’t we? I won’t ask you to come by in the morning—all three of us are walking over to the Park to call on Lady Middleton.”
He smiled, and bowed, and promised he would be back at Barton Cottage by four o’clock sharp.

The very next day, Mrs. Dashwood set off to pay her promised call on Lady Middleton, accompanied by Elinor and Margaret. Marianne begged off at the last minute, pleading a stack of trivial unfinished chores she could not bear to leave half-done, and her mother smiled knowingly, perfectly certain Willoughby had promised the night before to call on Marianne while the rest of the household was out. No one pressed her to come.

When they trundled back up the cottage drive hours later, they spotted Willoughby’s sleek curricle and his small liveried servant waiting by the door, and Mrs. Dashwood’s face lit up—her hunch, she thought, had been exactly right. But the second they stepped across the threshold, something was very wrong. Marianne came tearing out of the parlor, cheeks streaked with fresh tears, handkerchief pressed tight to her eyes, so lost in her own grief she did not even notice her family. She fled up the stairs three at a time without a word.

Shocked and afraid, the rest of them hurried into the parlor she had just left, to find Willoughby leaning heavy against the mantelpiece, his back turned to the room, like he was fighting to steady himself enough to face them. He spun around when he heard them enter, and his face was raw, red-rimmed, crumpled with the same agony that had just overtaken Marianne.
“Is she ill?” Mrs. Dashwood cried, stepping forward at once.
“I hope not,” he said, forcing a wobbly, unnatural smile, trying to sound light when every line of his body screamed distress. “It is I who ought to be the one unwell. I have just had the cruellest possible disappointment.”
“A disappointment?”
“Mrs. Smith, this morning, has chosen to exercise the right her wealth gives her over her poor dependent cousin. She has dispatched me on urgent business to London. I have only just received my orders, said my goodbyes at Allenham, and came straight here to say farewell to all of you.”
“London? You are leaving this morning?”
“Almost this very instant.”
“How terrible. But surely Mrs. Smith will not keep you away from us long?”
He flushed hot, his gaze darting away. “You are far too kind. But I have no intention of coming back to Devonshire any time soon. My visits to Mrs. Smith’s estate never last less than twelve months apart.”
“Is Mrs. Smith your only friend? Is Allenham the only house in this whole neighborhood you are welcome? Shame on you, Willoughby—you do not need an invitation to Barton Cottage.”
His color deepened, and he stared fixed at the floor, voice thick. “You are far too good to me.”

Mrs. Dashwood glanced over at Elinor, bewildered, and Elinor was equally stunned. No one spoke for long, the room heavy with unspoken confusion.
“My dear Willoughby,” Mrs. Dashwood said at last, “you will always have a home at Barton Cottage, whenever you choose to come. I will not press you to return before you are sure it will not offend Mrs. Smith— I trust your judgment, and I would never doubt that you want to come back.”
“My current circumstances,” Willoughby stammered, so flustered he could barely get the words out, “are such that I—I dare not flatter myself I can take you up on that.” He trailed off, unable to finish. Mrs. Dashwood was too shocked to speak, and another heavy silence fell.
“It is foolish to linger here any longer,” he said at last, with a hollow little smile. “I will not torment myself staying around friends whose company I cannot bear to leave right now.”
He said a hasty, stiff goodbye to all of them and fled the room. They watched him climb into his curricle, snap the reins, and in less than a minute he was gone over the hill. Mrs. Dashwood could not speak for the weight of her fear and grief, and slipped off to her own room to cry alone.

Elinor was just as uneasy as her mother. She turned the whole strange scene over in her head, tight with doubt. Willoughby’s odd, distant goodbye, his obvious embarrassment, the fake cheer he had forced, and worst of all, the way he would not accept their open invitation—none of it was like the Willoughby they had known, none of it was how a man in love with Marianne would act. One moment she feared he had never meant to make any serious, honest commitment to her sister, the next she wondered if they had fought bitterly before they got home. And yet Marianne loved him so completely, so fiercely, that a real quarrel felt almost impossible. No matter what had happened between them, her sister’s grief was undeniable, and Elinor’s heart ached for the violent sorrow she knew Marianne was clinging to upstairs, letting it wash over her entirely, no urge to hide or control it.

Half an hour later, Mrs. Dashwood came back down, eyes red but face determined, and sat down to pick up her sewing.
“Our dear Willoughby is miles away from Barton by now, Elinor,” she said softly. “And he is carrying a very heavy heart, I know.”
“It is all so strange,” Elinor said. “He left in an instant. Only last night he was here, happy, warm, so openly affectionate to Marianne. And now, with barely ten minutes warning, he is gone, saying he will not come back for a year! He was not himself, he did not speak like himself, you saw it too. What could have happened? Did they quarrel? Why else would he refuse your invitation so strangely?”
“It was not that he did not want to accept it, Elinor, I saw that clear as day. He was not allowed to. I have thought it all over, and I have an explanation that makes perfect sense.”
“You do?”
“Of course. Mrs. Smith has guessed how he feels about Marianne, and disapproves. She probably has plans for him to marry some rich heiress of her own choosing, so she has invented this fake London errand to get him out of Devonshire and separate them. He knows she will never approve of the match, so he cannot tell her he and Marianne are engaged, and as her poor dependent cousin, he has no choice but to obey her, and stay away for a while. You will say it might not be true, I know, since you love to doubt where you can, but I will not let you talk me out of trusting this. Can you think of any other explanation that makes as much sense?”
“None, I admit,” Elinor said. “I will hope you are right. But it would have been so much more like Willoughby to tell us the truth plainly. Secrecy might make sense, but I still cannot help wondering why he would hide it from us.”
“You cannot blame him for stepping out of his usual character when he has no other choice. So you agree he deserves the benefit of the doubt? That makes me happy—he is acquitted of all blame, then.”
“Not entirely. It makes sense that they would hide their engagement from Mrs. Smith, if they are engaged. But there is no reason to hide it from us.”
“Hide it from us? My dear child, you cannot possibly think they have been secretive! Every day you have looked at them and thought they were too open, too careless with their affection for anyone watching. His behavior for the last fortnight has declared a hundred times over that he loves Marianne, that he sees her as his future wife, that he thinks of all of us as family. Has he not asked for my consent with every look, every kind attentive gesture? How can you possibly doubt they are engaged? Why would Willoughby, who must know how deeply Marianne loves him, leave her for months at a time without saying a single word to confirm his feelings?”
“Every single thing points to an engagement except one,” Elinor said. “Neither of them has ever said a single word about it to any of us. And that one fact weighs heavier than all the rest, for me.”
“You are far too hard on him,” Mrs. Dashwood laughed, half exasperated. “Willoughby is an honorable, kind man. No one in the whole county has ever spoken a bad word about him. Why would you assume he is deceitful?”
“I hope he is not, I believe he is not,” Elinor cried. “I love him, truly, and the thought of doubting his honor hurts me as much as it hurts you. It was not my choice, I was just so shocked by how different he acted this morning. But I will not encourage those doubts. He had just said goodbye to Marianne, and saw her break down in tears right before we walked in. He was embarrassed, caught between not wanting to anger Mrs. Smith and not wanting to seem rude to us. It is no wonder he was flustered.”
“That is exactly it,” her mother said, relieved. “Their marriage will be a long way off, with his situation so dependent on Mrs. Smith, so a little secrecy right now is the only sensible choice.”

Margaret walked into the room just then, and Elinor fell quiet, turning all her mother’s words over in her head, letting her old unease soften, hoping with all her heart she was right.

Marianne did not appear again until dinner time. She walked silently to her place at the table, eyes puffy and swollen, and could not bring herself to meet any of her family’s gazes. She would not eat a bite, would not say a word, and when her mother reached across the table to squeeze her hand gently, her last little bit of composure shattered. She burst into loud, unrestrained sobs and ran straight back out of the room. That raw, unmanageable grief lasted the whole rest of the evening. Marianne had no desire to control her feelings, no will to calm herself down. The smallest, most accidental mention of anything that even tangentially reminded her of Willoughby broke her all over again, and no matter how carefully her family watched their words, it was impossible to avoid every single thing tied to the man she loved more than life itself.

Marianne Dashwood would have deemed herself unforgivably selfish if she’d managed to sleep a single sound hour the first night after Willoughby rode away. To wake the next morning well-rested, unburdened by grief, would have felt like a betrayal of every tender promise they’d whispered to each other beneath Barton’s ancient oak trees – too shameful for her to ever face her mother or sisters. As it happened, she had no chance to feel that guilt at all: she lay wide awake till dawn, sobbing through the majority of the dark hours, her pillows crumpled and soaked with tears. She rose at sunrise with a throbbing headache, too raw to speak, too sick at heart to swallow a single bite of food, and waved off every gentle attempt at comfort her family offered. No one could soothe a grief as fierce and fiery as hers.

Once breakfast was cleared away, she slipped out alone, wandering the lanes of Allenham for hours, lingering at every spot that held a memory of Willoughby: the fallen log where they’d shared a picnic of wild strawberries, the stone wall where he’d sketched her as she laughed, the cottage garden where he’d plucked her a red rose that still retained its scent pressed between the pages of her favourite poetry book. She cried almost nonstop till midday, replaying every word he’d said the morning he mounted his horse to leave.

That evening, she sank into the same soft, sharp indulgence of grief. She played every song on the pianoforte that they’d ever sung together, every duet where their voices had tangled perfectly, till her fingers ached, and she sat staring at the handwritten sheet music he’d copied out for her, tears dripping onto the ink, until her heart felt so heavy there was no more space for sadness to settle. This quiet torture became her daily ritual: hours at the piano, half-singing, half-sobbing, her voice breaking completely when the memory of his laugh played in her head. She only read the books they’d pored over side by side, turning to every marked, dog-eared page, and letting the contrast between their happy past and her empty present twist her heart all over again.

Such wild, unregulated pain could not burn bright forever. After a few days, it softened into a quieter, constant melancholy, but her long solitary walks, her silent hours sitting by the open window staring down the lane Willoughby had taken, still sparked sudden, loud bursts of weeping that shook her whole body.

No letter arrived from Willoughby, and Marianne never once asked for one. Mrs Dashwood was surprised by the silence, but Elinor’s gentle worry bloomed into sharp unease. Her mother, though, was ever-ready with a romantic, convenient explanation that soothed her own mind completely. “You forget, Elinor,” she said, “Sir John fetches and drops off all our post himself. We already agreed their engagement must be a secret for now – how could they possibly exchange letters without Sir John fumbling through them and spilling the news to every servant in the shire?”

Elinor could not argue that logic, and tried to cling to it to calm her own fears. But there was one simple, straightforward way to settle all the mystery at once, and she could not stop herself from suggesting it. “Why do you not just ask Marianne if she and Willoughby are engaged?” she said. “From you, her dearest, most indulgent mother, the question could never give offence. She has never hidden anything from you, not once.”

“I would never do such a thing for the world,” Mrs Dashwood protested. “If by some chance they are not yet engaged, how much pain that enquiry would inflict! Even if they are, it would be cruel to force her to reveal a secret they have chosen to keep private. I would never earn back her trust if I pressured her into a confession she is not ready to give. I know my Marianne – she loves me too well, and I will be the very first person she tells the moment the time is right. I will never force the confidence of someone I love, least of all my own child.”

Elinor thought that delicacy was overstrained, given how young Marianne was, and tried to argue further, but to no avail. Common prudence, practical care, all common sense was drowned out in Mrs Dashwood’s soft, romantic respect for her daughter’s feelings.

For days, no one in the family dared speak Willoughby’s name in front of Marianne, though Sir John and Mrs Jennings had no such tact: their teasing jibes about her pining for her handsome suitor only added fresh pain to her already raw days. Until one evening, when Mrs Dashwood picked up a volume of Shakespeare by accident, and exclaimed, “Marianne, we never finished *Hamlet*, did we? Our dear Willoughby left before we could get to the final act. We will tuck it away, so when he comes back… though it may be months, perhaps, before he returns.”

“Months?” Marianne cried, her head flying up, her eyes bright with shocked disbelief. “No – not months. It will only be weeks.”

Mrs Dashwood apologized immediately for her careless words, but Elinor felt a quiet spark of relief. Marianne’s outburst made it clear: she had absolute faith in Willoughby, absolute certainty he intended to come back for her.

A week after Willoughby had ridden away, Elinor finally succeeded in persuading Marianne to join her and Margaret on their daily walk, instead of slipping off alone as she had done every day before. She had been deliberately avoiding all company on her rambles: if her sisters planned to walk the downs, she snuck off to the narrow lanes, if they spoke of heading to the valley, she climbed the hills alone, vanishing before they could call her over. But Elinor refused to let her shut herself away any longer, and at last Marianne gave in.

They walked along the valley road in near silence: Marianne’s thoughts were too tangled, too tied up in Willoughby to make small talk, and Elinor was content to have pulled her sister out of the cottage, making no further demands. Beyond the mouth of the valley, the land opened up, rich but less wild, and they found themselves on the long stretch of highway they had travelled the very first day they arrived at Barton. They stopped there, a spot they had never reached on any of their prior walks, to gaze out at the view of their cottage far off in the distance.

Off down the road, they soon spotted a figure on horseback, riding fast towards them. In minutes they could see he was a gentleman – and Marianne’s face lit up so bright it outshone the sun. “It’s him! It is, truly!” she cried, and started running towards him at a full sprint.

“Marianne, no,” Elinor called after her, hurrying to catch up. “You are mistaken. That is not Willoughby. He is not tall enough, he does not move with Willoughby’s easy, bright grace.”

“He is! He is!” Marianne insisted, not slowing down. “His coat, his horse, his smile – I knew he would come back soon!”

Elinor hurried to keep pace with her, half-afraid the stranger would notice Marianne’s wild excitement and think her foolish, and they were soon only thirty yards away. Marianne glanced up at the rider again, and the colour drained straight from her face. Her heart plummeted, and she spun on her heel to rush back the way she’d come, when two familiar voices called her name, and a third – almost as familiar to her as Willoughby’s – begged her to stop.

She turned around, stunned, to see Edward Ferrars.

He was the only person in the whole world who could have not been Willoughby and not made her burst into fresh tears of disappointment right there on the road. He was the only one who could draw a smile from her, even through her damp eyes. She brushed her tears away, grinning to greet him, and for a little while her own heartache faded, replaced by joy for Elinor.

He dismounted, handed his horse off to his servant, and walked back with them towards Barton Cottage, for he had ridden there that day expressly to visit them. The whole family welcomed him warmly, but none more so than Marianne, who greeted him with far more open warmth than even Elinor herself. To Marianne’s eyes, though, something was very wrong. Edward was supposed to be in love with Elinor – so why was he so stiff? He looked flustered, hardly seemed pleased to see them at all, no bright smile, no rapturous greeting. He barely spoke unless someone asked him a direct question, and he did not give Elinor a single special look, not a single gentle gesture a lover would offer. Marianne watched him, more and more confused, until she almost felt a flicker of dislike for Edward. How dull his manners were, compared to Willoughby’s bright, open warmth.

After the first flurry of greetings, Marianne asked if he had ridden straight from London. No, he told her, he had been in Devonshire for a full fortnight.

“A fortnight?” Marianne repeated, shocked. He had been in the same county as Elinor for two whole weeks, and had not come to see her till now? He looked uncomfortable as he explained he had been staying with friends near Plymouth.

“Did you visit Sussex recently?” Elinor asked him, softly.

“I was at Norland a month ago,” he replied.

“Oh, dear Norland!” Marianne exclaimed. “How does it look?”

“Dear Norland,” Elinor said, smiling a little, “no doubt it looks as it always does this time of year. The woods and paths are carpeted thick with fallen dead leaves.”

“Oh!” Marianne cried, her eyes glistening. “I used to feel such wild joy watching those leaves fall! I would walk for miles, laughing as the wind sent them swirling all around me. No one there cares for that now, though. They see leaves as a nuisance, sweep them up as fast as they can, and hide them from sight.”

“Not everyone shares your passion for dead leaves, Marianne,” Elinor said, gently.

“No, my feelings are rarely understood,” Marianne murmured, drifting off into a daydream for a moment, before she shook herself and pointed out the view to Edward. “Look, there is Barton Valley. Stare at those hills – have you ever seen anything so beautiful? To the left, tucked in the woods, you can see the edge of Barton Park. And beyond that furthest grand hill, that is our little cottage.”

“It is a pretty place,” Edward said. “Though those low meadows down there must be horribly muddy in the winter.”

“Mud?” Marianne said, bewildered, under her breath. “How can you look at all this beauty and only think of mud?”

“Are your neighbours pleasant here?” he asked, ignoring her shock. “Are the Middletons good company?”

“Not at all,” Marianne said immediately. “We could not have chosen worse people to live near.”

“Marianne!” Elinor cried, horrified. “How can you say that? They are a most respectable family, and they have been nothing but kind to us. Have you forgotten all the happy afternoons we have spent at their home?”

“No,” Marianne mumbled quietly. “I have not forgotten all the painful afternoons, either.”

Elinor chose not to scold her further, and turned her attention back to Edward, making an effort to keep the conversation flowing, talking about their cottage, its little comforts, drawing only short, polite remarks from him. His cold reserve stung her deeply, and she felt half angry, half hurt. But she was determined to treat him as she had always treated him, to ignore his strange distance and not let him see how much he had wounded her that day.

The moment Edward Ferrars stepped across the threshold of Barton Cottage, Mrs. Dashwood’s surprise lasted no more than a single breath. In her eyes, his coming to call on Elinor at their quiet home was the most natural thing in the world, and her face broke at once into a glow of unfeigned, delighted welcome that far outstripped any fleeting wonder. He had arrived weighed down by shyness, cold reserve, a stiff awkwardness that had clung to him all the ride from Exeter—but that thin armour could not stand against the Dashwoods’ warm, unforced affection. It had already begun to crack before he crossed the door, and Mrs. Dashwood’s soft, captivating manners swept the last of it away entirely. Any man half in love with one of her daughters could not help but grow fond of her too, and Elinor’s heart lifted to see him relax at last, looking more like the easy, gentle young man she knew so well. He leaned in to ask after their days of walking and sketching, praised the sunlit charm of their cottage, admired the rolling green view out the parlour window, was kind and attentive to every one of them—and yet, there was a heavy quiet over him. He was not in spirits. The whole family noticed it at once, and Mrs. Dashwood, at once convinced his mood came from his mother’s stubborn selfishness, sat down to dinner seething a little at all overbearing parents who tried to warp their children to fit their own ambitions.

When the plates were cleared and they all pulled their chairs close around the crackling parlour fire, she turned to him with a gentle tease. “What plans has Mrs. Ferrars laid out for you now, Edward? Are you still destined to be some great famous orator, no matter how little you want it?”

He huffed a quiet, tired laugh. “No. I hope my mother has finally accepted I have no more talent for public life than I have desire for it.”

“But then how will you ever earn the fame your whole family demands of you?” she pressed on. “You have no love of spending grand sums, no knack for charming strangers, no profession, none of that brash confidence that pushes men to the top—you will find it a very hard road to make a name for yourself that way.”

“I have no wish to try,” he said softly. “I do not care to be distinguished at all. Thank heaven, no one can force me to become a genius or a great speaker against my will.”

“You have no ambition, I know that well,” Mrs. Dashwood said. “Your wishes are all perfectly moderate.”

“I daresay they are no more moderate than anyone else’s,” he replied. “I want to be perfectly happy, same as every soul alive—but I have to find that happiness in my own way. All the greatness in the world will never give it to me.”

“How strange that anyone would think it could!” Marianne cried, leaning forward, her eyes bright. “What do wealth and grandeur have to do with true happiness, at all?”

“Grandeur very little, no,” Elinor said, “but wealth has quite a great deal to do with it.”

“Elinor, for shame!” Marianne shook her head. “Money only buys happiness when there is nothing else left to give you. Past the point of a comfortable competence, it cannot bring you any real satisfaction, not where your own peace of mind is concerned.”

“Ah, then I expect we actually agree,” Elinor said, smiling. “Your idea of a competence and my idea of what counts as necessary wealth are probably almost exactly the same. The way the world works now, we both know there are no nice little comforts to be had without that baseline, don’t we? Your ideas are just framed more nobly than mine. Come, tell me—what would that perfect competence of yours amount to, a year?”

“Eighteen hundred, or two thousand a year,” Marianne said, unhesitating. “No more than that.”

Elinor laughed out loud. “Two thousand a year? I would call one thousand a year perfect wealth! I knew exactly how that answer would go.”

“And two thousand a year is hardly extravagant,” Marianne protested. “You can hardly keep a household running on less. I am not being unreasonable at all. The proper number of servants, a carriage, maybe two, and a pair of hunters—you could not possibly afford all that on a smaller income.”

Elinor bit back another smile, recognizing that Marianne was already itemizing every comfort of the future home she imagined sharing with Willoughby at Combe Magna.

“Hunters?” Edward repeated, blinking a little. “But why must you have hunters? Not everyone hunts.”

Marianne flushed. “But most people do, around here,” she said.

“I wish,” Margaret cut in suddenly, struck by a bright new thought, “that someone would give each of us a huge fortune all our very own!”

“Oh, I wish that too!” Marianne cried, her cheeks glowing, her eyes sparkling at the mere thought of such unbridled joy.

“I think we are all agreed on that wish,” Elinor said, “no matter what we say about wealth not buying happiness.”

“Oh, dear!” Margaret sighed. “How happy I would be! I do not even know what I would spend all that money on!”

Marianne’s face lit up like she could see every purchase in her mind’s eye.

“I think I would be quite stuck trying to spend that much money, if all my girls were rich without ever needing a penny of mine to do it,” Mrs. Dashwood said.

“Easily solved,” Elinor told her. “You would immediately start all those little improvements you have been planning for the cottage for months. You will run out of money faster than you know it.”

“And what a flood of orders would go out from this house to London, if you all came into a fortune!” Edward laughed, the light coming back into his face for a moment. “What a glorious day for the booksellers, the music shops, the print sellers! You, Elinor, would commission every new fine print that came out to be sent straight to Barton. And Marianne? I know her soul well enough—there is not enough sheet music in all of London to satisfy her. And the books! Thomson, Cowper, Scott—she would buy every new edition, over and over, I swear she would buy up every last copy in the city just to keep them out of the hands of people who would not appreciate a single line. She would buy every book ever written about how to admire a fine old twisted tree on a country walk. Am I right, Marianne? Forgive me if I am being saucy, but I wanted to show you I never forgot all our old little debates.”

“I love to be reminded of the past, Edward,” Marianne told him warmly. “Happy or sad, I love to look back on it. You will never offend me by talking about old times. And you are quite right about how I would spend at least a little of that money—all my spare cash would go straight to new music and new books for my shelves.”

“And the rest of the fortune would go to buying annuities for your favourite poets and their families, I expect?” Edward teased.

“No, I would have far better uses for it than that,” she said.

“Then perhaps you would give it as a prize to whoever writes the best defence of your favourite maxim— that no one can ever fall in love more than once in their whole life? I assume you still hold to that opinion?”

“Of course I do,” Marianne said firmly. “At my age, my opinions are already quite settled. I do not expect to ever see or hear anything that could change my mind on that.”

“Marianne is as steadfast as ever, you see,” Elinor said. “She has not changed one bit.”

“Only grown a little more grave than she used to be,” Edward said.

“Now, Edward,” Marianne protested, “you have no right to tease me for that. You are hardly the giddiest, most lighthearted person in the room yourself.”

“Why would you think that?” he replied, and he sighed, soft and heavy. “But gaiety was never a part of my nature, I suppose.”

“Nor is it really part of Marianne’s, either,” Elinor put in. “I would not call her a frivolous, lively girl at all. She is earnest, full of fire for everything she cares about, talks a great deal sometimes, always with all her heart—but she is not often truly merry.”

“I believe you are right,” Edward said. “And yet, all this time I have always thought of her as that bright, bubbly lively girl.”

“I catch myself making that exact kind of mistake all the time,” Elinor said. “I misjudge people completely in some way or another— decide they are far more cheerful, or far more grave, far cleverer or far stupider than they actually are, and I can never work out why I got it so wrong. Sometimes I go by what people say about themselves, far more often I go by what everyone else says about them, and never stop to take the time to judge them for myself.”

“But surely it is right to let other people’s opinions guide you?” Marianne said. “I thought our judgment was given to us only to defer to the judgment of our neighbours. That has always been your rule, Elinor, has it not?”

“Never, Marianne,” Elinor said. “My rule was never meant to make you surrender your own good sense. All I ever tried to do was influence how you behave towards people. You must not twist my meaning. I will admit, I have often wished you would be more courteous to the people we know in general—but when have I ever told you to adopt their opinions, or fall in line with their judgments on things that matter deeply?”

“You have never managed to get your sister to go along with your plan of universal gentle civility, have you?” Edward asked Elinor. “Are you making no headway at all?”

“Quite the opposite,” Elinor said, glancing pointedly at Marianne, a little amused.

“My judgment is entirely on your side of the argument,” Edward told her. “But I am afraid my actions all fall on your sister’s side. I never wish to offend anyone, but I am so foolishly shy, that I often come off as cold and negligent, when I am only held back by my own natural awkwardness. I have thought more than once that nature must have meant me to prefer the company of ordinary, uneducated people, I am so ill at ease around genteel strangers.”

“Marianne never has shyness to excuse any inattention she shows,” Elinor said.

“She knows her own worth far too well to suffer from that false kind of shame,” Edward said. “Shyness is only the result of feeling you are inferior in some way or another. If I could convince myself my manners were perfectly easy and graceful, I would never be shy again.”

“But you would still be reserved,” Marianne said. “And that is far worse.”

Edward jolted like she had struck him, his face flushing bright crimson. “Reserved? Am I reserved, Marianne?”

“Yes, very much so.”

“I do not understand you,” he said, his voice tight. “Reserved? How? In what way? What am I not telling you? What do you imagine I am hiding?”

Elinor was surprised by how deeply he had taken the comment, and hurried to laugh the tension away. “Do you not know my sister well enough to tell what she means? She calls anyone reserved who does not talk as fast as she does, and gush over every pretty sight and poem with as much rapture as she feels herself.”

Edward did not answer. The heavy, quiet gravity that had hung over him earlier settled back over him, thicker than before. He sat silent, staring into the flames, dull and withdrawn, and no one could coax another light word out of him for the rest of the evening.

Elinor’s chest ached with quiet, unshakable unease as she watched Edward Ferrars drift through their cottage days. His visit should have been pure joy, but no matter how kind his words, no light of true, unburdened warmth shone in his eyes. It was impossible to miss how unhappy he was—yet try as she might, Elinor could not be certain his old, tender affection for her had not faded entirely. One moment he would look at her with that soft, lingering gaze she’d once thought only she could draw out of him, and the next he would draw back into cold, formal reserve, leaving her to wonder if she’d imagined the warmth at all.

The next morning, before the rest of the household had roused for breakfast, he slipped into their little parlour to find Elinor and Marianne already sipping their tea. Ever eager to give the pair every chance to nurture their quiet attachment, Marianne made a hasty excuse to run up to her room, her feet barely tapping the stairs before she heard the parlour door swing open again. She spun around to find Edward standing in the entryway, flustered, stammering that he meant to walk down to the village to check on his horses before they were all ready to sit for breakfast, that he would be back before long.

When he returned an hour later, he was brightened, full of praise for the valley spread out below their cottage. From the higher ground of the village, he’d looked over every winding wooded path and sun-dappled meadow, and the view had delighted him entirely. The subject was catnip for Marianne, who immediately leaned in, ready to gush over every crag and wild patch of gorse she loved most, and press him for every detail of what had caught his eye on the walk. But Edward laughed and held up a hand to stop her.

“You must not quiz me too far, Marianne,” he said. “I know nothing at all of this ‘picturesque’ craze everyone speaks of. I shall offend you with my ignorance, I promise. I would call a steep, dramatic crag just steep, a wild, uneven thicket strange and uncouth, and any distant half-hidden hill simply out of sight, instead of attributing its soft blur to the magic of hazy atmosphere. You must be content with my honest admiration: this is a very fine country. The hills are steep, the woods full of strong, tall timber, the valley snug and welcoming, dotted with rich meadows and neat little farmhouses. It is exactly what I want beautiful scenery to be—equal parts lovely and useful. I have no doubt it counts as picturesque by your standards, full of rocks and grey moss and brambles, but all of that is quite lost on me.”
“I am afraid that is far too true,” Marianne said, frowning. “But why on earth would you boast of such a lack of taste?”
“I suspect,” Elinor cut in, smiling softly, “that Edward is running from one kind of affectation straight into another. He is sick of all the people who pretend to adore wild, untamed nature far more than they actually do, just to sound clever and refined—so he affects to care far less for the beauty around him than he really does.
