He is as stubborn about his own little brand of pretension as any of them.”
“Exactly so,” Marianne agreed, nodding vehemently. “Admiring landscape has become nothing but empty jargon now. Everyone parrot the exact turns of phrase the first writer of picturesque beauty coined, no matter what they actually feel. I detest that kind of empty talk so much I often bite my own feelings back, for fear I will have no words to describe them that have not been worn thin of all meaning.”
“And I swear to you,” Edward said, turning back to Marianne, “that you feel every bit of that deep delight in a fine prospect you claim to, and I would never doubt it. But in return, you two must allow me to feel no more than I admit. I do not care for crooked, gnarled, dying trees—give me tall, straight, flourishing oaks any day. I do not want tattered, half-ruined cottages, or fields full of nettles and thistles and wild heather. A snug, warm farmhouse makes me far happier than a distant medieval watchtower, and a crowd of laughing, well-fed villagers pleases me far more than any band of romantic banditti you could describe.”

Marianne stared at him, astonished, then turned to look at Elinor with pity, as if her sister could ever tolerate such an unromantic perspective. Elinor only laughed, and the topic was dropped. Marianne fell silent, lost in thought, until a small detail right in front of her caught her eye. Edward was sitting beside her, and as he took his cup of tea from Mrs. Dashwood, his hand passed directly in front of her face. On one of his fingers, he wore a small plain gold ring, its center set with a neatly plaited lock of hair.
“I never saw you wear a ring before, Edward!” she blurted out. “Is that Fanny’s hair? I remember her promising you a lock of hers once. But I could have sworn her hair was far darker than that.”

The second the words left her mouth, Marianne knew she had blundered. A deep, burning flush rose all the way up Edward’s neck to his ears, and he flickered a quick, guilty glance over at Elinor before answering. “Yes, it is my sister’s hair. The setting always casts a different shade over it, you know.”
Elinor had met that fleeting, flustered look, and her heart swelled soft and warm. She knew at once the hair was hers, not Fanny’s—Marianne assumed it had been a free gift from Edward’s sister, but Elinor knew full well he must have snipped it from her without her noticing, some quiet moment she had never caught. She did not take it as an insult, not for a second. She pretended not to have noticed anything amiss at all, immediately striking up a conversation about their jar of homemade plum jam to distract the rest of the room, while resolving to find an excuse to lean close to that ring later, to confirm the color was exactly the soft chestnut of her own locks.

Edward’s embarrassment lingered long after that, settling into a quiet, distracted gravity that hung over him for the rest of the morning. Marianne berated herself for her carelessness over and over, and would have felt far worse if she had known how little Elinor was actually pained by the incident.

By midday, Sir John and Mrs. Jennings arrived at the cottage, having heard news that a gentleman had come to stay, and eager to get a look at their new guest. It did not take Sir John long, helped along by his mother-in-law’s well-honed talent for prying, to connect the name Ferrars to the F they had heard young Margaret mention in relation to Elinor. A whole mine of raillery was already building in his mind, only held back by the fact that they barely knew Edward well enough to tease him about it openly. Even so, Elinor could read every one of his significant, winking looks, and knew perfectly well how much of the secret their little household had already let slip to their neighbors.

Sir John never visited the Dashwoods without pressing them to an invitation, and this time he was determined to entertain their new guest properly. “You simply must come drink tea with us tonight,” he insisted. “We shall be all alone otherwise, and tomorrow you must absolutely come dine with us—we’ll have a full, merry party.”
Mrs. Jennings chimed in at once. “And who knows? We might even push the tables back and have a dance, that’s sure to tempt you, Miss Marianne!”
“A dance?” Marianne exclaimed, blinking in surprise. “Impossible! Who is there even to dance with?”
“Who? Why you, the Careys, and the Whitakers, of course!” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “You did not think we’d have no one to dance with just because that certain unnamed gentleman is off visiting his aunt, now did you?”
“God, I wish Willoughby was here right now,” Sir John sighed loudly.
The comment made Marianne’s cheeks flame bright crimson, and new little sparks of suspicion lit up in Edward’s eyes. He leaned in to Elinor, quiet, and asked her who Mr. Willoughby was. She gave him a short, vague explanation, but Marianne’s glowing, flustered face told him far more than words ever could.

As soon as Sir John and Mrs. Jennings had taken their leave, Edward wandered over to Marianne, a faint, playful glint in his eye. “I’ve been guessing,” he whispered. “Shall I tell you what I’ve guessed about Mr. Willoughby?”
Marianne tilted her head, confused. “What on earth do you mean?”
“Shall I tell you?” he repeated, smiling.
“Certainly.”
“Then I guess that Mr. Willoughby is very, very fond of hunting.”
Marianne stared at him, half scandalized, half helpless with laughter at his quiet mischief. After a moment of flustered silence, she swatted lightly at his arm. “Oh Edward! How can you tease me so? But I hope one day soon, I am certain of it, you will meet him—and you will love him, I promise.”
Edward blinked, a little surprised at how earnestly she spoke. “I have no doubt I will,” he said. He would never have dared to make the little joke at all, if he had not assumed Willoughby was just some casual new friend of the Dashwoods, a silly little running joke between the whole household.

Edward lingered seven sweet, tangled days at the Barton cottage, every hour stitched through with quiet joy he seemed determined to burn out before it could settle. Mrs. Dashwood pressed him again and again to stay longer, but it was as though he had set his own heart to a small, sharp penance: he would tear himself away the very moment his happiness with them swelled to its brightest peak.

His spirits shifted like the valley mists those last few days, uneven but steadily lighter—he wandered the surrounding lanes with Elinor till he knew every wild rose thicket by name, leaned in to laugh at Marianne’s piano playing till his eyes crinkled, and never once spoke of leaving without a soft, unmistakeable sigh. He swore he had no engagements anywhere else, no plans at all for the days ahead, and even hesitated to name the place he would go once he turned their lane. Yet go he insisted he must. No week had ever slipped by so fast; he repeated the marvel of it over and over, and every other word he spoke gave the lie to his own stubborn resolve. There was no pleasure for him at Norland, he said. He detested the noise and cold formality of London. Their kindness was worth more to him than anything in the world, and his greatest happiness was here at Barton. Still, he would leave them at the end of the week, against their wishes and against his own, with no actual tie binding his time to anyone else.

Elinor laid all this strange, contradictory behaviour at the feet of his mother, and it was a mercy for her own frayed feelings that Mrs. Ferrars was little known to her—so easy to make her the general excuse for every oddity in her son’s actions. Disappointed, a little vexed, and sometimes stung by his uncertain, half-hearted attention to her, she still chose to view his choices with all the gentle allowance she had been forced to fight so hard to extend to Willoughby not long before. His low spirits, his secrecy, his inconsistency—all of it she blamed on his lack of independence, on his quiet dread of Mrs. Ferrars’s sharp temper and rigid plans for his future. The shortness of his visit, his iron resolve to leave, all came back to the same old, tired grief: a man forced to temporize with a parent who held every thread of his fortune and his freedom in her fists. She daydreamed of the day that struggle would end, when Mrs. Ferrars would soften, and Edward would be free to chase his own happiness. But when those wishes felt too empty, she turned back to the small, vivid proofs of his affection: every lingering glance he sent her way, every half-stammered compliment, and above all the thin, plain silver ring he never once took off his finger, the tiny, unspoken promise he wore against his skin.

“I think, Edward,” Mrs. Dashwood said at their breakfast table on his last morning, “you would be a far happier man if you had a profession to fill your days and give purpose to your plans. It might inconvenience your friends, of course—you would not have so much time to spend with us. But” she added with a soft smile, “it would benefit you greatly in at least one small way: you would never have to stand wondering where on earth to go once you left our door.”

“I assure you,” Edward replied, his voice heavy, “I have thought that very same thing for years. It has always been, and will always be, the greatest misfortune of my life that I have no necessary work to occupy me, no profession to give me purpose, or any sort of true independence. My own silly scruples, and those of my family, turned me into this idle, helpless thing. We could never agree on what path I ought to take. I always loved the church, and I still do—but that was not nearly grand enough for them. They pushed me toward the army, and that was far too loud and flashy for me. The law was deemed genteel enough—plenty of young men with chambers in the Temple shine in high society, driving flashy gigs around town—but I had no taste for it, even the shallow, performative version my family approved. The navy was fashionable, but by the time they thought of it I was too old to enter. At last, because there was never any real need for me to work at all, because I could be as frivolous and expensive without a uniform as I could be in one, they decided idleness was the most honourable and advantageous choice of all. And an eighteen-year-old boy is hardly ever stubborn enough to resist his family begging him to do nothing at all. So I was sent up to Oxford, and I have been properly idle ever since.”

“Then I suppose the consequence will be,” Mrs. Dashwood laughed, “that since leisure has not brought you happiness, your own sons will be raised to a dozen different pursuits, professions, and trades, just as Columella’s were.”

“They will be raised,” he said, very serious, “to be as unlike me as it is possible for human beings to be. In feeling, in action, in every circumstance of their lives.”

“Come now, that is just the low spirits speaking,” she chided gently. “You are melancholy this morning, and imagine anyone unlike yourself must be happier. But remember that the pain of parting from friends touches everyone at times, no matter their station or work. Your happiness is already almost yours—you only need patience, or to give it a prettier name: hope. Your mother will give you that independence you crave in time. It is her duty, and soon it must become her happiness too, to not let your whole youth waste away in discontent. A few months can change so very much.”

“I think,” Edward said, quiet and bitter, “that a great many months could pass without bringing me a single good thing.”

That despondent note hung over their parting, which came not long after, and left a heavy, uncomfortable weight on all of them—most of all Elinor, who found the ache slow to soften, even when she set her whole will to subduing it. She refused to follow the path Marianne had taken after Willoughby left, moping in solitude, hiding away from the family, wasting hours indulging grief until it grew sharper and deeper. The moment Edward’s figure vanished down the lane, Elinor sat herself at her drawing table, and worked busily the whole day. She neither sought out nor avoided talk of him, kept up her usual interest in the household’s small affairs, and if this discipline did not lessen her own grief, it at least kept it from swelling unnecessarily, so her mother and sisters never fussed over her or worried she was breaking.

To Marianne, this behaviour was no more admirable than Marianne’s own indulgence had seemed to Elinor. She had already decided that perfect self-command was impossible for anyone with real, strong feelings, and worthless for anyone who had feelings so calm they never troubled them. She could not deny her sister loved Edward, even if the admission stung—but she proved the strength of her own soft heart by loving and respecting Elinor all the same, even through that petty, mortifying conviction.

Elinor never shut herself in her room, never wandered off alone for hours, never lay awake half the night to ruminate—but she still found plenty of quiet moments to think of Edward, his words, his strange contradictory behaviour, all the shifts of her own feelings: tender one minute, pitying the next, then approving, then doubting, then softening again. When the rest of the family were busy, when no one was talking, every bit of solitude she needed wrapped around her. Her thoughts could not be chained down to her drawing. The past and the future, tied up in the man she loved, forced their way to the front of her mind, engrossing all her memory, her reflection, her fancy.

She was pulled out of one such reverie one morning, not long after Edward’s departure, by the sound of new visitors arriving. She was entirely alone in the parlour, and the click of the small wicket gate at the edge of their green front court drew her eyes to the window. A whole party was walking up the path: Sir John, Lady Middleton, Mrs. Jennings, and two strangers, a gentleman and a lady she had never seen before. Sir John spotted her at once, left the rest of the party to knock at the door, and tramped across the lawn to make her open the casement to speak to him, the space between the door and window so small they could not talk without the rest of the party overhearing.

“Well!” he grinned. “We’ve brought you new strangers. What do you think of them?”

“Hush, they will hear you!” Elinor whispered.

“Never mind if they do. It’s only the Palmers. Charlotte’s very pretty, I promise you. Look this way and you’ll see her.”

Elinor said she would be perfectly able to greet them in two minutes without craning out the window. “Where is Marianne? Did she run off to hide when she saw us coming? I saw her pianoforte is open.”

“I believe she’s out walking.”

Mrs. Jennings came bustling up before Sir John could say more, too impatient to wait to come inside before telling her story. She hollered through the open casement, “My dear, how do you do? How is Mrs. Dashwood, and where are all your sisters? What, all alone? You’ll be glad of a bit of company! I’ve brought my other son and daughter to call on you—only think how sudden! I heard a carriage last night when we were drinking tea, and never thought it could be them. I was convinced it was Colonel Brandon coming back, and I said to Sir John, ‘I do believe that’s a carriage, it must be Colonel Brandon—’”

Elinor had to turn away from her to let the rest of the party in. Lady Middleton formally introduced the two strangers, just as Mrs. Dashwood and Margaret came downstairs. They all settled in the parlour to look one another over, while Mrs. Jennings kept right on telling her story as she walked through the front hall.

Mrs. Palmer was several years younger than Lady Middleton, and entirely unlike her sister in every possible way. Short and plump, with a bright, pretty face, she carried the most unreserved, sunny good humour imaginable. Her manners were less polished than Lady Middleton’s, but far more warm and endearing. She walked in with a smile on her face, smiled through her whole visit, and was still smiling when she left, only breaking into full peals of laughter in between. Her husband was a grave, distant young man of twenty-five or six, more fashionable and seemingly sharper than his wife, but with no inclination whatsoever to be polite or make anyone feel welcome. He stepped into the room with an air of great self-importance, bowed barely to the ladies without saying a word, glanced briefly over the whole room, grabbed a newspaper off the table, and proceeded to read it for the entire rest of his visit.

Mrs. Palmer, by contrast, was full of natural, unforced cheer, and hardly sat down before she burst out gushing about how perfect their little cottage was. “Oh! What a delightful room! I never saw anything half so charming! Mama, just think how much nicer it is than the last time I was here! I always thought this such a sweet little place,” she said, turning to Mrs. Dashwood, “but you’ve made it absolutely magical! Look, sister, how lovely everything is! I want a house just like this for myself, don’t you, Mr. Palmer?”

Mr. Palmer did not answer her, and never even lifted his eyes from his newspaper.

“Mr. Palmer never hears me sometimes,” she laughed. “It’s so ridiculous!”

Mrs. Dashwood had never before thought inattention qualified as wit, and stared at them both in faint surprise. Meanwhile Mrs. Jennings kept talking at the top of her voice, recounting every detail of how shocked they had been the night before when the Palmers turned up, till every last part of the story was told. Mrs. Palmer laughed again and again at the memory, and the whole room agreed two or three times over that it was a most pleasant surprise.

“You can imagine how glad we were to see them,” Mrs. Jennings added, leaning in to Elinor and lowering her voice like it was a great secret, even though they sat on opposite sides of the parlour. “But I do wish they hadn’t rushed their journey so much, coming all the way around London on some silly business—you know,” she nodded significantly at her daughter, “it’s very bad for her in her condition. I told her she ought to stay home and rest this morning, but she insisted on coming with us, she was so desperate to see you all!”

Mrs. Palmer laughed and said it would do her no harm at all.

“She’s due to have the baby in February,” Mrs. Jennings confided further.

Lady Middleton could not bear that line of conversation, so she forced herself to ask Mr. Palmer if there was any interesting news in the paper.

“No, nothing at all,” he replied, and kept right on reading.

“Here comes Marianne!” Sir John shouted. “Now Palmer, you’re about to see the most beautiful girl you ever laid eyes on.”

He went straight out to the hall, wrenched the front door open, and ushered Marianne inside himself. Mrs. Jennings asked her the second she stepped in if she had been walking to Allenham, and Mrs. Palmer laughed so hard it was plain she knew exactly what the reference meant. Even Mr. Palmer looked up from his newspaper to stare at Marianne for several long minutes, before dropping his gaze back to his pages.

Mrs. Palmer’s eye then caught the collection of drawings hanging on the parlour walls. She jumped up to examine them. “Oh dear, these are so beautiful! Mama, look how lovely they are! I could stare at them forever!” She sat back down a minute later, and promptly forgot all about the sketches.

When Lady Middleton stood up to leave, Mr. Palmer rose too, set down his newspaper, stretched, and glanced around at all of them. “My love, have you been asleep?” his wife giggled. He did not answer her, only observed after a final look around, that the room was very low-ceilinged, and the ceiling was crooked. Then he bowed, and left with the rest of the party.

Sir John had been badgering them all the while to come dine at the park the next day. Mrs. Dashwood, who did not wish to accept more dinners at the park than she could invite the Middletons back to the cottage, refused for herself, and said her daughters could do as they pleased. But the girls had no great curiosity to watch Mr. and Mrs. Palmer eat their dinner, and no expectation of enjoying the evening, so they tried to excuse themselves too, saying the weather looked uncertain. But Sir John would not take no for an answer—he would send the carriage for them, they must come. Lady Middleton pressed them too, and Mrs. Jennings and Mrs. Palmer added their own pleas, till the young ladies had no choice but to give in.

“Why do they insist on dragging us over there?” Marianne huffed the second the door shut behind their guests. “They say the rent on this cottage is very low—but it feels a very harsh bargain, if we are expected to dine at the park every single time anyone comes to stay with them, or with us.”

“They are not being unkind by all these invitations,” Elinor said softly. “They are just as eager to be civil to us now as they were when we first arrived. The parties only feel tedious and dull because we carry a little weight in our own hearts these days. The change is not in them—it is in us.”

The next afternoon, as the Miss Dashwoods stepped through one door of the Park’s drawing room, streaks of cold November rain smudging the windows behind them, Mrs. Palmer came skittering in through the opposite entry, her cheeks pink from the chill, her good humour and giddy sparkle as bright as ever. She squeezed all three of their hands in turn, warm and affectionate as if they were dearest long-lost friends, and gushed over how delighted she was to see them.
“I was so afraid this horrid rain would keep you home!” she exclaimed, sinking onto the sofa to tuck Elinor on one side and Marianne on the other. “It would have been such a shame, for we’re off again tomorrow, you know. We absolutely must go, the Westons are coming to stay with us next week. The whole trip was such a surprise! I had no idea we were bound for Barton until the carriage was already pulled up to our door, and Mr. Palmer asked out of nowhere if I’d like to come. He’s so droll! He never tells me a single thing ahead of time. I only wish we could stay longer – but I do hope we’ll all meet again in town very soon.”
The Dashwoods were forced to gently dash that expectation, as they had no funds for a London winter.
“Not go to town?” Mrs. Palmer laughed outright, scandalised. “I shall be so disappointed if you don’t! I could get you the loveliest little house imaginable, right next to ours in Hanover Square. You simply must come. I’ll happily chaperon you to every ball and dinner for as long as you like, right up until I’m confined, if Mrs. Dashwood doesn’t care to venture out into public herself.”
They thanked her warmly, but held firm against all her entreaties, even when her husband wandered into the room mid-plea.
“Oh my love!” she called, tugging at his sleeve. “You must help me persuade the Miss Dashwoods to come to town this winter!”
Her “love” said nothing at all. He offered the ladies only the faintest, most perfunctory bow before launching into a petulant complaint about the weather.
“How detestable all this rain is,” he grumbled. “This sort of weather makes every thing and every body unbearable. The dullness seeps indoors just as it does out of doors, turning every acquaintance tedious. What on earth is Sir John thinking, not having a billiard room in his house? So few people understand what comfort even is! Sir John is every bit as stupid and lifeless as this dreary weather.”
The rest of the party soon drifted into the room, and Sir John boomed out a greeting to Marianne before leaning in teasingly.
“I’m afraid, Miss Marianne, you couldn’t take your usual walk out to Allenham today, eh?”
Marianne’s face fell grave, and she said nothing, her fingers twisting tight in the fabric of her skirt.
“Oh, don’t be so sly with us!” Mrs. Palmer giggled. “We all know all about it, I assure you! And I admire your taste immensely – he’s so very handsome, that Mr. Willoughby. We don’t live all that far from him in Somerset, you know – not more than ten miles, I’d wager.”
“Nearer thirty,” her husband corrected dryly.
“Ah, well! Hardly a difference at all!” she waved off. “I’ve never been to his house, but everyone says it’s such a sweet, pretty place.”
“The vilest, most desolate spot I have ever laid eyes on in my life,” Mr. Palmer said.
Marianne stayed perfectly silent, but her wide, bright eyes gave away just how closely she was hanging on every word.
“Is it really that ugly?” Mrs. Palmer blinked. “Well then, I suppose the pretty house everyone gushes about must belong to someone else.”
When they gathered in the dining room, Sir John groaned at the sight of only eight place settings. “My dear,” he said to his wife, “it’s so provoking we’re so few today! Why ever didn’t you ask the Gilberts to join us?”
“Sir John, I told you before you asked,” Lady Middleton replied serenely, “that it was impossible. They dined with us just last week.”
“Now you and I, Sir John,” Mrs. Jennings cut in cheerfully, “would never stand on such silly ceremony!”
“Then you would both be very ill-bred,” Mr. Palmer pointed out.
“My love, you contradict absolutely everyone!” his wife laughed, as merry as ever. “Don’t you know you’re being perfectly rude?”
“I did not contradict anyone by calling your mother ill-bred,” he said.
“Go on, abuse me as much as you like!” the good-natured old lady cackled. “You took Charlotte off my hands, and you can’t give her back. So I have the whip hand over you forever.”
Charlotte laughed so hard she snickered, gushing that she didn’t care how cross he got with her, they had to live together after all, and she wouldn’t trade his droll moods for anything. No one could have been more unshakably good-natured, or more determined to be happy, than Mrs. Palmer. Her husband’s performative indifference, insolence, and general discontent never stung her – if anything, when he scolded or snapped, she found it delightfully diverting.
“Mr. Palmer is so droll!” she whispered to Elinor later. “He’s always out of sorts!”
Elinor, watching him for a while, did not believe he was genuinely as ill-natured or ill-bred as he pretended to be. She suspected his temper was a little soured, as many a man’s was, by the realisation that he had married a very silly woman thanks to some inexplicable bias toward a pretty face – but that was too common a mistake for any sensible man to be permanently broken by. No, it was far more likely a hunger for distinction that made him treat everyone with contempt, and rail against every small thing around him. He simply wanted to appear superior to everyone else. It was a common enough motive, not worth wondering at – though his methods, while they might establish he was far ruder than anyone else, were unlikely to endear him to anyone but his wife.
“Oh, my dear Miss Dashwood!” Mrs. Palmer said, a moment later. “I have such a favour to ask of you and your sister. Will you come spend Christmas at Cleveland with us? Do say yes, especially while the Westons are staying! You can’t imagine how happy it will make me – it will be perfectly delightful! My love,” she turned to her husband, prodding his arm, “don’t you long to have the Miss Dashwoods come stay at Cleveland?”
“Certainly,” he replied, with a cold little sneer. “I came all the way to Devonshire for no other reason at all.”
“There now!” his wife beamed. “You see Mr. Palmer expects you – you simply can’t refuse.”
Elinor and Marianne both politely but resolutely declined the invitation.
“But you simply must come!” Mrs. Palmer pressed on. “I know you’ll adore it. The Westons will be there, and we’re so gay just now! Mr. Palmer is forever gallivanting around the county canvassing for the election, so there are dozens of new people coming to dine every night that I’ve never met before – it’s perfectly charming! Though poor fellow, it is terribly fatiguing for him, he has to make every single person he meets like him.”
Elinor had to bite her lip to keep from laughing as she agreed how very hard that must be.
“Won’t it be so wonderful when he’s in Parliament?” Charlotte gushed. “I shall laugh so hard! It will be absurd to see all his letters addressed to him with an M.P. on the end. But do you know, he says he will never frank my post for me? He swears he won’t. Don’t you, Mr. Palmer?”
Mr. Palmer ignored her.
“He hates writing, you know,” she continued, unphased. “He says it’s a dreadful bore.”
“No,” he snapped. “I never said anything so irrational. Don’t go palming all your silly turns of phrase on me.”
“There now, you see how droll he is!” she giggled to Elinor. “He’s always like this! Sometimes he won’t speak to me for half a whole day, then out he pops with some absurd little remark about absolutely nothing at all.”
When they returned to the drawing room, Mrs. Palmer surprised Elinor by leaning over to whisper, “You do like Mr. Palmer excessively, don’t you?”
“Certainly,” Elinor said. “He seems very agreeable.”
“I’m so glad you do! I knew you would, he’s so pleasant. And Mr. Palmer is excessively fond of you and your sisters, I can tell you – he will be dreadfully disappointed if you don’t come to Cleveland. I can’t imagine why you keep objecting!”
Elinor quickly steered the conversation away, and asked if the Palmers saw much of Mr. Willoughby, back home in Somerset. She hoped to hear a more unbiased account of his character than the Middletons’ partial acquaintance could give, something that would set all her lingering worries about Marianne at rest.
“Oh dear, yes! I know him extremely well,” Mrs. Palmer replied. “Not that I ever actually spoke to him, you understand – but I saw him constantly in town. I just never happened to be staying at Barton when he was at Allenham, what bad luck! Mama met him once here before, but I was with my uncle at Weymouth. I dare say we would see a great deal of him at Cleveland if his estate wasn’t so far out of the way. He’s hardly ever at Combe Magna anyway, from what I hear – and even if he were, Mr. Palmer would never call on him, he’s in the opposition, you know, and the place is miles out of the way. But I know exactly why you’re asking about him! Everyone says your sister is going to marry him. I’m monstrously glad of it, that means she’ll be my neighbour!”
“Upon my word,” Elinor said, “you know far more about the matter than I do, if you have reason to expect such a match.”
“Oh don’t pretend to deny it! Everyone is talking about it!” Mrs. Palmer laughed. “I heard it in London, right before we left.”
“My dear Mrs. Palmer!”
“Upon my honour, I did! I ran into Colonel Brandon on Bond Street, Monday morning just before we left town, and he told me himself.”
Elinor was stunned. “Colonel Brandon told you that? That can’t be right. Even if it were true, he would never share such private news with someone who had no particular stake in it.”
“But I assure you it’s true, let me tell you how it happened!” Charlotte insisted. “When we met him, he turned around and walked with us, we started chattering about my brother and sister, and all sorts of things, and I said to him ‘Colonel, I hear there’s a new family settled at Barton Cottage, Mama writes that they’re all very pretty, and one of the girls is going to marry Mr. Willoughby of Combe Magna. Is it true? You must know, you’ve been in Devonshire so recently.’”
“And what did the Colonel say?” Elinor asked, her heart tight.
“Oh, he didn’t say much at all! But he looked like he knew it was perfectly true, so I set it down as fact right then. It will be so delightful! When is the wedding to be?”
“I hope Colonel Brandon was well?” Elinor asked, dodging the question.
“Oh, perfectly well! He sang your praises the whole walk, wouldn’t stop saying how wonderful you are.”
“I am very flattered by his good opinion. He seems an excellent, uncommonly pleasing man.”
“So do I! He’s such a charming man, it’s a terrible pity he’s so grave and dull all the time. Mama says he was in love with your sister too, you know – and that’s such a compliment, he almost never falls in love with anyone.”
“Is Mr. Willoughby well known in your part of Somerset?” Elinor asked.
“Oh, everyone knows him! Though I suppose not many are actually intimate with him, since Combe Magna is so far out of the way – but every last person thinks he’s extremely agreeable. No one is more liked than Mr. Willoughby wherever he goes, you can tell your sister that. She’s a monstrous lucky girl to get him, though he’s far luckier to get her – she’s so handsome and sweet, nothing could ever be good enough for her. Though I don’t think she’s all that much handsomer than you, you know – I think you’re both excessively pretty, and Mr. Palmer thinks so too, even if he wouldn’t admit it last night.”
Elinor was glad for even that small, unprompted testimony in Willoughby’s favour.
“I am so glad we’ve finally become friends!” Charlotte went on cheerfully. “Now I hope we shall always be great companions. You can’t imagine how long I’ve wanted to meet you! It’s perfect that you live at the cottage. And I’m so glad your sister is going to make such a good marriage! I hope you’ll visit them at Combe Magna all the time, everyone says it’s such a sweet place.”
“Have you known Colonel Brandon very long?” Elinor asked.
“Oh, ages! Ever since I married my Mr. Palmer. He’s a great particular friend of Sir John’s,” she added, lowering her voice to a giddy whisper. “I do believe he would have been very glad to marry me, if he could. Sir John and Lady Middleton wished for it desperately. But Mama didn’t think the match was good enough for me, otherwise Sir John would have asked him, and we would have been married straight away.”
“Had Colonel Brandon never spoken of his regard for you to you, himself, before Sir John proposed the match to your mother?”
“Oh no! But if Mama hadn’t objected, I’m sure he would have asked me, right away. He’d only seen me twice back then, I was still at school. But I’m far happier as I am now, anyway. Mr. Palmer is exactly the sort of droll, fun husband I like.”

The Palmers departed for Cleveland the very next morning, leaving the two households at Barton to fall back on one another’s company for entertainment. But that quiet lull barely lasted long enough for Elinor to untangle her thoughts of their recent visitors: she still found herself wondering how Charlotte Palmer could be perpetually giddy and content with no discernible cause, how Mr. Palmer could be so dryly sharp and clever yet choose to fade into gruff silence beside his frivolous wife, and how very common, and very strange, that deep, quiet mismatch between husbands and wives often was. Before she could settle those thoughts, Sir John and Mrs. Jennings’ relentless, zeal for social merriment had already lined up a new set of acquaintances for the Dashwood sisters to meet and observe.

On a spontaneous morning trip into Exeter, the pair had stumbled across two young women whom Mrs. Jennings had thrilled to discover were distant relations of hers. That tiny connection was more than enough for Sir John: he immediately invited the two girls to stay at Barton Park the second their existing engagements in Exeter were finished. The girls threw aside their previous plans the moment the invitation was offered, and when Sir John returned home to break the news, Lady Middleton was thrown into a flutter of very real alarm. She was to play host to two girls she had never laid eyes on, with no proof of their elegance, or even their basic gentility, and her husband and mother’s blithe assurances on that front counted for nothing at all. Their being distant cousins only made the situation worse. Mrs. Jennings’ attempts to console her daughter fell flat immediately, as she chirped that they need not worry over the girls being fashionable—they were all family, after all, so they simply had to put up with one another. There was no calling the visit off now, so Lady Middleton resigned herself to the arrangement with all the frosty philosophy of a well-bred woman, contenting herself only with giving her husband gentle, pointed reprimands about the matter five or six times each day.

When the young ladies arrived, they were far from unrefined or unfashionable: their dresses were bright and new, their manners soft and civil, they gushed over every inch of Barton Park’s house, and fell into raptures over every piece of furniture they saw. Most importantly, they doted on the Middleton children so fiercely that Lady Middleton’s good will was fully won before they had even spent an hour in the house. She declared them perfectly agreeable girls—high praise, coming from her ladyship, who rarely wasted energy on enthusiasm. Buoyed by her praise, Sir John immediately trotted down to the Dashwood cottage to tell them the Miss Steeles had arrived, and to swear they were the sweetest girls in all of England. Elinor did not put much stock in that kind of praise; she knew very well that “the sweetest girls in the world” could be found in every corner of the country, in every shape, with every sort of face, temper, and wit.

Sir John begged the whole family to walk up to the Park that very instant to meet his guests. He was a benevolent, gregarious man who could not bear the thought of hoarding new friends even from people who were barely related to him.
“Come now, do,” he pressed. “Pray come—you simply must. You can’t imagine how much you’ll like them. Lucy is shockingly pretty, and so good humoured! The children are hanging all over her already as if they’ve known her for years. And both of them are dying to meet you most of all—they heard in Exeter that you three are the most beautiful creatures alive, and I told them that’s the whole truth, and more besides. They brought a whole coach full of toys for the children, too. How can you be so cross as to refuse? You know you’re cousins after a fashion—you’re my cousins, they’re my wife’s, so it stands to reason you’re related.”

He could not bully them into coming that very minute, though. The most he could extract was a promise that they would call at the Park within the next two days, before he left them, bewildered at their lack of haste, to go back and brag about the Dashwoods to the Steeles exactly as he had bragged about the Steeles to the Dashwoods.

When their promised visit took place, the sisters found nothing to admire in the eldest Miss Steele, Anne, who was nearly thirty, with a plain, vacant face that bore no hint of sharpness. But the younger, Lucy, who was barely two-and-twenty, was undeniably pretty: her features were soft and bright, she had a quick, sharp eye and a crisp, lively air that lent her a distinct charm even if she had no real grace or elegant poise. Their manners were unfailingly civil, and Elinor soon credited them with a good deal of practical sense, as she watched how carefully, how steadily, they curried favour with Lady Middleton. They never stopped raving over the children’s beauty, chasing after their notice, humoring every single one of their whims. Every spare moment they had away from the children was spent gushing over whatever trivial thing Lady Middleton was doing, or begging for a pattern of the elegant new gown she had worn the day before, which they swore had left them utterly enchanted.

As luck would have it for people who court favour through such small foibles, a fond mother, greedy as she is for praise of her children, is also the most credulous creature alive. Her demands for flattery are endless, but she will swallow every scrap of it offered to her. So Lady Middleton watched with perfect maternal complacency as the two Miss Steeles let the children untie their sashes, yank their hair down over their ears, rummage through their workbags, and steal their knives and scissors. She saw no harm in it, no discomfort, and felt only mild surprise that Elinor and Marianne sat so calmly nearby without fighting for a turn to be tormented by her darlings.
“John is in such spirits today!” she laughed when her eldest boy grabbed Anne Steele’s handkerchief and threw it straight out the open window. “He’s full of little monkey tricks.” A minute later, when her second son pinched Lucy’s fingers hard enough to make her wince, she cooed, “How playful William is!”
“And here is my sweet little Annamaria,” she added, pulling her three-year-old close to caress her. The little girl had not made a sound for two whole minutes. “She is always so gentle and quiet—there never was such a good little thing in the world.”

But as she leaned in to kiss the child, a pin stuck in her headdress scratched little Annamaria’s neck, and this paragon of quiet gentleness immediately set up a scream so loud and violent no noisy, unruly child could have topped it. The mother’s consternation was great, but it was nothing next to the alarm of the two Miss Steeles. All three of them rushed to soothe the crying girl: she was seated in her mother’s lap, smothered in kisses, her scratch bathed with lavender water by a kneeling Lucy, her mouth stuffed full of sugar plums by Anne. Rewarded so richly for her tears, the little girl had no reason to stop crying. She screamed and sobbed loudly, kicked her brothers when they tried to approach her, and none of their efforts worked until Lady Middleton suddenly remembered that the previous week, apricot marmalade had soothed a bruised temple perfectly. She immediately ordered the same remedy for the tiny scratch, and the little girl’s screams faded just a little at the mention of the sweet preserve. Lady Middleton carried her out of the room to fetch it, the two boys trotting after her despite her protests to stay, and all at once the four young ladies were left in a quiet that the room had not known for hours.
“Poor little love,” Anne Steele said the second they were gone. “It could have been such a terrible accident.”
“Hardly,” Marianne said bluntly. “Not for a tiny scratch like that. That’s the way people overreact when there is nothing at all to actually fear.”
“Lady Middleton is such a sweet woman, isn’t she?” Lucy Steele said quickly.

Marianne was silent. She could never bring herself to say something she did not truly feel, no matter how small the occasion, so all the work of polite small talk fell to Elinor. She did her best, speaking of Lady Middleton with more warmth than she actually felt, though far less than Lucy was showing.
“And Sir John is such a charming man, too!” Anne cried.
Elinor’s praise was simpler, far less effusive: she said he was perfectly good humoured and friendly.
“What a perfect little family they have! I have never seen such beautiful children in all my life. I am already completely devoted to them— I have always been utterly fond of children.”
“I could tell that much this morning,” Elinor said, with a small smile.
“I think you must find the Middletons far too indulgent with the children,” Lucy said, sounding almost shy. “Perhaps they are a little spoiled, but it is so natural for a mother like Lady Middleton. For my part, I love to see children full of life. I cannot bear them when they are quiet and tame.”
“For my part,” Elinor replied, “while I am at Barton Park, I never look at quiet, tame children with anything but relief.”

A short pause followed, before Anne Steele, who seemed desperate to keep the conversation going, spoke up abruptly:
“How do you like Devonshire, Miss Dashwood? I expect you were very sad to leave Sussex, weren’t you?”
Elinor was a little surprised by how familiar the question was, but she said that yes, they had been sad to leave.
“Norland Park is such a beautiful place, isn’t it?” Anne added.
“We have heard Sir John speak of it endlessly,” Lucy put in quickly, as if apologizing for her sister’s boldness.
“Anyone who has seen Norland would admire it,” Elinor said. “Though no one could love its beauty as much as we do.”
“Did you have lots of handsome young beaux there?” Anne went on. “I expect you don’t have nearly as many here in Devonshire. For my part, I always think a good beau makes everything far more fun.”
“Why would you imagine there are fewer respectable young men in Devonshire than in Sussex?” Lucy said, looking embarrassed at her sister.
“Oh, my dear, I never said there weren’t lots of fine beaux in Exeter,” Anne protested. “I just didn’t know what the men were like around Norland, and I was worried the Miss Dashwoods might get bored at Barton without the numbers they were used to. But I suppose young ladies like you don’t care about beaux all that much. For my part, I think they are lovely, as long as they dress neatly and behave politely. I can’t stand a messy, unkempt man. There is Mr. Rose in Exeter, for example— a very smart beau, clerk to Mr. Simpson the lawyer, but you can’t bear to run into him first thing in the morning, he looks so unkempt. I suppose your brother was quite a famous beau before he married, Miss Dashwood, since he is so wealthy?”
“I am afraid I do not even know what the word ‘beau’ really means,” Elinor said, laughing a little. “But I can tell you this: if he was one before he married, he is still one now, for nothing about him has changed at all.”
“Oh no, no one counts married men as beaux,” Anne said. “They have far more important things to do.”
“Lord, Anne!” Lucy cried. “You can talk about nothing but beaux— you will make Miss Dashwood think you care for nothing else.” To redirect the conversation, she started gushing over the Dashwood cottage’s furniture.

That short exchange was more than enough for Elinor. Anne’s coarse, silly forwardness made her completely unlikable, and Elinor was not fooled by Lucy’s pretty face and sharp eye: she saw clearly that the girl had no real elegance, no honest artlessness, and she left the Park that day with no desire to grow any closer to them.

The Miss Steeles felt very differently. They had come to Exeter full of carefully rationed admiration for Sir John, his family, and all his relations, and they lavished no small portion of it on the Dashwood sisters, whom they declared the most beautiful, accomplished, agreeable young women they had ever met, and whom they were determined to grow very close to. Elinor soon realized that that intimacy was inevitable. Sir John was firmly on the Steeles’ side, and their combined pressure could not be resisted. They were fated to spend an hour or two nearly every day in the same room, bound together by that casual, forced familiarity that passes for friendship in the country. Sir John thought that simply being in the same room counted as being close friends, and he never stopped scheming new ways to bring them all together, fully convinced he was forging a perfect bond. He did everything he could to make them open up to one another, too, spilling every tiny, delicate detail he knew about the Dashwoods’ lives to the Steeles. Before Elinor had even seen the Steeles twice, Anne Steele was already offering her congratulations on Marianne having caught such a handsome, smart beau since they moved to Barton.
“It will be so lovely for her to marry so young,” Anne chirped. “I hear he is very handsome, a total beau. And I hope you have the same luck very soon, Miss Dashwood— though I expect you already have a secret beau hidden away somewhere, don’t you?”

Elinor did not doubt for a second that Sir John had no more discretion about teasing her over her quiet regard for Edward Ferrars than he had about teasing Marianne over Willoughby. In fact, her secret attachment was his favourite joke, newer and more speculative than Marianne’s had ever been. Since Edward’s visit, every dinner they shared had ended with Sir John raising his glass to her “best affections”, winking and nodding so broadly that everyone at the table noticed. He had turned the letter F into a running joke, endlessly referencing it until Elinor had long decided it was the most overrated witticism in the entire alphabet.

The Miss Steeles, as she expected, got to hear every bit of that teasing. Anne’s curiosity about the name of the mysterious suitor was impertinent, entirely in keeping with her nosy nature, but Sir John did not drag the secret out for long. He got just as much glee out of spilling the name as Anne got out of hearing it.
“His name is Ferrars,” he whispered loudly, leaning across the table. “But don’t you dare tell anyone. It’s a great secret.”
“Ferrars!” Anne Steele repeated, her eyes lighting up. “Mr. Ferrars is the lucky man? The brother of your sister-in-law, Miss Dashwood? He is a perfectly agreeable young man, isn’t he? I know him very well.”
“Anne, how can you say that?” Lucy cut in, shaking her head at her sister. “We only met him once or twice at my uncle’s house in Exeter. That is hardly enough to say we know him very well.”

Elinor heard every word, her attention caught fast, her chest tight with shock. She could not stop wondering who this uncle was, where he lived, how they had ever come to meet Edward. She ached for the conversation to continue, even if she did not want to join in herself, but no more was said. For the first time in her life, Elinor found herself thinking Mrs. Jennings was far too incurious about trivial gossip, or far too unwilling to share what she knew. The sly, knowing edge in Anne Steele’s voice as she spoke Edward’s name left her with a sharp, lingering suspicion: the girl thought she knew something unflattering about him, some secret Elinor had never heard. But no matter how Elinor’s curiosity nagged at her, the subject of Mr. Ferrars was never brought up again by the Miss Steeles, not even when Sir John openly mentioned his name later that evening.

Marianne, whose generous spirit had never suffered impertinence, vulgarity, poor wit, or even a trivial difference of taste to pass without quiet irritation, was now so raw with the unspoken grief of Willoughby’s desertion that she could not bear the forward, giddy advances of the Miss Steeles at all. Her unwaveringly cold behaviour snuffed out every attempt the sisters made to grow intimate with the Dashwood household, and Elinor soon noticed that this chill had pushed the two women to favour her above all others—none more so than Lucy, who seized every spare moment to draw Elinor into conversation, chattering freely of her own thoughts and small adventures in an obvious bid to win her friendship.

Lucy was naturally quick-witted, with remarks that were often sharp and surprisingly amusing, and Elinor frequently found her pleasant enough company for half an hour’s stroll. But her native cleverness had never been nurtured by any formal education: she was ignorant, ill-read, and her total lack of general knowledge could not be hidden even by her constant, obvious efforts to appear polished and accomplished. Elinor pitied her, at first, for the wasted gifts a proper education might have turned into something respectable. But that soft pity faded when she saw the total absence of delicacy, honesty, and integrity in the girl, the calculated assiduity and hollow flattery she deployed to win favour at Barton Park. There could be no real pleasure in the company of someone who paired ignorance with total insincerity, who could never meet Elinor on equal ground in conversation, and whose obsequious deference to her felt valueless, given how slyly she treated everyone else around them.

“You will think this a very odd question, I dare say,” Lucy said to her one afternoon, as the two walked back from the grounds of Barton Park to the Dashwood cottage, “but pray tell me—are you personally acquainted with your sister-in-law’s mother, Mrs. Ferrars?”

Elinor did think it an exceedingly odd question, and her face betrayed her surprise even as she replied that she had never once met Mrs. Ferrars.

“Indeed!” Lucy said, blinking. “I wonder at that— I was certain you must have seen her at Norland sometimes. Then I suppose you cannot tell me what sort of a woman she is?”

“No,” Elinor answered carefully, not wanting to voice her quiet private opinion of Edward’s mother, and disinclined to satisfy what felt like impertinent curiosity. “I know nothing at all of her.”

“I am sure you think me very strange, prying about her like this,” Lucy said, her eyes fixed intently on Elinor’s face. “But there are reasons— I wish I might dare to tell you them! But at least you will do me the justice to believe I do not mean to be rude.”

Elinor gave her a civil reply, and they walked on in silence for several minutes, before Lucy spoke again, her voice wavering with apparent hesitation.
“I cannot bear for you to think me nosy and unkind. I would do anything in the world rather than earn a bad opinion from someone whose good opinion is so worth having as yours. I should not fear at all to trust you, and I would be so grateful for your advice on how to manage a situation as uncomfortable as mine— but there is no need to trouble you with it now. I am only sorry you do not know Mrs. Ferrars.”

“I am sorry I do not, too,” Elinor said, utterly bewildered. “If knowing my opinion of her could be of any use to you. But I never had the slightest idea you were connected to that family at all, I confess— so I cannot help being surprised by how seriously you are asking after her character.”

“I do not blame you for being surprised, not at all,” Lucy said, looking down with a soft, bashful little air, only flicking a quick glance at Elinor’s face to gauge her reaction. “But if I dared tell you everything, you would not be half so shocked. Mrs. Ferrars is nothing to me, now— but the time may come, and how soon it comes depends entirely on her, when we will be very closely connected indeed.”

“Good heavens!” Elinor exclaimed, her heart jumping. “What do you mean? Are you acquainted with Mr. Robert Ferrars? Is that possible?” The thought of such a frivolous, scheming girl as a sister-in-law struck her as deeply unpleasant.

“No,” Lucy replied, her eyes now locked steadily on Elinor’s. “Not Mr. Robert Ferrars. I have never even met him. I am acquainted with his eldest brother.”

For a beat, Elinor could not breathe. Shock washed over her so sharply it would have been agonizing, if she had not immediately rejected the claim as impossible. She turned to stare at Lucy in silence, her mind reeling, unable to fathom what lie the girl could be telling or why. Though her face flushed pale then hot, she stood firm in her disbelief, no hint of a swoon or fit clouding her resolve.

“You are quite right to be surprised,” Lucy continued. “You could never have suspected this before, I am sure— for he never dropped the smallest hint of it to you or any of your family. We meant this to be a great secret, and I have kept it perfectly faithfully until this very hour. Not a single soul in my family knows of it but my sister Anne, and I would never have told you if I did not trust your secrecy completely. I thought it only fair to explain, after I asked you so many odd questions about Mrs. Ferrars. And Mr. Ferrars will not be displeased when he learns I trusted you— he has the highest respect for all your family, and looks on you and your sisters as if you were his own.”

Elinor was silent for long moments, too stunned to speak. At last she forced herself to respond, her tone calm enough to hide most of her astonishment and quiet dread. “May I ask how long your engagement has stood?”

“We have been engaged these four years.”

“Four years!” Elinor echoed, reeling, but still refusing to believe it. “I did not even know you were acquainted until just the other day.”

“Our acquaintance stretches back many years, long before that,” Lucy said. “He was under my uncle’s care for a long while, you know.”

“Your uncle?”

“Mr. Pratt, at Longstaple, near Plymouth. Did he never speak to you of Mr. Pratt? He lived there four years, as a pupil. My sister Anne and I often stayed at my uncle’s house, that is how we met. We became engaged a year after he left his studies there, and he spent almost all his free time visiting us afterwards. I was most unwilling to agree to an engagement without his mother’s knowledge and approval, as you may well imagine, but I was too young, and loved him too well, to be as prudent as I ought to have been. You do not know him as well as I do, Miss Dashwood, but you have surely seen enough of him to know how easy it is for a woman to grow sincerely attached to him.”

“Certainly,” Elinor heard herself say, though she scarcely knew what she spoke. Then a surge of faith in Edward’s honour washed over her, and she was certain Lucy was lying. “Engaged to Mr. Edward Ferrars! I must beg your pardon, but there must be some mistake of name or person. We cannot possibly mean the same Mr. Ferrars.”

“We mean no other,” Lucy laughed softly. “Mr. Edward Ferrars, eldest son of Mrs. Ferrars of Park Street, brother to your sister-in-law John Dashwood’s wife. You cannot possibly think I would be mistaken in the name of the man all my happiness depends on.”

“It is most strange,” Elinor said, caught in a painful, confused tangle of doubt, “that he never once mentioned your name to me.”

“Considering how carefully we hid our connection, that is not strange at all,” Lucy replied. “You knew nothing of me or my family, so there was no reason to speak my name, and he was always so afraid his sister Fanny would suspect something— that was more than enough reason for him to keep quiet.”

Elinor’s fragile hope of Lucy’s falsehood crumbled, though her self-possession did not break. “Four years you have been engaged,” she said, her voice steady.

“Yes, and heaven knows how much longer we will have to wait,” Lucy sighed. “Poor Edward! It makes him so hopeless.” She pulled a tiny, painted miniature out of her pocket. “To make sure there is no mistake, please look at this face. It does not do him justice, I know, but I am sure you cannot fail to recognise who it is for. I have carried it now for more than three years.”

She passed the painting to Elinor, and no lingering doubt could survive the sight of it: it was undeniably Edward’s face. Elinor handed it back almost instantly, confirming the likeness.

“I have never been able to give him my own portrait in return, which vexes me terribly— he has begged me for it a hundred times,” Lucy prattled on. “But I shall sit for it the very first chance I get.”

“You are quite right,” Elinor said, her tone perfectly calm. They walked on a few paces in silence, before Lucy spoke again.
“I am sure you will keep this secret faithfully, for you must know how much depends on it. If his mother found out, she would never approve— I have no fortune at all, and I hear she is an exceedingly proud woman.”

“I did not ask you for your confidence,” Elinor said, “but you do me no more than justice to trust me. Your secret is safe with me. But you must pardon my saying I do not see why you chose to tell me this. You cannot possibly have thought my knowing of it would keep it any safer.” She looked hard at Lucy, hoping to spot a flicker of deception in her face, but the girl’s expression never shifted.

“I was afraid you would think I took a great liberty, telling you all this,” Lucy said. “I have not known you long in person, but I have heard descriptions of you and your family for years, and the moment I met you, I felt as if we were old acquaintances. Besides, after asking you so many odd questions about Mrs. Ferrars, I felt I owed you an explanation. I am so unfortunate— I have no one else to turn to for advice. Anne is the only other person who knows, and she has no judgment at all, she does far more harm than good. I am always terrified she will blurt the secret out. You saw the other day, when Sir John mentioned Edward’s name at dinner, I thought my heart would stop, sure she would spill everything. You cannot imagine what I have suffered these four years, all this suspense, seeing him hardly more than twice a year. I wonder my heart has not broken entirely.”

She pulled out a handkerchief to dab at her eyes, but Elinor felt no spark of compassion.
“Sometimes,” Lucy went on, wiping her cheeks, “I think it would be kinder for both of us to break off the engagement entirely.” She stared directly at Elinor as she spoke. “But I never have the resolution to do it. I cannot bear the thought of hurting him so badly. And for my own sake too— he is so dear to me, I do not think I could bear to let him go. What would you do in my place, Miss Dashwood? What advice would you give me?”

“Pardon me,” Elinor said, startled, “but I can give you no advice at all in these circumstances. You must trust your own judgment.”

“Of course,” Lucy said, after a long silence between them. “Someday his mother must provide for him, but poor Edward is so low-spirited. Did you not think him dreadfully down when he stayed at Barton? He was so miserable when he left us at Longstaple to come visit you, I was afraid you would think he was ill.”

“Did he come straight from your uncle’s house to visit us, then?”

“Oh yes, he stayed a full fortnight with us. Did you think he came directly from London?”

“No,” Elinor said, every new detail of Lucy’s account settling over her like cold lead. “I remember he told us he had spent a fortnight with some friends near Plymouth.” She remembered, too, how confused she had been at the time that he would never name those friends, never speak of them further.

“Did you not think him sadly out of spirits?” Lucy repeated.

“We did, especially when he first arrived.”

“I begged him to cheer up for fear you would suspect what was wrong, but he could not help being melancholy— knowing he could not stay more than a fortnight with us, seeing how upset I was. Poor fellow! I am sure he is just as down now; he writes in the lowest of spirits. I got a letter from him just before I left Exeter.” She pulled a letter out of her pocket, letting Elinor glance casually at the handwriting on the address. “You know his hand, I dare say? It is usually so neat, but this is scrawled, he was tired, I expect, he had just filled a whole sheet writing to me.”

Elinor saw that the handwriting was unmistakably Edward’s. All last remaining doubt vanished. The portrait, she had told herself, might have been an accident, not a gift from him. But secret letters, regular correspondence, could only exist under a formal, binding engagement. For a second her head swam, her heart sank so heavy she could barely stand, but she summoned every last scrap of resolution, fighting back the wave of agony that threatened to overwhelm her, and steadied herself almost at once.

“Writing to each other is the only comfort we have in all these long separations,” Lucy said, tucking the letter back into her pocket. “Well, I have his portrait, at least, but poor Edward has none of mine. He says if he had my likeness he would be perfectly content. Last time he visited Longstaple, I gave him a lock of my hair set in a little ring, he says that is some comfort, but not half as good as a portrait. Did you happen to notice that ring on his finger when he stayed with you?”

“I did,” Elinor said, her voice perfectly composed, though a storm of agony, humiliation, and shock raged beneath it, worse than anything she had ever felt in her life.

Thankfully, they reached the cottage just then, and the conversation could not go any further. The Miss Steeles sat with the Dashwoods for a few minutes more before returning to Barton Park, leaving Elinor at last free to sit alone, and grieve the quiet, tender hope of a future with Edward Ferrars that had just been utterly, irrevocably destroyed.

---

Elinor could not shake the weight of Lucy Steele’s confession, no matter how hard she tried to dismiss it as a lie. What possible reason could Lucy have to invent such a scandalous story? There was no temptation great enough to justify the folly of fabricating an engagement that would risk so much for nothing, and every small, forgotten detail of the last months lined up like proof, unassailable no matter how fiercely her own wishes argued against it.
Their acquaintance under Mr. Pratt’s roof in Plymouth was an undeniable, alarming foundation. Then Edward’s recent extended visit to that very same corner of Devonshire, his quiet, brooding melancholy, his frustration at his own stalled prospects, his fumbling, inconsistent behavior around her at Norland—even the Miss Steeles’ uncanny familiarity with every tiny trivial story of the Dashwood family at their old home, details Elinor had never shared with a casual acquaintance. The miniature portrait Edward had once all but hidden from her, the awkwardly worded letter he’d sent when he left Norland, the thin ring of platted hair he’d worn on his little finger for months that he’d never thought to explain: all of it stacked into a body of evidence she could no longer dismiss, no matter how partial her heart was to him.
For a few sharp, burning minutes, she resented him, raged at the fact she had been played for a fool. But the anger melted fast into softer, sadder thoughts. Could he have ever truly meant to deceive her? Could he have faked the tender, unguarded affection everyone around them had witnessed at Norland? Her mother, Marianne, even Fanny Dashwood, his own priggish cousin, had all teased her about how clearly Edward doted on her. He loved her. She could not convince herself otherwise.
That realization was the softest, cruelest balm of all. It made her want to forgive him almost everything. He had been wrong, deeply wrong, to linger at Norland after he realized his feelings for her outgrew the bounds of his old promise—but if he had hurt her, he had hurt himself far worse. She might grieve her lost hopes and find peace with time. He had no such escape. Trapped between a family that would disown him for an unsuitable marriage, and a wife who was ill-educated, manipulative, and entirely selfish, what future could he ever hope for? The infatuation of a nineteen-year-old boy might have once blinded him to Lucy’s flaws, but four years of growing, maturing while Lucy spent her days in frivolous, shallow company had long since erased whatever simple charm she might once have had. That the prospect of his mother’s fury and family rejection might feel like a small relief next to the life he was bound to… Elinor found herself weeping for him far more than she wept for herself.
Secure in the knowledge she had done nothing to earn this unhappiness, and determined not to lose her respect for Edward no matter the truth, she steeled herself to hide the whole painful secret from her mother and Marianne. She rose, washed the traces of tears from her face, and joined them for dinner barely two hours after her every dearest hope had crumbled. No one at the table suspected a thing: that Elinor was quietly mourning a love she would never be allowed to claim, while Marianne’s head was full of nothing but Willoughby, certain his whole heart belonged to her, and craning to spot his carriage every time one rumbled past the cottage gate.
Hiding the secret was no burden to Elinor, either. It spared her the distress of seeing her mother and Marianne break their hearts over her pain, and the inevitable, furious condemnation of Edward that their partial affection for her would spark—condemnation she knew she was far too raw to bear. She was stronger alone, her own good sense propping her up so well that her cheerfulness never wavered even when her regret cut sharpest.
Before long, she found herself eager to speak to Lucy again, in private. She wanted to hear every small detail of the engagement laid out clearly, to judge for herself if Lucy truly felt any real affection for Edward, and most of all, to convince Lucy with her calm composure that she was no threat to her claim—that she had no intention of standing between them, so Lucy had no reason to fear her. It was obvious Lucy was jealous, after all. Edward must have spoken of her constantly, for Lucy to dare entrust a near-stranger with a secret so great, purely to stake her claim. Elinor understood exactly what her rival wanted: for her to learn of the engagement, and step back from Edward of her own accord. Elinor was happy to give her that reassurance, even if it meant facing the truth head on.
Weeks dragged on without a single spare minute of privacy. The weather was too stormy for long walks away from the group, and every gathering at the Middleton’s or the cottage was full of noise: Sir John’s raucous jokes, shrieking children, loud card games and silly party games that left no room for quiet, private talk. It was only when Sir John popped over to the cottage one morning, begging them all to dine at the Park that very day, that Elinor saw her chance. He had to attend a club meeting in Exeter, he explained, and Lady Middleton would be left dreadfully lonely with nothing but her mother and the two Miss Steeles for company. Elinor accepted instantly, knowing Lady Middleton’s quiet, decorous parties would never descend into the chaotic, all-noise bustle her husband insisted on. Her mother and Margaret agreed at once, and even Marianne, though she hated calling on the Middletons, was gently persuaded to come along, so she would not shut herself away from every small chance of amusement.
The dinner was exactly as dull as Elinor had predicted. Not a single original thought or remark passed between them, and when they moved to the drawing room after the meal, Lady Middleton’s brood of children trotted after them, clinging to their mother’s skirts and chattering nonstop, so Elinor could not risk a single word to Lucy. When the tea things were cleared away, the card tables were set out, and Elinor feared she had missed her chance entirely.
“I am so glad,” Lady Middleton said to Lucy, “that you are not finishing little Anna Maria’s filigree basket tonight. Working that fine craft by candlelight will strain your eyes so dreadfully. We will finish it tomorrow, and the dear little love will hardly mind the delay.”
Lucy caught the hint at once, and smiled brightly. “You are quite mistaken, Lady Middleton! I only waited to check if you needed me at the card table first. I would never disappoint that sweet child for anything in the world. If you can spare me, I will go right back to my work straight after supper.”
Elinor seized the opening the second Lucy pulled her work table close. “If I end up sitting out the first round of cards,” she said smoothly, “I would be delighted to help you roll the delicate papers for that basket. There is still so much left to do, two of us could finish it in no time at all. I should love to lend a hand.”
Lucy welcomed her instantly, and Lady Middleton was only too happy to agree, pleased to know her spoiled little Anna Maria would get her basket on time. Marianne, meanwhile, had already turned on her heel without a by-your-leave, announcing she detested cards and was off to play the piano, which had just been tuned the week prior. She was already lost in her favorite dramatic pieces seconds later, the rich, loud notes drifting across the room, drowning out every soft word two people sitting side by side at the work table might choose to exchange.
Perfectly hidden from the card players, the two women worked side by side at the same basket, seemingly the best of friends, with all the privacy Elinor could have hoped for to finally broach the secret that had hung heavy over her for days.

Tucked in the quietest corner of Barton Park’s sunlit drawing room, where Marianne’s fierce, thundering concerto at the far end of the piano would swallow every soft word they spoke, Elinor drew a steadying breath before she began, her tone firm but kind, no trace of the roiling ache she’d carried in her chest since Lucy had spilled her secret engagement a week prior.
“I would be failing to honour the trust you placed in me,” she said gently, “if I pretended I had no wish to hear more, no care for how this all will turn out for you. I won’t waste breath apologizing for bringing it up again.”
Lucy’s whole face lit up with relief. “Oh, thank you! You’ve taken such a weight off my shoulders. I’ve been half-convinced for days I offended you with what I told you that Monday, that you’d decided to go cold on me.”
“Offended me? How could you ever think that?” Elinor’s reply was utterly, unfeignedly sincere. “I would never want you to feel that. The fact you trusted me enough to share such a vulnerable secret is the highest compliment I could be paid.”
Lucy’s small, sharp eyes narrowed, bright with meaning. “And yet I swear, your manner felt so distant, so displeased that day, I was miserable for hours afterwards. I scolded myself for being so bold as to dump all my troubles on you. But I’m so glad it was just my silly imagination running wild. If you knew how often I turn this engagement over in my head, every single hour of every day, you’d forgive any little impropriety from me, I’m sure—talking to you about it is the only relief I’ve had in four long years.”
“I don’t doubt that for a second,” Elinor said softly. “You have nothing to fear from me sharing this. But your situation is so terribly stuck, isn’t it? You’ll need every scrap of affection the two of you share to get through all these hardships ahead. Mr. Ferrars has almost no independent fortune, I take it? He’s entirely dependent on his mother’s goodwill?”
“He’s only got two thousand pounds of his own,” Lucy said, shaking her head. “It would be madness to marry on that, I know. For my part, I could live on pennies and never complain, I’ve always been used to very little. But I love him too much to be selfish, to rob him of every penny his mother would leave him if he marries against her wishes. We have to wait. It could be years, and years. Any other man would get tired of that, but I know nothing in the world could shake Edward’s loyalty to me.”
That conviction must carry you through so much, Elinor thought, and said it out loud, even as her chest ached. “And he must be just as sure of your loyalty, too. If that kind of mutual love had faded over four years of being apart, like so many engagements do, you would be in a truly pitiable spot right now.”
Lucy looked up quickly, but Elinor kept her face perfectly neutral, no flicker of the grief that sat heavy behind her eyes.
“Edward’s love for me has already been tested over and over, all those years we were apart after we got engaged,” Lucy said proudly. “It stood the test perfectly. I’d be a fool to doubt it now. He’s never once given me a single reason to worry.”
Elinor couldn’t decide if she wanted to smile bitterly or sigh. Lucy, meanwhile, kept right on talking.
“I’m naturally a jealous sort, you know. We live such different lives—he’s out in society all the time, I’m stuck in the country, we’re apart for months on end. I’d notice instantly if anything changed in him: if he was low for no reason, if he talked about some other lady too much, if he seemed less happy at Longstaple than he used to be. I’m not usually sharp about that kind of thing, but where Edward’s concerned? I could never be tricked.”
All very well for you to say, Elinor thought, but neither of us is fooled, are we?
After a short silence, she asked, “What’s your plan, then? Are you just… waiting for Mrs. Ferrars to die? That’s such a bleak, awful thing to stake your whole future on. Won’t Edward ever tell her the truth, take a little temporary anger from her now instead of condemning both of you to years and years of limbo?”
“Only if that anger would be temporary!” Lucy’s face fell. “Mrs. Ferrars is so proud, so stubborn. If she found out about us, she’d turn around and leave every single penny she owns to Robert, Edward’s younger brother. The thought of that, for Edward’s sake, makes me too scared to rush anything.”
“For your own sake too, surely? That’s not selfishness, that’s common sense.”
Lucy stared at Elinor for a long moment, silent.
“Have you ever met Mr. Robert Ferrars?” Elinor asked, to break the pause.
“Not at all, I’ve never even seen him. But I hear he’s nothing like his brother—silly, and a terrible, preening coxcomb.”
“A great coxcomb!” Miss Steele’s voice cut across the room, sharp, as she’d caught the word when Marianne paused for half a bar in her music. “Oh, I know what you two are gossiping about—your favourite beaux, of course!”
“No, sister, you’re quite wrong,” Lucy huffed. “Our favourite beaux are not coxcombs at all.”
“I can vouch for Miss Dashwood’s, that’s for certain,” Mrs. Jennings cackled from her armchair, having half-woken up from her doze. “That young man of hers is one of the most modest, sweet-mannered lads I’ve ever met. And Lucy’s just a sly little thing, she won’t ever tell anyone who she’s sweet on.”
“Oh, I’m sure Lucy’s beau is just as modest and well-behaved as Miss Dashwood’s,” Miss Steele said, winking pointedly around the room.
Elinor blushed hot, despite every effort not to. Lucy bit down on her lip and glowered daggers at her older sister.
Nobody said a word for a long stretch, until Lucy leaned in closer, her voice barely a murmur, even with Marianne’s thunderous concerto roaring over them.
“I have a plan, actually. I’m going to tell you the secret, because you’re part of it. You’ve spent enough time with Edward to know he’d far rather be a clergyman than do any other work, right? My idea is that he takes orders as soon as he possibly can, and then you could use your influence—out of your friendship for him, and I hope out of some kindness to me too—to persuade your brother John to give him the Norland living. I hear it pays very well, and the current priest isn’t long for this world. That would be more than enough for us to marry on, and we can sort out the rest with time and luck.”
“I would always be happy to do anything I could to show Mr. Ferrars how much I respect him,” Elinor said, trying to keep her tone even. “But don’t you see? My favour wouldn’t be needed at all here. Edward’s Mrs. John Dashwood’s own brother. That’s more than enough recommendation for her husband.”
“But Mrs. John Dashwood would never want Edward to go into the church,” Lucy said.
“Then I’m afraid my influence wouldn’t do a single thing to change her mind.”
They fell silent again, for several minutes, before Lucy heaved a great, heavy sigh.
“I think the wisest thing we could do is just call the whole engagement off, right? We’re blocked on every side. It would hurt like mad for a little while, but we’d both be happier in the long run. Won’t you give me your advice, Miss Dashwood?”
“No,” Elinor said, smiling to hide how wildly her heart was hammering. “I can’t give you advice on that. You know perfectly well my opinion wouldn’t mean anything to you unless it lined up with what you already wanted to do.”
“You do me wrong,” Lucy said, very solemn. “I respect your judgment more than anyone else’s in the whole world. If you looked at me and said, ‘Break off your engagement to Edward Ferrars right now, it’ll make both of you far happier,’ I’d do it, no question.”
Elinor blushed, thinking of how false that promise was, coming from the woman who was meant to marry the man she loved. “That compliment would make me far too nervous to give any opinion at all. You’re making me out to have far too much power. Splitting up two people who love each other? That’s far too big a responsibility for someone who’s no part of this relationship.”
“Exactly! Because you’re not involved, you’re impartial,” Lucy said, a little stung, leaning heavy on that last word. “That’s why your opinion would carry so much weight. If you were biased by your own feelings, no one would care what you thought.”
Elinor decided it was better not to reply at all, not to risk making them both say something unforgivable, and half made up her mind never to bring the subject up ever again. Another long pause followed, until Lucy broke it, back to her usual sweet, obliging tone.
