“Will you be in London this winter, Miss Dashwood?”
“Certainly not,” Elinor said.
“Oh, what a shame!” Lucy’s eyes brightened, like she’d just got very good news. “I’d have loved so much to run into you there! But I’m sure you’ll end up going anyway, your brother and sister will invite you to stay with them.”
“Even if they do, I won’t be able to accept.”
“How terrible for us!” Lucy sighed. “Anne and I are heading up to London at the end of January, to stay with some relations who’ve been begging us to visit for years. I’d never bother going if I didn’t have a reason—Edward will be there in February. London wouldn’t have a single charm for me otherwise, I’m far too dull for parties there.”
Before Elinor could reply, the first rubber of cards finished, and she was called over to take a seat at the table. The private, tense conversation between the two women was over, and neither of them minded that much—nothing they’d said had made either of them like the other a single bit more than before. As Elinor picked up her hand of cards, her mind was racing with a miserable, heavy certainty: Edward wasn’t just stuck to an engagement with a woman who didn’t truly love him, he had no chance at even a tolerably happy marriage. Lucy was only holding him to the promise for her own security, and she knew perfectly well he was already weary of it.
From that day on, Elinor never brought the subject up again. Lucy, though, never missed a chance to slip it into conversation whenever she could, gushing about how happy she was every time a letter from Edward arrived. Elinor always treated those mentions with perfect calm and caution, and steered the conversation away as soon as politeness would let her. Those little talks were a treat Lucy didn’t deserve, and far too dangerous for Elinor’s own fragile self-control.
The Miss Steeles’ visit to Barton Park dragged on far longer than the original invitation ever intended. Everyone at the park doted on them, Sir John threw a fit at the very mention of them leaving, and even though they kept swearing every single week that they had urgent, pre-planned engagements back in Exeter that they absolutely had to return for immediately, they ended up staying almost two full months. They stayed right through all the feasting, private balls, and grand dinner parties that mark the Christmas season, that most festive time of year that demands endless celebrations to show how important it is.

Though Mrs. Jennings spent nearly three-quarters of every year roving between the homes of her married children and dearest friends, chattering her way through dinners and card parties from Devonshire to Derbyshire, she still kept a settled, much-loved home of her own. After her husband, a shrewd, good-natured tradesman, passed away, she’d settled there for every winter: a warm, chintz-draped house tucked into a quiet street just off Portman Square, scented year-round with dried lavender and spiced orange peel, stuffed full of the wedding favours and baby gifts she’d hoarded from all her grandchildren’s celebrations.

As the first frosts of January crisped the hedgerows around Barton Park, her thoughts turned stubbornly back to that London house, and one grey afternoon over tea, she blurted out an invitation to the two eldest Dashwood sisters so abruptly it left them both blinking over their teacups. Elinor, not yet noticing the way Marianne’s cheeks flushed bright pink, or the bright, restless spark in her eyes that had been dim ever since Willoughby left Devonshire, gave a gentle but firm refusal on both their behalves, certain she spoke for their shared wish not to leave their mother all alone in the depth of winter.

Mrs. Jennings blinked at the reply, then leaned forward, patting Elinor’s arm earnestly. “Oh, Lord, my dear, your mother will scarce miss you! I’ve set my whole heart on having you both. You won’t be the slightest inconvenience, I swear it – I won’t put myself out of sorts one bit. We three can ride up in my chaise, no fuss, and once we’re in town, if you tire of my silly old rounds, you can go off with either of my girls whenever you please. I’ve raised three children of my own, after all – your mother will know I’m more than fit to look after you two, and mark my words, I’ll make sure every handsome young bachelor in London hears what sweet, clever girls you are. If I don’t get at least one of you well married before the season’s out, it won’t be for lack of me speaking up!”

Sir John, who’d been slouched in his chair nodding along, boomed out his agreement. “I say! Miss Marianne wouldn’t turn it down if Miss Dashwood here wasn’t being so cautious. Hard on the girl, isn’t it, to miss out on all the fun just because her sister’s not in the mood? I’d advise you two to sneak off with Mrs. Jennings when you next get bored of Barton, and not say a word to Elinor about it!”

“Nonsense, I’ll be over the moon to have Marianne whether Elinor comes or not!” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “More the merrier, that’s what I say. If you two get sick of my old rambling, you can huddle in the corner and whisper and laugh at my odd ways all you like, I won’t mind a bit. But I must have at least one of you with me – I’m used to having Charlotte at my side every minute, and I can’t bear the thought of poking around that big London house all by myself. Come on, Marianne, shake on it! And if Elinor changes her mind later, all the better!”

Marianne’s whole face lit up as she leaned forward, voice warm and earnest. “Thank you, ma’am, from the very bottom of my heart. Your invitation is the kindest thing anyone has offered me in months, and I can scarce think of anything that would make me happier than to come – but my mother, my dearest, dearest mother… Elinor is right, if our absence made her the tiniest bit less happy or comfortable, I could never forgive myself. Nothing would ever tempt me to leave her if that was the cost.”

Mrs. Jennings promised her over and over that Mrs. Dashwood would be perfectly well off with Margaret for company, and by then Elinor had seen the truth clear as day: Marianne, who so often bristled at Mrs. Jennings’ loud, unfiltered manners, who would snap if the older woman teased her too freely about young men, cared nothing for any of that. Her longing to see Willoughby again was so strong it washed out every other annoyance, every usual irritation, every scruple she’d ever held. It was proof of how deep her love ran, so fierce Elinor could scarce bring herself to oppose it any longer. She referred the final decision to their mother, even though she knew Mrs. Dashwood would never say no to anything that made Marianne’s eyes shine that bright – and Elinor dared not voice her own reason for dreading London, the tangled secret of Edward Ferrars and Lucy Steele that she’d been carrying alone for months.

When they brought the invitation home to their cottage, Mrs. Dashwood did not hesitate a second. She could see Marianne’s whole heart was set on the trip, and she was certain a few weeks in London would do both girls a world of good. “I am delighted!” she cried, beaming. “This is exactly what I’ve been hoping for! Margaret and I will have the coziest, quietest time here without you two – we’ll pore over new books, practice our duets, you’ll come home and find Margaret can play the whole of her piano sonata without a single mistake! I’ve got a little plan to redecorate your bedrooms too, which we can do without disturbing you at all. It’s right that you two see London, meet other young people, learn the ways of the world. And you’ll get to see your brother John, too – no matter what silly disagreements he and I have had, I don’t want my girls estranged from their own family.”

Elinor smiled faintly. “You’ve thought of every joy, mama, but there is one objection I don’t think you’ve accounted for. Mrs. Jennings is very kind, but her company can scarcely be called a pleasure, and being under her protection won’t lend us any of the respectability you might wish for us.”

Marianne’s face fell for a second, before she leaned forward, eager to argue. “If that’s all that’s stopping you, Elinor, it need not stop me. I don’t care a fig for any of that. I can put up with every small inconvenience, every silly comment, every tedious hour, without the smallest effort.”

Elinor bit back a smile, thinking of all the times she’d had to remind Marianne to be polite to Mrs. Jennings when she’d snapped over some harmless tease. She resolved in that moment that if Marianne was set on going, she would go too – she could not let her impulsive, passion-led sister loose in London alone, with no one to steady her when her heart ran away with her. She felt a small flicker of relief, too: Lucy had told her Edward Ferrars would not be arriving in London until February. If they kept their visit short, they could be back at Barton long before he ever set foot in the city.

“I will hear no more of these silly objections,” Mrs. Dashwood said firmly. “You will both go, and you will both have a wonderful time. And Elinor, if you would only let yourself look forward to it, you might find far more joy there than you expect – perhaps even a pleasant surprise from Mr. Ferrars, or his family.”

Elinor’s chest tightened. She had long hoped to gently soften her mother’s certainty that Edward would one day marry her, to make the coming hurt less sharp when the truth of his secret engagement came out. She spoke as steadily as she could. “I like Edward very well, and I will always be glad to see him. But as for the rest of his family, I do not care at all if I ever make their acquaintance.”

Mrs. Dashwood smiled, and said no more. Marianne stared at Elinor in astonishment, and Elinor realized she might as well have kept quiet.

It was settled soon after that they would accept the invitation fully. Mrs. Jennings was over the moon, chattering all the way home about the new gowns she’d help the girls pick out in town. Sir John was equally delighted – for a man who hated nothing more than being bored and alone, adding two more people to the London party was a triumph. Even Lady Middleton made the effort to look pleased, giddy at the thought of showing off her new silk embroidery to new company. And the Miss Steeles, especially Lucy, could not have been happier: now Elinor would be in London, too, close at hand where Lucy could keep an eye on her.

Elinor found herself far less reluctant to go than she’d expected. When she saw her mother humming happily about the house making plans, and Marianne bouncing around the cottage, her old animation fully restored, singing the old ballads Willoughby used to recite and tucking the little locket with a lock of his hair into her travelling bag, she could not bring herself to feel unhappy about the trip. Marianne’s joy was so bright it felt almost like magic, and she could scarce bring herself to doubt it would turn out well.

The only time Marianne’s giddiness faded was the day of their departure, in the first week of January. She clung to her mother, sobbing into her shoulder, as if she feared she would never see her again. Mrs. Dashwood was scarcely less tearful. Only Elinor, standing quietly beside them, felt a little twist of foreboding, wondering what secrets and joys London held in store for them all.

The Middletons would follow them to town a week later. The Miss Steeles would stay on at Barton Park until the whole party left, and no one guessed that the quiet streets of Portman Square would hold more heartbreak, and more romance, than any of them could imagine.

Elinor could scarcely believe she was settled in a carriage beside the boisterous, warm-hearted Mrs. Jennings, bound for London as her houseguest, when only days prior she’d reasoned their acquaintance was far too new, their ages and temperaments far too mismatched, to ever agree to such a trip. But all her careful, sensible objections had melted away before that bright, impetuous ardour Marianne and their mother shared for every new possibility, and Elinor could not even resent the choice, not when she watched her sister’s whole soul light up with giddy, consuming expectation. Beside Marianne’s burning, uncomplicated hope of meeting Willoughby in the city, her own quiet, uncertain longing for Edward felt faint and shadowed; she found herself wishing, for a foolish moment, that she could trade places, that she had that same vivid, animating possibility to look forward to.
Very soon now, she told herself, they would learn exactly what Willoughby’s intentions were. It was almost certain he was already in London, and Marianne’s fidgeting eagerness to leave Barton left no doubt she expected to find him there. Elinor resolved to watch carefully: to learn all she could of his character, to study every detail of his behaviour towards her sister, and work out the truth of what he wanted long before they had met many times. If he proved unworthy, she would make Marianne see sense, no matter how hard it was. If he was true and honourable, she would banish all selfish, envious comparisons, all quiet regret that her own happiness could not be so bright, and throw all her energy into celebrating her sister’s joy.
The three-day journey gave everyone a preview of how things would go in London. Marianne sat almost entirely silent, wrapped up in her own daydreams, only speaking to cry out to Elinor when a stretch of rolling, sun-dappled countryside caught her eye, far too preoccupied to make small talk with Mrs. Jennings. Elinor stepped easily into the breach: she laughed at the older woman’s endless anecdotes, asked after every one of her acquaintances, teased her gently, and listened to every rambling story with apparent delight. Mrs. Jennings, for her part, fussed over them endlessly, determined to make their trip as comfortable as possible, and only grew flustered at the inns when she could not bully them into admitting they preferred salmon over cod, or boiled fowl over veal cutlets.
They pulled up to Mrs. Jennings’ London house by three o’clock on the third day, stiff from carriage travel and giddy to sink into the warmth of a crackling drawing room fire. The house was elegant, richly furnished, and the young ladies were shown immediately to a cosy, sunlit guest apartment that had once belonged to Mrs. Jennings’ daughter Charlotte. Over the mantelpiece hung a lopsided landscape stitched entirely in coloured silk, a souvenir of the seven years Charlotte had spent at a prestigious London boarding school.
Dinner would not be served for two whole hours, so Elinor sat down immediately to pen a letter to their mother. Marianne sank into a chair beside her, reaching for paper and ink too.
“I’m writing home to Mama,” Elinor said gently. “Wouldn’t you rather wait a day or two to send your letter?”
“I’m not writing to Mama,” Marianne replied quickly, her cheeks flushing pink, as if she wanted no further questions.
Elinor asked no more. It struck her at once that the letter was for Willoughby, and the obvious, happy conclusion followed: no matter how secretive the pair were trying to be, they must be formally engaged. That quiet certainty lifted her spirits, and she went back to her own letter with a lighter heart.
Marianne’s note was finished in mere minutes. It was barely longer than a scrap of paper, folded, sealed, and addressed with shaking, eager haste. Elinor caught a glimpse of a large, looping W scrawled across the front before Marianne rang for the footman, begging him to run the note straight to the two-penny post. There was no doubt left now.
Marianne’s mood stayed high for the rest of the afternoon, but there was a sharp, fluttery edge to her excitement that stopped it from feeling like pure joy. She could barely force herself to eat a bite of dinner, and once they returned to the drawing room, she jumped at every distant rumble of a carriage, every faint footstep on the street outside. Elinor was grateful Mrs. Jennings had retreated to her own rooms to unpack, and could not see how wildly on edge her sister was.
By the time the tea tray was brought in, Marianne had already been disappointed half a dozen times by knocks on the neighbours’ front doors. Then a loud, sharp rap sounded on their own door. Elinor was sure it was Willoughby. Marianne leaped to her feet, flying towards the entryway. The next few seconds of silence were unbearable to her: she wrenched the door open, ran a few steps towards the stairs, and listened for a long, breathless moment, before turning back to the drawing room, her face glowing with rapture.
“Oh Elinor, it’s Willoughby! It really is!” she cried, half ready to throw herself into his arms—only to find Colonel Brandon standing in the doorway instead.
The shock was too great to bear calmly. Marianne did not say a single word, only turned on her heel and fled the room.
Elinor was disappointed too, but far more upset on the Colonel’s behalf. A man so openly, tenderly fond of her sister had just seen Marianne’s utter, unmistakeable grief at the sight of him, and he was staring after her with such naked astonishment and concern that he almost forgot to greet Elinor politely.
“Is your sister unwell?” he asked, his voice tight with worry.
Elinor stumbled through an explanation, blaming travel fatigue, a headache, low spirits after their long journey, anything that could excuse Marianne’s rude, heartbroken flight. He listened with grave, earnest attention, then pulled himself back to politeness, speaking of how delighted he was that they had come to London, asking gentle questions about their trip and all the people they had left behind at Barton.
The conversation dragged on quietly, both of them preoccupied with far weightier thoughts. Elinor ached to ask if Willoughby was in town, but she could not bear to hurt the Colonel by prying after his rival. At last, for lack of anything better to say, she asked if he had remained in London ever since their last meeting at Barton.
“Yes,” he said, shifting awkwardly in his chair. “Almost ever since. I have gone down to Delaford for a few days at a time, but I never found the chance to return to Barton.”
The odd, tense manner of his answer brought all the memories of his sudden, mysterious departure from Barton flooding back, along with all the rumours Mrs. Jennings had chattered about, and Elinor feared she had pried far deeper into his private business than she had any right to.
A moment later Mrs. Jennings bounced into the room, as loud and cheerful as ever. “Oh Colonel! I am monstrous glad to see you! So sorry I couldn’t greet you earlier, but I’ve been running myself ragged settling all my household accounts after being away so long—why, I’ve been busier than a bee since dinner! But how on earth did you guess I’d be back in London today?”
“I heard the news at the Palmers’ house, where I dined this afternoon,” he said.
“Oh, of course! And how are they all? How is Charlotte? I’ll warrant she’s getting rounder by the day with that baby of hers.”
“Mrs. Palmer is perfectly well, and she asked me to tell you that you will see her tomorrow without fail,” he replied.
They chattered on for a long while, Mrs. Jennings teasing the Colonel about his reserved ways, joking that it was a fine thing to be young and handsome, and that no one knew what would come of Marianne and Willoughby both being in the same city now. The Colonel grew quieter and more thoughtful once Marianne crept back into the room, and he did not stay long after that. No other visitors called that evening, and all three ladies agreed to head up to bed far earlier than usual.
Marianne woke the next morning with her spirits fully restored, the disappointment of the previous night seemingly wiped away by her bright, unshakable certainty that Willoughby would call that day. They had barely finished breakfast when Mrs. Palmer’s barouche pulled up to the door, and she ran laughing into the room, overjoyed to see all of them at once. She pretended to be furious that they had accepted her mother’s invitation instead of her own, even though she would have been deeply hurt if they had turned the chance down.
“Mr. Palmer was so delighted when he heard you were coming with Mama!” she babbled. “I can’t even remember what he said exactly, but it was the drollest thing you ever heard!”
After an hour or two of what Mrs. Jennings called “comfortable chat”—meaning endless questions after every acquaintance they shared, and gales of unprovoked laughter from Mrs. Palmer—she suggested they all accompany her to Bond Street to run errands. Elinor and Mrs. Jennings agreed at once, for they both had purchases to make, and even Marianne, who initially refused, was persuaded to come along.
Every step they took, Marianne’s eyes darted across the crowds, scanning every face for a glimpse of Willoughby. In every shop they stepped into, she was completely detached from the ribbons, fabrics, and bonnets the rest of the party admired, restless and impatient, refusing to give any opinion on any item Elinor asked her about, even those they both needed. She grew openly vexed when Mrs. Palmer dawdled endlessly, enchanted by every pretty, expensive new item she saw, too indecisive to buy any of them.
They did not get back to the house until late in the afternoon. No sooner had they stepped over the threshold than Marianne flew up the stairs, and when Elinor followed, she found her sister turning away from the entry table with a face crumpled in sorrow: no Willoughby had called while they were out.
“Has no letter been left for me since we went out?” Marianne asked the footman, as he came in carrying their parcels.
He assured her no post, no visitor, no porter had left a single note for any of them.
“How very strange,” Marianne whispered, turning to stare out the window, her voice thick with disappointment.
How strange indeed, Elinor thought, a cold unease settling in her chest. If Marianne had not known Willoughby was in London, she would have sent her letter to his estate at Combe Magna, not to a London address. If he *is* in the city, why has he not called? Why has he not written? Her heart ached, and she resolved that if these strange, upsetting circumstances dragged on for a few more days, she would write to her mother at once, and make her see how reckless they had been to let such a young girl conduct a secret, unconfirmed engagement with a man they barely knew, with no transparency, no proof that his intentions were honourable.
Mrs. Palmer and two of Mrs. Jennings’ oldest, closest friends joined them for dinner. Shortly after tea, Mrs. Palmer left to attend another evening engagement, and Elinor was roped into making up a fourth at their whist table. Marianne had never bothered to learn the game, so she was left to her own devices—but the evening brought her no joy. She would pick up a book for a minute or two, only to fling it aside and start pacing the drawing room back and forth, pausing every time she passed the window, holding her breath, listening for the knock that never came.

“The way this unseasonably soft weather holds on,” Mrs. Jennings chattered over their breakfast table the first full morning the Dashwood sisters spent in her Berkeley Street home, “Sir John will kick up a terrible fuss next week when it’s time to leave Barton. There’s no sorrow like a sportsman’s, wasting a fine clear day indoors! Poor dears, I always pity them, they take it so near to heart.”

Marianne jumped to her feet, voice bright with sudden, unlooked-for joy, and darted to the window to press her palm to the fog-frosted pane. “I never even thought of that! This weather will keep so many of them lingering in the country just a little longer.”

That stray, lucky thought restored every spark of her good humor. She skipped back to the table, cheeks pink with hope. “It is perfect weather for their sport, of course. How they must enjoy it—though,” she added, her tone softening into a quiet, eager edge of anxiety, “it can’t possibly last. This late in the year, after all that rain we had back home? We’re bound to get a sharp frost any day now. Maybe even tonight, this absurd mildness can’t hold on much longer.”

Elinor cut in quickly, hoping to steer Mrs. Jennings’s sharp gaze away from reading Marianne’s secret, unspoken hope that the frost would drive Willoughby straight to London, and straight to her door. “I’ve no doubt Sir John and Lady Middleton will be in town by the end of next week, at any rate.”

“Aye, dear, you may be sure of it,” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “My daughter Mary always gets exactly what she wants.”

Elinor’s own silent guess was that Marianne would dash off a letter to Combe Magne that very afternoon, posted before anyone could catch a glimpse of the address. If she did, the note was written and slipped to the post boy so discreetly that even Elinor’s watchful eye couldn’t confirm it. Still, even with that small uncertainty hanging over her, Elinor could not be miserable when Marianne glowed, half giddy over the soft sun, half trembling with anticipation of the coming frost that would bring the man she loved straight to her.

Most of that first week was spent trundling through London’s streets dropping calling cards at Mrs. Jennings’s friends’ homes to announce her arrival. Marianne never once stopped checking the direction of the wind, craning to read the shifts in the clouds, pressing a cold hand to her cheek to convince herself the air had turned sharper.
“Elinor, don’t you feel how much colder it is than it was this morning?” she would say, hovering over her sister’s shoulder. “My hands are stiff even inside my muff. It wasn’t this sharp yesterday, was it? The clouds are pulling apart too—there’ll be bright, clear frost by afternoon, I just know it.”

Elinor alternated between smiling at her sister’s stubborn, tender optimism and aching for her, in case none of that hope amounted to anything. But Marianne was unshakable: every night’s bright fire glow, every morning’s crisp gray sky, was a sure sign the hard frost was coming, and Willoughby with it.

The sisters could not have asked for a kinder hostess. Mrs. Jennings never made them call on any of her stuffy old city acquaintances that would leave them flustered or ill at ease, her household ran on generous, warm terms, and Elinor soon found herself far more comfortable than she had expected. Their evening parties, whether held at home or out at a friend’s house, were mostly gatherings built around card games, with little to amuse anyone who didn’t care for the tables—but Elinor found far more joy in her daily chats with Colonel Brandon, who had a standing open invitation to the house, and stopped by nearly every day. He came ostensibly to check on Marianne, but he always lingered to talk to Elinor, and she could not miss how his quiet, gentle affection for her sister seemed to grow deeper and more tender by the day. It grieved her to watch him stare at Marianne with that soft, hungry earnestness, and to notice his spirits sinking lower and lower, far heavier than they had ever been back at Barton.

Roughly a week after their arrival, the thing they had both been half waiting for happened. When they trundled back from their morning drive, there was a visiting card laid on the entry table.
“Good God!” Marianne gasped. “He was here while we were out.”

Elinor’s own chest lightened at last, relieved to know Willoughby was actually in London. “Don’t worry,” she said, “he will call again tomorrow, I’m certain of it.” Marianne did not even seem to hear her, and the second Mrs. Jennings stepped into the hall, she snatched the precious card up and ran off to her room to stare at it.

That one small discovery sent Marianne’s already frayed anticipation spiking higher than ever. She could not sit still, could not read, could not sew, every hour of the day spent listening for the knock at the door that would announce him. The next morning, she insisted the others leave her behind when they went out to pay calls, certain Willoughby would come while the house was quiet.

When Elinor and the others returned, one glance at Marianne’s pale, tight face told her everything: Willoughby had not come. A servant walked in a moment later, setting a new note on the table.
“It’s for me!” Marianne cried, lunging forward.
“No, miss, it’s for Mrs. Jennings.”
Marianne snatched it anyway, her face falling when she saw the address. “It really is for her. How infuriating.”
“You were expecting a letter, though, weren’t you?” Elinor asked, unable to hold her tongue any longer.
“Yes, a little—hardly anything at all.” She paused, then lifted her chin to meet Elinor’s gaze. “You don’t trust me, do you?”
“Marianne, how can you say that to me?” Elinor said, flushing. “You are the one who never shares anything.”
“Me?” Marianne fired back. “You never tell anyone a single thing about yourself! We are exactly the same, you see. We both have nothing to hide, but you refuse to speak of what matters to you, and I—well, I never hide a single thing.”
Elinor could not argue. She was bound to keep Lucy Steele’s secret, and could not explain why she was so guarded, so she did not push Marianne for further openness.

Mrs. Jennings read the note aloud when she came in: it was from Lady Middleton, announcing she and Sir John had arrived in Conduit Street the night before, and inviting the three of them and the Dashwood sisters over for dinner the following evening. Sir John had been tied up with hunting business, and Lady Middleton had caught a bad cold, so they had not been able to call first at Berkeley Street.
The invitation was accepted, but Marianne fought going right up until the last minute. She still had not seen Willoughby, and she refused to risk missing his second call by being out of the house. It took Elinor nearly an hour of gentle persuasion to make her agree to come, for the sake of basic politeness to their hostess.

The party at the Middletons’ was as chaotic as Elinor expected. Sir John, barely settled in London, had already rounded up nearly twenty young people and thrown together an impromptu dance. His wife was mortified: back in the country, a last-minute dance was perfectly acceptable, but in London, where everyone was so fussy about their reputation for elegance, it would look terribly unrefined if word got out she had hosted a tiny, informal ball with only two fiddles and a sideboard of food for refreshments.
Mr. and Mrs. Palmer were there too. Mr. Palmer, who had deliberately avoided visiting his mother-in-law at Berkeley Street since their arrival, barely even nodded at the sisters when they walked in, glancing at them for half a second like he had no idea who they were, before tipping a lazy, indifferent salute at Mrs. Jennings from across the room.
Marianne scanned the entire ballroom the second she stepped over the threshold, and one quick look told her Willoughby was not there. She sank onto the nearest unoccupied chair, determined to neither enjoy herself nor speak to a single person.
After an hour or so, Mr. Palmer sauntered over to chat with the sisters, acting surprised to see them in town, even though Colonel Brandon had told him of their arrival ages ago, and he had once made a whole string of crude little jokes about their coming to London.
“I thought you two were still stuck in Devonshire,” he drawled.
“Did you?” Elinor said.
“When are you going back?”
“I have no idea,” she replied, and that was the end of their conversation.

Marianne had never hated dancing more in her life, and never left a party so sore and exhausted. She complained nonstop all the way back to Berkeley Street.
“Aye, I know exactly why you’re so worn out,” Mrs. Jennings said, winking. “If a certain nameless gentleman had turned up, you’d have danced till dawn no problem. And honestly, it was terribly rude of him not to show. He was invited, you know.”
“Invited?” Marianne gasped, going pale.
“Sir John ran right into him on the street this morning, my daughter Middleton told me.”
Marianne said nothing more, but her face was crumpled with hurt so sharp Elinor’s ached for her. She resolved that very minute to write to their mother at Barton first thing the next morning, tell her all her fears that Willoughby was not as sincere as he seemed, beg her to come to London and pull the truth out of Marianne before her heart broke irreparably. Her resolve hardened even more when she saw Marianne hunched over her desk after breakfast the next day, scribbling furiously at a letter that could not possibly be addressed to anyone but Willoughby.

Mrs. Jennings stepped out to run errands halfway through the morning, and Elinor sat down to begin her letter to her mother immediately. Marianne paced restlessly from window to window, too anxious to sit still, too raw to make conversation, before sinking down to sit huddled by the fire, lost in dark, quiet thought.
Elinor had barely gotten her letter half written when the front door knocker rapped, and the servant announced Colonel Brandon. Marianne, who had seen him coming up the steps through the window, hated the thought of making polite small talk with a guest just then, and slipped out of the room before he even crossed the threshold.
He looked far graver than usual, and even though he said he was glad to find Elinor alone, as if he had something very private to tell her, he sat twisting his gloves for several long minutes without speaking. Elinor was certain he had come to talk about Marianne, and waited patiently for him to begin.
At last he lifted his head, his voice thick with quiet agitation.
“When shall I congratulate you, Miss Dashwood, on gaining Mr. Willoughby as a brother?”
Elinor was completely taken aback. She had no answer ready, so all she could do was ask him what he meant.
He tried to force a smile, but it was wobbly. “Your sister’s engagement to Mr. Willoughby is common knowledge around town. Everyone talks of their upcoming marriage.”
“It can’t possibly be common knowledge,” Elinor said, surprised. “Her own family does not know it is settled.”
He looked shocked. “I beg your pardon, I did not mean to be impertinent. But I had no idea there was any secret kept! They correspond openly, and every soul in our circle—Mrs. Jennings, the Middletons, Mrs. Palmer—all speak of it as a done deal. I might still have doubted it, if I had not walked in just now and seen your sister’s handwriting on the letter the servant was carrying to post for Mr. Willoughby. I came to ask you, to hear it from someone I trust, that it is all settled. That there is no chance for… that there is nothing left for me to hope for.”
His quiet, unguarded admission of his love for Marianne hit Elinor like a soft, heavy weight. She could not speak for a long moment, and when she did, she knew she must be kind, even if she did not know the full truth of the situation. She told him that while neither Marianne nor Willoughby had ever formally announced an engagement, she had no doubt of their deep mutual affection, and that their correspondence came as no surprise at all.
He listened to her in silence, and the second she finished speaking, he stood up quickly, his face pale with grief.
“Then I wish your sister every happiness in the world,” he said, his voice shaking. “And I wish Willoughby the strength to deserve her.”
He tipped his hat, and left without another word.
Elinor sat staring at the half-written letter on her desk, no comfort at all in what had just passed. She was left with the heavy, sad weight of Colonel Brandon’s broken heart hanging over her, and her fear for Marianne’s future sharpened to a point. She could not even bring herself to wish for the pain of Brandon’s unrequited love to be lifted, not when every turn of events seemed to be leading straight towards the heartbreak that would confirm it forever.

For three full, empty days that stretched longer than any Elinor could remember, nothing happened to soften her quiet regret that she’d written to their mother in the country, laying out the tangled mess of Marianne’s heartbreak. Willoughby never called, never sent a note, never even loitered on the street outside their Berkeley Street lodgings. By the fourth evening, they were due to accompany Lady Middleton to a grand town party, while Mrs. Jennings stayed home to tend to her youngest ailing daughter.

Marianne prepared for the event as if she were only marking time, not even pausing to smooth the ribbons on her favorite gown or run a comb through her half-tousled curls. She sat hunched by the drawing-room fire long after tea, completely lost to her own brooding thoughts, so disconnected from the world around her that she did not stir or shift her position even when Elinor passed right in front of her. When the maid finally announced Lady Middleton was waiting for them at the front door, Marianne jolted upright as if woken from a deep trance, as if she had forgotten they were expected anywhere at all.

They arrived at the party right on time, riding in the Middleton carriage through a long line of other waiting vehicles, before alighting, climbing the candle-lit stairs, and hearing their names called out loud from landing to landing for the crowd to hear. The room they stepped into was dazzling with chandeliers, packed shoulder to shoulder with guests, and sweltering enough to make the silk of their gowns cling damp to their backs. After curtsying to their hostess to perform their required polite duty, they were swallowed up in the throng, adding their own share of body heat to the already stifling space.
When Lady Middleton settled in for a long game of Cassino, Elinor spotted two empty chairs a short distance from the card table, and tugged Marianne over to sit, knowing her sister had no energy left to drift through the small talk of strangers.

They had not been seated five minutes before Elinor caught sight of Willoughby, standing barely three yards off, deep in earnest conversation with a fashionably dressed young woman whose hat was piled high with ostrich plumes. Their eyes met first, and he gave her a stiff, distant bow, not making the smallest move to approach, not even sparing a glance for Marianne who he must surely have seen the second he looked up. Elinor turned to check her sister, and that exact moment Marianne spotted him. Her whole face flooded with sudden, bright, disbelieving joy, and she would have sprung up and run to him immediately if Elinor had not clamped a firm hand on her arm to hold her back.
“Good heavens!” Marianne gasped, barely loud enough for Elinor to hear. “He’s there—he’s right there! Why won’t he look at me? Why can’t I go speak to him?”
“Be calm, I beg of you,” Elinor whispered back, heart thrumming. “Do not let every stranger in this room see how you feel. Perhaps he has not noticed you yet.”
Elinor did not even believe that lie herself, and Marianne was far too raw for composure, her face tight with an agony of impatience that twisted every line of her features.

At last Willoughby turned fully toward them. Marianne shot to her feet, spoke his name in a soft, trembling tone full of old affection, and held her hand out to him. He walked over, but his gaze stayed fixed almost entirely on Elinor, as if he was actively forcing himself not to meet Marianne’s eyes, and asked in a hurried, hollow tone after Mrs. Dashwood, and how long they had been in town. Elinor was so stunned by the cold formality of it that she could not get a single word out in reply.
Marianne’s face burned crimson. “Good God, Willoughby!” she cried, voice breaking with emotion. “What is the meaning of this? Have you not received all my letters? Will you not even shake my hand?”
He could not avoid the gesture then, but his touch was light and fleeting, as if holding her hand burned him. Elinor watched him fight to steady his expression, forcing it into a flat, impassive calm after a long pause.
“I did myself the honor of calling at Berkeley Street last Tuesday,” he said evenly, “and was very sorry not to find you all at home. I hope my card was not mislaid.”
“But my notes?” Marianne pressed, frantic now. “This is some terrible mistake, I know it is—what can possibly be the matter? Tell me, Willoughby, for heaven’s sake tell me what has gone wrong.”
He made no reply, his face flushing bright red and his old embarrassment flooding back, until he caught the eye of the fashionable young woman he had been speaking to earlier. The small jolt of it made him snap back to self-control. “Yes, I did receive word that you had arrived in town, the note you were kind enough to send,” he said, then gave a shallow, distant little bow, turned on his heel, and walked straight back to rejoin his friend.

Marianne went deathly white, her legs buckling under her, and she sank back hard into her chair. Elinor was certain she would faint at any second, so she leaned over to shield her sister from the curious stares of nearby guests, dabbing lavender water to her temples to bring her around.
As soon as Marianne could draw breath again, she gripped Elinor’s wrist. “Go to him,” she begged. “Force him to come back to me. Tell him I must see him—must speak to him this instant. I will not rest, I will not have a single moment’s peace until this misunderstanding is sorted out. Go to him now.”
“It cannot be done here,” Elinor soothed. “This is no place for such conversations. Wait, only until tomorrow.”
It was all she could do to hold Marianne back from chasing after Willoughby herself. There was no chance of persuading her to feign even a small degree of composure, and she kept breaking into quiet, muffled exclamations of wretchedness under her breath. A few minutes later, Elinor saw Willoughby slip out the door toward the staircase, and told Marianne he had left the party entirely. Marianne immediately begged her to ask Lady Middleton to take them home, so miserable she could not bear to stay another minute.
Lady Middleton was halfway through a rubber of cards, but far too polite to refuse a guest who claimed to feel unwell. She handed her cards off to a friend, and their carriage was fetched as quickly as the staff could manage. No words were exchanged on the ride back to Berkeley Street. Marianne was trapped in a silent, heavy agony too deep for tears, and the luck that Mrs. Jennings was still out when they arrived let them slip straight up to their private rooms without pausing for questions. A whiff of hartshorn revived Marianne a little, and she soon undressed and climbed into bed, asking to be left alone.

Elinor stepped out to wait for Mrs. Jennings to return, with plenty of quiet time to turn every second of the night over in her head. She had no remaining doubt that Willoughby and Marianne had been fully, secretly engaged. It was equally clear he had tired of that bond. No matter how desperately Marianne clung to the hope of some innocent misapprehension, Elinor could not explain his behavior away as anything but a total, cruel shift in his feelings. The only thing that softened her rage at him was that flicker of guilt and embarrassment she had spotted on his face, proof he had not set out to toy with Marianne’s heart from the very start with no honest affection at all. Absence had worn down his regard, Elinor reasoned, and some new, more convenient match had made him choose to cast Marianne off—but the love he had shown her at Barton Cottage had once been real.
For Marianne, though, the pain of that night’s meeting was only the start. Elinor thought of her own lot by comparison: she still held all her deep respect and affection for Edward, no matter what barriers or distance might come between them, and her heart had that solid, steady ground to lean on. But every bright romantic dream Marianne had ever tied to Willoughby was shattering before her eyes, and it looked more and more likely that this separation would be final, irreconcilable, and sharper than any pain she had ever known.

The frigid January sun had barely scratched a pale, watery glow through the frosted bedroom windows before the housemaid had even crossed the hall to light their chamber fire. Half-dressed, her stockings slipping down her calves and her hair falling loose unbound over her shift, Marianne Dashwood knelt pressed to the window-seat cushions, soaking up every scrap of dim light to guide her quill, writing so fast that only the constant, streaming tears blurring her eyes slowed her hand.

Elinor was woken at last by her sister’s ragged, half-stifled sobs. She lay quiet for a long minute, watching Marianne heave with grief, her chest tight with quiet, unspoken anxiety, before she spoke in a tone so soft it barely disturbed the cold air. “Marianne, may I ask—?”

“No, Elinor,” Marianne choked out, her voice strung tight with a desperate, unnatural calm that broke the second the words left her mouth. “Ask nothing. You will soon know all.”

That fragile hold on composure vanished entirely. Minutes dragged by before she could bring herself to dip her quill in the ink again, every few words interrupted by a fresh burst of weeping that forced her to drop her pen. It was plain to Elinor that Marianne knew, deep in her bones, this was almost certainly the last letter she would ever write to Willoughby.

She tended to her sister as gently and unobtrusively as she could, and would have wrapped an arm around her to soothe her, if Marianne had not begged her, frayed and trembling with raw, nervous irritability, not to speak a single word to her for any price. It was a mercy for both that they did not linger shut in the room together long. No sooner was Marianne fully dressed than she could not bear to stay still, wandering restlessly through every corner of the house, avoiding the sight of every servant and acquaintance until the breakfast bell rang. She would not touch a single bite of food at the table, and Elinor spent the whole meal deliberately drawing all of Mrs. Jennings’s chatter to herself, never urging Marianne to eat, never pitying her openly, never drawing the older woman’s curious eye to her sister’s obvious distress.

Breakfast was a favorite indulgence of Mrs. Jennings’s, and stretched on for nearly an hour. They had just settled around the shared work table to sort sewing scraps when a servant brought a letter to Marianne. She snatched it out of his hand so fast her knuckles scraped the envelope, her face draining to a ghastly, deathly white, before she turned and ran out of the room without a word. Elinor’s stomach dropped so fast she thought she might faint, her hands shaking so hard she could barely keep hold of her sewing, certain she would not be able to hide her own distress from Mrs. Jennings. But the good-natured old woman saw nothing amiss, cackling at the sight of Marianne’s haste as she measured lengths of worsted for her new rug.
“Lord, I never saw a young woman so desperately in love in all my days!” she laughed. “My own girls were silly enough, but nothing to her! Miss Marianne is quite a different creature, all wrapped up in that man. I hope he doesn’t keep her waiting to marry him much longer—it’s downright grievous to see her looking so pale and forlorn. Pray, when do you two expect the wedding?”

Elinor forced a stiff, wobbly smile to her face, even as her chest ached. “You really have talked yourself into believing my sister is engaged to Mr. Willoughby, ma’am? I thought it was only a joke, but such a serious question makes it clear you are far more invested in the story than that. I must beg you not to deceive yourself any longer. Nothing would surprise me more than to hear they intend to marry.”

“For shame, Miss Dashwood! How can you talk so?” Mrs. Jennings hooted. “Everyone knows it is a match! They were over their heads in love the second they met in Devonshire, weren’t they? I saw them together every single day, all day long, out walking or riding or cozied up by the fire! And didn’t Marianne come up to London with me on purpose to buy her wedding gowns? You’re so sly about your own affairs, you think no one else has any sense—but the whole of town has known about their engagement for weeks! I tell everyone, and so does my daughter Charlotte.”

“Indeed, ma’am,” Elinor said, very serious now, “you are mistaken. Spreading that story is unkind, and you will find that out soon enough, no matter if you won’t believe me now.”

Mrs. Jennings just laughed again, but Elinor had no strength left to argue. She was burning with the need to know exactly what Willoughby had written, so she hurried up the stairs to their shared bedchamber, and opened the door to find Marianne stretched across the bed, half-choking on her sobs, one crumpled letter clutched tight in her fist, two more discarded on the sheets beside her. Elinor did not say a word. She sat down on the edge of the bed, took Marianne’s cold hand in her own, kissed her forehead over and over, and then burst into tears herself, so violent at first they matched Marianne’s own.

Marianne could not speak, but she clung to her sister’s hand as if that touch was the only thing holding her above the wave of grief swallowing her whole. After a long while of them crying quietly together, Marianne pressed all the letters into Elinor’s hands, covered her face with her linen handkerchief, and sobbed so hard it was almost a scream. Elinor knew that this excess of pain had to run its course before it could fade, so she sat beside her, holding her hand, until the worst of the sobs quieted, before she turned eagerly to Willoughby’s letter. It was dated Bond Street, mid-January:
> I have just had the honour of receiving your letter, for which I beg to return my sincere acknowledgments. I am much concerned to find there was anything in my behaviour last night that did not meet your approbation; and though I am quite at a loss to discover in what point I could be so unfortunate as to offend you, I entreat your forgiveness of what I can assure you to have been perfectly unintentional. I shall never reflect on my former acquaintance with your family in Devonshire without the most grateful pleasure, and flatter myself it will not be broken by any mistake or misapprehension of my actions. My esteem for your whole family is very sincere; but if I have been so unfortunate as to give rise to a belief of more than I felt, or meant to express, I shall reproach myself for not having been more guarded in my professions of that esteem. That I should ever have meant more you will allow to be impossible, when you understand that my affections have been long engaged elsewhere, and it will not be many weeks, I believe, before this engagement is fulfilled. It is with great regret that I obey your commands in returning the letters with which I have been honoured from you, and the lock of hair, which you so obligingly bestowed on me.
> I am, dear Madam,
> Your most obedient
> humble servant,

Elinor’s blood ran cold with indignation as she read, again and again, every line more cruel than the last. She had known the letter would confirm Willoughby’s inconstancy, that their connection was broken forever, but she never imagined he could write something so devoid of honor, so shamelessly, insultingly cold. He did not even acknowledge the warmth they had shared at Barton, did not admit he had broken faith, did not express a single flicker of regret. Every sentence was a deliberate insult, proving him a hard, unprincipled man. For a long while she sat frozen, staring at the page, half-wanting to cry out that Marianne had escaped a lifetime of misery married to such a monster, but holding her tongue, terrified that even that truth would cut her sister deeper. Lost in her own thoughts of Willoughby’s depravity, and of Edward, the quiet, gentle man whose own secret heartache she had carried alone for months, she forgot all about the other three letters in her lap, forgot how long she had been sitting there, until the sound of carriage wheels crunching up the drive pulled her back. She glanced out the window, and saw Mrs. Jennings’s chariot, which they had not ordered to leave until one o’clock that afternoon.

She hurried down to excuse herself, telling Mrs. Jennings Marianne was far too unwell to leave, and the kind woman accepted the apology at once, wishing Marianne a speedy recovery before she set off on her calls. Elinor returned to the bedroom just in time to catch Marianne as she stumbled off the bed, faint and giddy, ready to collapse to the floor. She had barely eaten a proper meal or slept through a full night in days, sustained only by the fever of waiting to see Willoughby, and now that the tension had snapped, she was left weak, head throbbing, her stomach roiling with nervous nausea. A glass of spiced warm wine Elinor fetched at once brought her back to herself a little, and she mumbled through her tears, “Poor Elinor! How unhappy I make you.”

“I only wish there was anything I could do to comfort you,” Elinor whispered.

It was too much for Marianne. She cried out, voice cracking, “Oh Elinor, I am so miserable I can scarce bear it!” before her sobs swallowed her words again.

“Exert yourself, my dearest Marianne,” Elinor begged, wrapping her arms tight around her. “If you give in to this grief you will kill yourself, and break the heart of every person who loves you. Think of our mother! Think of the pain she would suffer seeing you like this—for her sake, you must try to hold on.”

“I cannot, I cannot,” Marianne wailed, clinging to her. “Leave me, if I distress you! Hate me, forget me! Do not torture me by telling me to exert myself! It is so easy for people who have no sorrow of their own to say that. Happy Elinor, you have no idea what I suffer.”

“Happy?” Elinor said, her voice solemn and soft, tears brimming in her own eyes. “Ah, Marianne, if you knew half the grief I carry… How could I ever be happy, watching you break apart like this?”

“Forgive me, forgive me,” Marianne sobbed, her arms tightening around Elinor’s neck. “I know you care for me, I know how good your heart is. But you are happy, you must be! Edward loves you! Nothing in the world can take that away from you.”

“So many things can,” Elinor said quietly.

“No, no, no,” Marianne cried, wild with pain. “He loves you, and only you. You have no cause to grieve.”

“I can take no pleasure at all while I see you this wretched.”

“And you will never see me otherwise,” Marianne moaned. “This misery of mine can never be healed.”

“You must not talk that way,” Elinor said. “Have you no friends, no comforts? Think how much worse this would be if you had been engaged for months, if you had committed your whole life to him before his true nature came out. Every extra day you loved him in good faith would only have made this blow far more terrible.”

“Engagement?” Marianne pulled back, her red, puffy eyes wide. “There was no engagement. He is not so unworthy as you think. He broke no formal promise to me.”

“But he told you he loved you.”

“Yes—no, he never said the words outright. He implied it every single day, at Barton, on every ride, every walk, every hour we spent together. Sometimes I thought he had spoken it out loud, but he never had. It was never formally declared.”

“But you wrote to him?” Elinor said, glancing at the other letters in her lap.

“Yes,” Marianne whispered. “How could that be wrong, after all that had passed between us? But I cannot talk about it.”

Elinor turned then to the three notes Marianne had sent Willoughby, her curiosity burning brighter than ever, and read them all. The first, written the day they arrived in town, was bright, full of excited hope, telling Willoughby she was in London, that she could not resist the chance to come see him, begging him to call on her that very night. The second, written after the dance at the Middletons’, was full of confusion and hurt, wondering why he had never answered her first note, why he had avoided her at the dance when she had heard he was invited. The third, written after the night Willoughby had snubbed her in the street, was raw and vulnerable, demanding he explain his cruel behavior, begging him to tell her if someone had lied about her to turn him against her, and asking him to return her letters and the lock of hair if his feelings for her had truly died.

Elinor’s heart ached for the impetuous, loving confidence that had led Marianne to write such tender notes, unprompted by any formal vow. “You do not understand,” Marianne said, when she saw Elinor had finished reading. “I felt as solemnly bound to him as if we had signed a legal marriage contract. As if we had spoken our vows before a hundred witnesses.”

“I believe that,” Elinor said softly. “But unfortunately, he did not feel the same way.”

“He did, Elinor! For weeks and weeks at Barton, he felt exactly that!” Marianne cried. “I know he did. Something evil, some cruel trick played on me, changed him. That lock of hair he sends back so casually? He begged me for it, on his knees almost, his eyes bright and soft, the last evening we spent together at Barton. You must remember how happy we were that night! You must remember how upset he was the morning he left, telling me we would not see each other for weeks, his voice breaking with tears. Could that man have written this cruel letter, of his own free will?”

For a long moment she could not speak, then she lifted her head, defiant through her tears. “I have been cruelly used—but not by Willoughby. Some other enemy, some woman he is now engaged to, must have lied about me, poisoned his mind against me. I would rather believe every single one of our acquaintances leagued together to ruin me in his eyes, than believe his heart is naturally so cruel. Besides you, Mama, and Edward, there is no one in the whole world I would not sooner suspect of wickedness than Willoughby.”

Elinor would not argue, only held her tighter. “Then let them never have the satisfaction of seeing you broken, my dear. Let your innocence be enough to carry you through, and rob them of the triumph they crave.”

“No,” Marianne sobbed. “Misery like mine leaves no room for pride. I do not care who sees how wretched I am. Let them all gloat. The people who suffer a little can be proud, can brush off insults, can pretend to be fine—but I cannot. I cannot hide how much this hurts.”

“For my sake, and Mama’s—”

“I would do anything for you two. But to smile, to pretend I am happy, when my heart is shattered? No one could ask that of me.”

They fell quiet then. Elinor paced back and forth between the window and the fire, her mind racing, not noticing the warmth of the flames against her hands, not seeing the street outside through the frosted glass. Marianne sat huddled at the foot of the bed, rereading Willoughby’s letter, shuddering at every line, before she burst out again, “That line! *‘The lock of hair, which you so obligingly bestowed on me’*. How dare he? That is unpardonable! Elinor, there is no way to justify that cruelty. Whoever this woman is, she planned this for months, plotted to steal him away from me! He never spoke of any other woman but me.”

Another long, quiet pause dragged by, Marianne’s whole frame shaking with quiet sobs. “I must go home,” she said at last. “I need to be with Mama. Can we leave tomorrow?”

“Tomorrow?” Elinor said. “That is far too soon. We are guests in Mrs. Jennings’s house, and it would be rude in the extreme to rush off with no warning.”

“Then in a day or two,” Marianne said. “But I cannot stay here long. I cannot bear all their questions, all their stares. The Middletons, the Palmers—their pity, especially Lady Middleton’s polite, empty pity, will kill me. I can’t stand it.”

Elinor helped her lie back down on the bed, but no position brought her ease. She twisted and turned, hysterical with pain, until Elinor finally persuaded her to take a few drops of lavender spirits to calm her nerves. The draught worked at last, and Marianne went still, lying quiet and motionless on the sheets, drained of every last tear, until Mrs. Jennings returned later that afternoon.

Mrs. Jennings came trundling back to their rooms the second she stepped through the front door, not even waiting for the maid to answer her knock before shoving it open, bonnet askew, face soft with unfeigned concern. “Oh my dears, how do you do?” she crooned, voice thick with pity, leaning over Marianne where the girl lay face-down on the bed. Marianne only turned her cheek further into the pillow, too raw to even speak.
“Poor little thing, she looks half dead, and no wonder!” Mrs. Jennings went on, turning to Elinor. “It’s all true, that horrid good-for-nothing scoundrel’s set to be married any day now. I swear I almost sank right through the floor when Mrs. Taylor told me half an hour ago—she heard it straight from Miss Grey’s own closest friend, else I’d never have believed such wickedness. I told Mrs. Taylor plain, if that’s true he’s treated a young lady I care for abominably, and I hope with all my soul his wife torments his heart out every day of their marriage. You can count on me to say that to anyone who’ll listen! I won’t stand for men leading young girls on like that, and if I ever run into him again I’ll give him a dressing-down he won’t forget for years. Still, there’s a comfort, my sweet Marianne—he’s not the only man worth having in the whole world, and with that pretty face of yours, you’ll never lack for admirers. I won’t fuss over you any longer, let you cry it all out, that’s the best way. Lucky the Parrys and Sandersons are coming over tonight, they’ll cheer you right up a bit.”
She tiptoed out of the room after that, as if even the soft thud of her slippers might make Marianne’s grief sharper.
To Elinor’s great surprise, Marianne insisted on coming down to dinner. Elinor tried to talk her out of it, but Marianne shook her head, eyes red-rimmed and hollow. “No, I’ll go down. I can bear it. If I stay locked up here, you’ll all fuss over me twice as much. It’s quieter among a crowd where no one’s paying me any mind.” Elinor, relieved to see her acting on even that small practical impulse, didn’t argue further. She helped Marianne smooth her crumpled gown while the girl still sat on the edge of the bed, and walked her down to the dining room the second the dinner bell rang.
Marianne looked wretched, her face drained of all color, but she ate a few more bites than Elinor dared hope, and stayed far calmer than expected. She never spoke a single word, though, too lost in her own swirling, hurting thoughts to even notice the world around her. If she had caught half of Mrs. Jennings’ well-meaning, unsubtle attentions—piling the richest meat on her plate, pressing every sweet delicacy within reach, chattering a mile a minute about trivial neighborhood news to distract her—she would never have held that calm.
Elinor thanked their kind hostess warmly on her sister’s behalf, when Marianne could not rouse herself to do so. Mrs. Jennings doted on Marianne that whole meal, the way a parent indulges their favorite child on the last day of their holiday. She staked claim to the warmest spot by the fire for her, nudged every treat within arm’s reach, and kept up a steady stream of light, silly stories meant to pull Marianne out of her dark mood. If Elinor’s chest hadn’t been tight with her sister’s grief, she might have laughed at the sight of Mrs. Jennings trying to cure a broken heart with plates of olives, heaped sweetmeats, and a roaring hearth fire.
It didn’t last long, though. When Mrs. Jennings repeated her little distractions three times over, Marianne finally jolted back to awareness. She gasped out a quiet cry of “Misery!” and waved Elinor off sharply so her sister wouldn’t follow, then fled the room as fast as she could.
“Poor soul!” Mrs. Jennings exclaimed the second the door clicked shut behind her. “It breaks my heart to see her so. She didn’t even finish her glass of wine, or the dried cherries I set out just for her! Lord, nothing seems to cheer her at all. I’d send all over town for anything I thought she might fancy, I swear I would. It’s the strangest thing, that a man could treat such a pretty, sweet girl so ill! But when one side has piles of money and the other has next to nothing, they don’t care a fig for love, do they?”
“The lady—Miss Grey, you said her name was—she’s very wealthy then?” Elinor asked.
“Fifty thousand pounds a year, my dear! Have you ever seen her? They say she’s a sharp, stylish girl, but no great beauty. I knew her aunt Biddy Henshawe well, she married an enormously rich man, the whole family’s rolling in money. Fifty thousand! And he needs it badly too, everyone says he’s deep in debt, what with his fancy curricle and his expensive hunters! No wonder he ran off. But that’s no excuse. A young man goes and courts a pretty girl, promises her the whole future, he has no right to break his word just because he’s short on cash and a richer girl offers to take him. Why couldn’t he sell his horses, let his big house, send his servants away, and cut back on his silly spending? I’d bet Marianne would have waited for him happily till he got back on his feet. But young men these days won’t give up a single bit of their pleasure, not for anything.”
“Do you know what Miss Grey is like? Is she said to be amiable?”
“I never heard a bad word about her, hardly ever heard her mentioned at all. Except Mrs. Taylor saying this morning that Miss Walker told her that Mr. and Mrs. Ellison—her guardians, you know—are dead glad she’s marrying, because she and Mrs. Ellison could never get along for five minutes.”
After a pause, Mrs. Jennings clucked her tongue. “Your poor sister’s gone up to her room to wail all alone now, isn’t she? There’s nothing we can do to cheer her up? Well, soon all our friends will arrive, that’ll take her mind off things a little. What game shall we play? I know she hates whist, is there any round game she might like?”
“Dear ma’am, your kindness is more than enough, but it’s quite unnecessary,” Elinor said softly. “Marianne won’t be coming back down this evening, I’m sure. I’ll persuade her to go to bed early, she’s terribly run down and needs rest.”
“Aye, that’s the best thing for her. Let her order whatever little supper she fancies and go straight to bed. No wonder she’s looked so pale and downcast these past two weeks! I suppose this awful news has been hanging over her all that time, and that letter that came this morning finished breaking her heart. I’m so sorry I teased her about letters the other day, I’d never have done that if I’d guessed what was going on! But how was I to know? I thought it was just a silly love note, and young people love to be teased about their sweethearts. Sir John and my daughters will be so upset when they hear! I ought to have stopped by Conduit Street on my way home to tell them, but I’ll see them tomorrow.”
“I would be so grateful if you’d ask Sir John and Mrs. Palmer never to mention Mr. Willoughby, not even to make the smallest allusion to what happened, in front of Marianne,” Elinor said earnestly. “Their own good nature will tell them how cruel it would be to let on they know anything about it when she’s near. The less anyone says to me on the subject, the easier it will be for me too, I’m sure you understand.”
“Lord yes, of course I do! It must be terrible for you to hear it talked of. I wouldn’t breathe a word of it to your sister for the whole world, you saw I didn’t say a thing all through dinner. Sir John and my girls are all thoughtful, I’ll give them a quiet hint, they’ll be as careful as can be. The less you talk about these heartbreaks, the faster they blow over and get forgotten. What good does gossiping do, anyway?”
“None at all in this case,” Elinor said. “It can only do harm, worse than most other such stories, because there are circumstances here that no one else needs to know, for everyone’s sake. I must do Mr. Willoughby that much justice—he never made any formal, binding promise to Marianne.”
“Law, my dear, don’t go defending him! No formal promise? He dragged her all over Allenham House, pointed out exactly which bedchambers they would live in after they were married! That’s as good as a promise to anyone with half a sense.”
Elinor didn’t push the matter further, for her sister’s sake. She knew no good would come of bringing the full truth out, not for Marianne, not for Willoughby. After a short quiet pause, Mrs. Jennings brightened up again, ever her usual cheerful self.
“Well, it’s an ill wind that blows no good, isn’t it? This will all work out for Colonel Brandon! He’ll have her in the end, mark my words. They’ll be married by midsummer, you’ll see! He’ll be over the moon when he hears this news. I hope he comes tonight. It’s a far better match for your sister, two thousand a year, no debts at all—except that little orphan girl he takes care of, but you can apprentice her out for almost nothing, it’s no trouble at all. Delaford is the loveliest place, all old-fashioned and cozy, with high garden walls draped with the best fruit trees in the whole county, and a mulberry tree in the back corner! Lord, your daughter Charlotte and I stuffed ourselves sick on mulberries the last time we visited. There’s a dove cote, lovely little stew ponds, a pretty little canal, everything you could ever want. It’s right next to the church, only a quarter mile from the turnpike road, so you never get bored—sit in the old yew arbour behind the house and you can see every carriage that goes past. A butcher lives right in the village, the parsonage is a stone’s throw away. It’s a thousand times nicer than Barton Park, where you have to send three miles for a cut of meat and the nearest neighbour is your own mother! As soon as I can, I’ll go cheer the Colonel up. One sorrow drives out another, right? We just have to get Willoughby out of her head.”
“If we can do that, ma’am,” Elinor said with a faint, tired smile, “we’ll manage perfectly well, with or without Colonel Brandon.”
She stood up then to go find Marianne, and found her exactly where she expected, huddled over the last smoldering embers of the fire, no other light in the room.
“Please, leave me alone,” was all Marianne said to her when she came in.
“I will leave you,” Elinor promised, “if you’ll get into bed.”
Marianne fought her at first, too raw and restless with pain to lie still, but Elinor’s soft, steady persuasion soon won her over. She laid her aching head on the pillow, and Elinor left her hoping she might get a few hours of quiet rest.
When she went back down to the drawing room, Mrs. Jennings came bustling in holding a crystal wine glass brimmed with dark amber liquid. “My dear, I just remembered I’ve got a bottle of the finest old Constantia wine in the whole house, it was my poor late husband’s favorite. Whenever he had that old colicky gout of his, he swore it did him more good than any medicine the doctor ever gave him. I brought a glass up for your sister!”
“Dear ma’am, you are too kind,” Elinor laughed softly, thinking of how different a broken heart was from a gouty stomach. “But I just left Marianne in bed, almost asleep. Rest is the only thing that will help her right now, if you don’t mind I’ll drink it myself.”
Mrs. Jennings was a little disappointed she hadn’t made it to Marianne five minutes earlier, but agreed to the compromise. Elinor drank most of the wine, and thought that if it could soothe a colicky gout, it might very well soothe a heavy, worried heart just as well.
Colonel Brandon walked in just as they were sitting down to tea. Elinor saw his eyes drift immediately around the room, searching for Marianne, and she knew at once he had not expected to find her there. He already knew she was gone.
Mrs. Jennings didn’t notice, though, leaning over to whisper at Elinor behind her teacup, “The Colonel looks as grave as a judge, he hasn’t heard a thing. Go tell him, dear.”
A few minutes later he pulled a chair up next to Elinor, and the quiet look in his eyes told her he already knew all of it, before he even opened his mouth to ask after her sister.
“Marianne is not well,” Elinor said. “She’s been unwell all day, we persuaded her to go to bed early.”
“Then,” he said hesitantly, “what I heard this morning might—there might be more truth to it than I could force myself to believe at first.”
“What did you hear?”
“I was in a stationer’s shop on Pall Mall, running an errand. Two ladies were waiting for their carriage, and one was talking about a coming match, not even bothering to keep her voice down, so I couldn’t help overhearing. The name John Willoughby came up over and over, and she said the marriage to Miss Grey was all settled, not a secret any longer, it would happen in just a few weeks, and as soon as the ceremony was over they would go straight to Combe Magna, his estate in Somersetshire. I can’t tell you how shocked I was. I waited in the shop till they left, asked who the talkative lady was, and it was Mrs. Ellison—Miss Grey’s guardian, as I later found out.”
“That’s correct,” Elinor nodded. “And you may have heard Miss Grey has fifty thousand pounds. That, if anything, is the reason for all this.”
“It may be,” he said, stopping for a moment, his voice soft and unsure. “And your sister—how did she take it?”
“Her pain is very sharp. I can only hope it fades quickly. It was a cruel shock. Up till yesterday she never doubted he loved her, and even now—she will still make excuses for him, if she can. He was very deceitful, and there’s a cold hardness in him I never expected.”
“Ah, there is,” Colonel Brandon said. He didn’t say anything more, and soon the tea things were cleared away and everyone settled down to their card games, so the conversation was dropped. Mrs. Jennings watched them chattering happily a little while, fully expecting that once Elinor told him the news the Colonel would light up, giddy with joy now that his rival was gone. But to her complete surprise, he stayed far more quiet, thoughtful, and grave all evening than he had ever been before.

Marianne woke the next morning after a night of far more rest than she’d expected, the sharp, heavy weight of her heartbreak settling over her the second her eyes fluttered open, no softer for the few hours of unconsciousness that had blotted it out. Elinor sat with her before breakfast, coaxing her gently to speak every painful thought out loud, and by the time the maid called them to the table, they had circled the same raw, ugly truth again and again: Elinor steady, reasonable, laced with quiet affection, Marianne volatile, swinging wildly between wild hope and black despair. One minute she was certain Willoughby must be as innocent and wronged as she was, caught in some terrible trap no one had yet uncovered; the next, she could find no excuse cruel enough to exonerate him. At times she swore she did not care who stared at her out in public, at others she swore she would lock herself away forever from prying eyes, and on rare bursts of energy, she thought she could face the world’s pity and whispers head on.
Only one feeling never shifted: she would not, if she could help it, be in Mrs. Jennings’ company, and if she was forced to, she would not speak a single word of her grief to the older woman. She was fully convinced Mrs. Jennings felt no real compassion for her. “No, no, it cannot be,” she cried. “She cannot feel. Her kindness is not sympathy, her good nature is not tenderness. All she wants is gossip, and she only likes me now because I give her something to talk about.”
Elinor did not need this outburst to know how easily Marianne’s over-sensitive, exacting mind led her to unfair judgments of anyone whose manners did not match her strict standard of soft, delicate grace. Half the world might be clever and good, and Marianne had brilliant gifts and a warm, generous heart, but she was neither reasonable nor candid: she expected everyone else to hold the exact same feelings as her own, and judged every action by how it made her feel in the moment.
An incident not long after breakfast only deepened her low opinion of Mrs. Jennings, through no fault of the older woman’s own. She burst into their room grinning, holding a letter outstretched, certain she was bringing the girls exactly what they needed to cheer them. “Now, my dears, I’ve got something I know will do you both a world of good.”
Marianne heard enough. In a single, bright, feverish moment, her imagination conjured the rest: it was a letter from Willoughby, scrawled in his bold familiar handwriting, full of tenderness, contrition, a perfect, satisfying explanation for every cruel thing that had passed, and he would rush in right behind it, fall to his knees at her feet, and prove every word with the love shining in his eyes. The illusion shattered the very next second. The handwriting on the envelope was her mother’s, a hand she had never once thought unwelcome before. The agony of that brutal crash from wild, giddy hope to total disappointment was sharper than any pain she had felt since Willoughby’s rejection. She could not even find words to reproach Mrs. Jennings for the cruel trick of that false hope, only dissolved into wild, heaving sobs. Mrs. Jennings, bewildered, murmured several soft expressions of pity before she left, still urging them to read the “comforting” letter she had brought.
But the letter gave little comfort. Willoughby’s name filled every page. Their mother, still fully confident in their secret engagement, and as warm in her faith in his constancy as she had always been, had written only after Elinor’s own warning note to beg Marianne to be open with them both about what was going on. Every line was soft with love for Marianne, affection for Willoughby, and unshakable conviction that the two of them would be happy together in the end, and Marianne sobbed through every word, the desperate urge to rush home to Barton overtaking her. Her mother felt dearer than ever, dearest for that very misplaced faith in the man who had hurt her, and she begged Elinor to let them leave at once. Elinor had no certain idea if staying in London or returning to Barton would be better for Marianne, so she only persuaded her to wait a little longer, until they had their mother’s clear instructions to guide them.
Mrs. Jennings left the house earlier than usual that day, unable to rest until she had shared the sad news with the Middletons and Palmers, and refusing Elinor’s offer to accompany her, she headed out for the rest of the morning alone. Elinor sat down at the writing desk with a heavy heart, knowing the full unvarnished news she was about to send their mother would be a brutal shock, and Marianne hovered beside her, fixed to the table, watching her pen move, grieving for the terrible burden Elinor had to carry, and grieving even more for the pain that their mother would feel when she read those lines.
A quarter of an hour later, a sharp rap at the door made Marianne’s frayed nerves jolt so hard she nearly knocked over the inkwell.
“Who can that be?” Elinor said. “It’s far too early for callers. I thought we would be safe for a little longer.”
Marianne darted to the window, and her face twisted with annoyance. “It’s Colonel Brandon. We are never safe from him.”
“He won’t come in, not while Mrs. Jennings is out,” Elinor said.
“I won’t count on that,” Marianne said, already retreating fast to her own bedroom. “A man with nothing useful to do with his own time will never hesitate to intrude on other people’s.”
Marianne’s unfair, hasty guess was proved right: the servant showed Colonel Brandon in. Elinor could see immediately he had come out of nothing but deep concern for Marianne, his face pale, his eyes heavy with melancholy, his brief, anxious question after her showing how much the grief of the last days had weighed on him.
