She could not forgive her sister for dismissing him so lightly.
“I met Mrs. Jennings on Bond Street,” he said after their first polite greeting. “She encouraged me to stop by, and I was glad of the excuse, because I hoped I might find you alone, which is what I very much wanted. My purpose—my only wish in coming here—is to give your sister comfort, or not immediate comfort, but lasting conviction of the truth. My regard for her, for you, for your mother… will you allow me to prove it by telling you some circumstances that I would never share if I did not believe it would help you? I have spent hours convincing myself this is the right thing to do, but I still fear I may be making a mistake.”
“I understand you,” Elinor said softly, leaning forward. “You have more to tell me of Mr. Willoughby, that will lay his true character bare. It will be the greatest act of friendship anyone could show Marianne. My gratitude is yours already, and hers will come in time. Please, tell me everything.”
“I will, though I make a very awkward narrator, Miss Dashwood. I hardly know where to begin. I must go back far earlier than last October, when I left Barton.” He paused, heaved a heavy, sorrowful sigh. “You probably do not even remember a conversation we had one evening at Barton Park, after the dance, when I spoke of a young woman I had once known, who bore a striking resemblance to your sister Marianne.”
“Indeed, I have not forgotten it,” Elinor said.
He looked relieved, and went on. “If my tender memory does not deceive me, they are alike not just in face, but in every part of their nature. The same warm, impulsive heart, the same vivid, eager imagination and bright, unquenchable spirit. That woman was my orphaned cousin, raised as my father’s ward, and we grew up side by side, playmates first, then lovers. I cannot remember a time when I did not love Eliza. By the time we were seventeen, we had planned to elope together for Scotland, but our secret was betrayed by the treachery of her maid. My father banished me to a relative’s home miles away, and locked Eliza up until he had forced her to marry my older brother, against every wish she held. Our family estate was deeply in debt, and her fortune was the only thing that could save it. My brother did not love her, he did not even treat her with basic kindness. I thought her love for me would carry her through any hardship, but it was not enough. By the time I got the news that she had divorced him, two years later, after I’d been sent off to my regiment in the East Indies, her fate was already sealed. She had been left with no friend to turn to, no one to guide her, and she fell into ruin. That news is what laid this permanent gloom over me, even to this day.”
He could not speak for a moment more, rising to pace the length of the small drawing room to regain his composure. Elinor could not speak either, her heart aching for the pain he had carried for decades. He came back to her a minute later, took her hand, pressed it gently with grateful respect, and went on.
“It was three years before I came back to England, and my first act was to search for her. I tracked her for months, until I found her in a debtor’s prison, wasted, thin, fading fast from consumption, nothing left of the bright, blooming girl I had once loved. The only comfort I had was that her life was almost over, and I could see her settled in quiet, comfortable lodgings, visit her every day until she drew her last breath. Before she died, she left me her three-year-old daughter, her child from the relationship that had led to her ruin. That little girl was my sacred trust. I raised her as my distant ward, and three years ago, when she turned fourteen, I sent her to live with a respectable woman in Dorsetshire who ran a small school for girls. Everything went well for two years, until last February, when she disappeared. She had begged me to let her travel to Bath with the family of one of her school friends, and I agreed, foolish as it turned out. I did not see her for eight whole months. I searched everywhere, and no one would give me a single clue where she had gone. Until October, the very morning we were all supposed to go to Whitwell for that picnic, I got her letter. That is why I left Barton so suddenly, no explanation, which I know confused and offended all of you. She told me the name of the man who had seduced her, who left her pregnant, penniless, no address, promising he would come back for her, and never returned. That man was Willoughby.”
“Good heavens!” Elinor gasped.
“His character is laid bare now for you to see,” Colonel Brandon said gravely. “Extravagant, dissipated, far worse than any of us guessed. For weeks, I watched Marianne love him, confident he would marry her, and I did not know what to do. I hoped that her goodness might reform him, but after he discarded her for Miss Grey’s ten thousand pounds, there is no telling what cruel designs he may have had on her, if he thought he could get away with it. When Marianne compares her own grief, which comes from no fault of her own, no shame, no ruin, to the fate of my poor Eliza, young, abandoned, her life broken before it had barely begun, she will see her own suffering is lighter than she imagines. That is the only thing that might ease her pain. I would never have told you all this, burdened you with my family’s long unhappiness, if I did not truly believe it will help her heal.”
Elinor thanked him with tears in her eyes, agreeing that the truth of Willoughby’s actions would ease Marianne’s desperate, fruitless efforts to find innocence in him, even more than the knowledge of his rejection had. When she asked if he had seen Willoughby since the Whitwell incident, he nodded, gravely. “We met by appointment. Eliza had named him, and I would not let him go unpunished for what he did. We dueled. Neither of us was hurt, and no one ever found out. I moved the younger Eliza out to a quiet cottage in the country, as soon as she recovered from giving birth to her child. She is safe now.”
He said his goodbyes not long after that, not wanting to intrude further on Marianne, leaving Elinor sitting alone, her chest full of deep compassion for all his long hidden grief, and a brand new, profound respect for the quiet, honorable man who had carried so much sorrow, and still acted only out of kindness for the people he loved.

Elinor repeated every word of Willoughby’s unflinching confession to Marianne almost as soon as they were alone, but the effect on her younger sister was not the softening, gradual healing Elinor had dared to hope for. Marianne did not doubt a single detail of the tale. She listened steady and still, no objections, no defensive excuses for Willoughby, no attempt to spin his actions gentle, only silent, streaming tears that spoke clearer than any words that she knew his guilt was undeniable.

That quiet acquiescence assured Elinor Marianne fully understood the truth of his character. She watched, relieved, when Marianne stopped darting to avoid Colonel Brandon when he called, when she spoke to him of her own accord, her tone soft with a compassionate respect for all he had suffered at Willoughby’s hands. Marianne’s sharp, angry restlessness faded, but it did not leave her less wretched. Her mind settled, only to sink into a heavy, unbroken gloom. She grieved far more deeply for the ruin of Willoughby’s character than she ever had for the loss of his heart: his cruel seduction and abandonment of Eliza Williams, the misery he had heaped on that innocent girl, the cold doubt of what his original intentions might have been towards her, all preyed on her spirits so thoroughly she could not bring herself to speak of her pain even to Elinor. Brooding over her sorrows in total silence, she inflicted far more pain on her sister than any loud, unreserved confession of her feelings ever could.

To write of Mrs. Dashwood’s reaction to Elinor’s letter would only be to repeat the exact hurt and anger her daughters had already felt. Her disappointment was nearly as sharp as Marianne’s, and her indignation at Willoughby’s cruelty even fiercer than Elinor’s. Page after page of urgent, loving letters arrived from Barton, one after another, full of all her grief and anxious solicitude for Marianne, begging her to bear up under the misfortune with fortitude. Elinor knew how terrible Marianne’s suffering must be, for their soft, tender-hearted mother to ever speak of fortitude instead of wrapping her youngest daughter in endless hugs.

Against her own deep desire to gather both her girls close at Barton, Mrs. Dashwood decided it would be far better for Marianne to stay away from Devonshire for a little longer. Every path, every cottage, every view at Barton would be stained with the memory of Willoughby, every spot Marianne had once walked with him rising up to stab her anew. She begged her daughters not to cut their visit to Mrs. Jennings short: the stay, never formally fixed, was already expected to stretch at least five or six more weeks. The bustle of London, new amusements, new faces, all the diversions they could never find at their quiet cottage, might, she hoped, occasionally distract Marianne from her own grief, even pull a flicker of small, unplanned amusement from her, no matter how fiercely Marianne might now spurn any thought of joy.

In London, too, Mrs. Dashwood knew Marianne was far less likely to ever stumble across Willoughby by chance. Every friend of theirs would cut his acquaintance entirely now, and there was no risk of him appearing unannounced. In the crowded, sprawling city, even random chance was far less likely to throw them together than in tiny, isolated Barton, where he was all but certain to visit Allenham with his new bride after the wedding. She had one last reason to urge them to stay: a letter from her son John had informed her he and his wife Fanny would arrive in London before the middle of February, and she thought it only right the sisters spend some time with their brother while they could.

Marianne promised to abide by her mother’s wishes, and submitted to the extended stay without a single complaint, even though it was the last thing she wanted. She was convinced it was a terrible, mistaken choice: staying in London denied her the only comfort that could ever ease her pain, the warm, unfettered sympathy of her mother, and trapped her in a whirl of noise and company that would never let her grieve in peace. Still, it was a small consolation to her that what felt like a curse for her might be a blessing for Elinor. For her part, Elinor suspected she would not be able to entirely avoid running into Edward Ferrars while they remained in the city, but she comforted herself that even that small risk to her own happiness was worth it to spare Marianne the agony of returning to Barton too soon.

Elinor’s efforts to guard Marianne from ever hearing Willoughby’s name spoken aloud paid off perfectly. Without even realizing it, Marianne reaped all the benefit: Mrs. Jennings, Sir John, even talkative Mrs. Palmer never breathed his name in front of her. But Elinor had no such luxury. Day after day, she was forced to listen to their loud, unbridled outrage at his behaviour.

Sir John could scarce believe it. “A man I always thought the world of! The best-natured fellow, the boldest rider in all of England! The whole affair’s unaccountable nonsense! I wish the devil would take him whole. I’ll never speak a word to him again, not if we’re stuck side by side waiting for a hunt for two whole hours. A scoundrel, a deceitful dog! The last time we met he offered me one of Folly’s prize puppies, and this is how he repays us all!”

Mrs. Palmer was equally furious in her own way. “I’m dropping his acquaintance entirely immediately, and I’m so thankful I never properly got to know him. I only wish Combe Magna wasn’t so close to Cleveland, but it’s no matter—it’s far too far to ever visit, and I hate him so much I’ll never say his name again. I’ll tell every single person I meet exactly how good-for-nothing he is.” She poured all her remaining sympathy into chasing down every tiny trivial detail of Willoughby’s upcoming wedding, and relaying it all to Elinor: which coachmaker was building his and Miss Grey’s new carriage, what painter had been commissioned to do his portrait, and which London warehouse had supplied all Miss Grey’s wedding finery.

Lady Middleton’s calm, polite unconcern was a rare, blessed relief to Elinor, worn thin by the clamorous, overbearing kindness of everyone else in their circle. It was such a comfort to know there was at least one person who would greet her without prying for details, or fussing over Marianne’s health. Elinor had been so worn down by forced, officious condolences lately that she found herself valuing good, quiet breeding far more than loud, unthinking good nature. Lady Middleton only spoke her mind on the matter once or twice a day, saying “It is very shocking, indeed!” before turning her full attention back to planning her own parties. She soon decided that as soon as Willoughby married and his new wife was a woman of fortune and fashion, she would call and leave her card, much to Sir John’s disapproval.

Colonel Brandon’s gentle, unobtrusive enquiries after Marianne were never anything but welcome to Elinor. He had more than earned the right to speak openly with her of her sister’s disappointment, through all the quiet, steady zeal he had poured in to ease Marianne’s pain. The greatest reward for the painful effort he had made to share his own old sorrows and Willoughby’s humiliating secrets was the soft pitying glance Marianne sometimes turned his way, the gentle lilt in her voice whenever she worked up the will to speak to him. Those small moments assured Elinor that Marianne’s goodwill towards him was growing, and gave her great hope it would deepen into something far warmer in time. But Mrs. Jennings, who knew nothing of the private understanding between Elinor and the Colonel, only saw that he remained as grave as ever, and could not be persuaded to propose to Marianne no matter how much she hinted. Within two days she decided they would not marry till Michaelmas instead of Midsummer, and by the end of the week she was convinced the match would never happen at all. The easy, confident friendship between Elinor and the Colonel soon had her joking that the Mulberry tree, the canal and the yew arbour back at her country estate would all be for Elinor and the Colonel, not Marianne, and she had long since stopped thinking of Lucy Steele and Edward Ferrars entirely.

Early in February, less than a fortnight after Elinor received Willoughby’s final cold letter, she was forced to break the news to Marianne that he was officially married. She had made sure the news came to her directly, before Marianne could spot it printed in the public papers she pored over every morning. Marianne heard the news with a forced, resolute composure, said nothing, shed no tears at first. But soon the tears burst out, and she spent the rest of the day in a state just as pitiable as when she first learned of his engagement to Miss Grey. The newlyweds left town immediately after the ceremony for their honeymoon, and Elinor, certain there was no longer any risk of Marianne running into them, set about coaxing her sister to take small, gentle walks out of the house for the first time since the news of Willoughby’s betrayal first broke.

It was just at this time that the two Miss Steeles, fresh off their arrival at their cousin’s lodgings in Holborn, turned up to pay a call on their grand relations the Middletons at their Conduit and Berkeley Street homes, and were greeted with enthusiastic warmth by everyone but Elinor, who could not bear the sight of them. Their presence only gave her anxiety, and she struggled to summon a polite, gracious response to Lucy’s unmistakeable glee at finding her still in town.
“I should have been so disappointed if I’d turned up and you’d already left,” Lucy repeated, emphasizing every word far too pointedly. “But I always knew you’d stay. I was almost certain you wouldn’t leave London so soon, even though you told me back at Barton you’d only be staying a month. I knew you’d change your mind when it came to it—it would have been such a waste to leave before your brother and sister arrived! Now of course you won’t be in any hurry to go. I’m so glad you didn’t keep to your word.”
Elinor understood her sly dig perfectly, and had to draw on every scrap of her self-command to pretend she did not.
“And how was your journey, my dears?” Mrs. Jennings asked.
“Not in the common stagecoach, I promise you,” Nancy Steele answered, bright and proud. “We came post the whole way, with the very smartest gentleman to attend us. Dr. Davies was coming up to town, so we joined him in his post-chaise, and he behaved so genteelly he paid ten or twelve shillings more than we did!”
“Oh, oh!” Mrs. Jennings cackled. “Very pretty indeed, and the Doctor’s a single man, I’ll wager!”
“Now everyone laughs at me about the Doctor,” Nancy simpered, feigning embarrassment. “I can’t think why. My cousins all say I’ve made a conquest, but I declare I never think of him from one hour to the next. The other day my cousin saw him crossing the street to call on us, and cried ‘Lord, Nancy, here’s your beau!’ My beau, indeed! I said. The Doctor is no beau of mine at all.”
“Aye, aye, that’s very pretty talk, but it won’t fool me,” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “The Doctor’s your man, I see it clear.”
“No, truly!” Nancy insisted, earnest as anything. “I beg you’ll contradict that story if you ever hear anyone repeat it.”
Mrs. Jennings promised she would do no such thing, and Nancy was perfectly happy.
“I suppose you’ll be going to stay with your brother and sister while they’re in town, Miss Dashwood?” Lucy prodded, returning to her line of pointed questions.
“No, I do not think we will,” Elinor replied.
“Oh yes, I’m sure you will,” Lucy smiled, not letting up.
Elinor did not bother to humour her with any further argument.
“How wonderful of Mrs. Dashwood to spare you both for so long a time away!”
“So long a time?” Mrs. Jennings cut in. “Why, their visit’s barely even begun!”
Lucy fell quiet for a moment.
“I’m so sorry we won’t get to see your sister,” Nancy said. “I’m sorry she’s unwell—” Marianne had slipped out of the parlour the moment they arrived.
“That is very kind of you. My sister will be equally sorry to miss seeing you, but she has been suffering from terrible nervous headaches lately that make her unfit for any company or conversation.”
“Oh dear, that’s a great pity! But we’re such old friends, Lucy and me! I’m sure she wouldn’t mind seeing us, and we wouldn’t say a single word to disturb her.”
Elinor politely but firmly declined the offer, explaining Marianne was likely resting on her bed, or still in her dressing gown, and could not receive visitors.
“Oh, if that’s all,” Nancy cried, jumping up, “we can just pop up to her room and see her anyway!”
Elinor’s temper snapped, and she was just about to scold her for her impertinence when Lucy cut in, sharply reprimanding her sister for her rudeness. It was a sharp, unkind rebuke that did nothing to soften Lucy’s own ill manners, but for once, it saved Elinor the trouble of dealing with Nancy’s foolish overfamiliarity.

After days of quiet resistance, Marianne finally yielded to Elinor’s gentle, repeated pleas, and consented to step out with her sister and Mrs. Jennings for a single half hour one bright London morning. She laid out strict terms first: no paying calls, no lingering over small talk, they would only stop at Gray’s jewelers on Sackville Street, where Elinor was sorting through a set of their mother’s old-fashioned family pieces to adjust and refit them.

As they pulled up to the shop door, Mrs. Jennings suddenly remembered she owed a short social visit to a friend at the far end of the street, and as she had no business of her own at Gray’s, it was settled that she would slip away to make her call and return to collect the girls once their errand was done.

Climbing the stairs to the showroom, the Miss Dashwoods found the counters crowded with customers, and not a single clerk free to take their order. They were forced to wait, choosing a spot at the far end of one counter where only one very fashionably dressed gentleman stood lingering. Elinor held a quiet hope that he might hurry through his business out of courtesy to the two ladies waiting, but his eye for finery and his obsession with getting exactly what he wanted far outstripped any impulse to politeness. He was in the process of commissioning a custom toothpick case for himself, and for a full quarter of an hour he fussed over every ivory, gold, and pearl design in the shop, debating every last detail of size, shape, and ornament until he had mapped out the exact piece his own fancy had dreamed up. For the whole wait, he spared the sisters nothing more than three or four broad, unapologetic stares — a show of attention that branded him on Elinor’s memory as a man of pure, sterling insignificance, wrapped up in the very height of empty, fashionable vanity. Marianne, however, escaped all irritation at his rude gawking and his silly, childish devotion to such a trivial trinket: she was so deep in her own private, grieving thoughts, she was as oblivious to the world around her as if she was alone in her bedroom at Barton Cottage.

At last the order was settled. Every detail of the toothpick case had been approved, the gentleman named the latest possible date he could bear to go without the finished piece, drew on his gloves with lazy, deliberate care, tossed one more arrogant glance at the Miss Dashwoods that seemed to demand their admiration rather than offer any courtesy, and sauntered off with an air of unshakable self-conceit thinly masked by pretended indifference.

Elinor wasted no time stepping up to the counter to explain her own business, and was just on the cusp of wrapping up the negotiation when a second man stepped up beside her. She lifted her eyes to his face, and gasped softly in surprise to find it was her own brother, John Dashwood. Their pleasure at seeing one another was warm and genuine enough to make for a very sweet, respectable reunion in the middle of Gray’s crowded shop. John was genuinely glad to run into his sisters, and his inquiries after their mother were respectful and thoughtful. He explained he and Fanny had been in London for two days already.

“I fully meant to call on you yesterday,” he said, “but it was impossible — we had to take Harry to Exeter Exchange to see the wild beasts, and spent the rest of the day with Mrs. Ferrars. Harry was utterly delighted. This morning I swore I’d find half an hour to drop by, but the first days in town are always so crowded with errands. I’ve come here to order Fanny a new wax seal. But tomorrow I absolutely will call on you in Berkeley Street, and be introduced to your friend Mrs. Jennings. I hear she’s a woman of very considerable fortune. And you must introduce me to the Middletons, too — as relations of my stepmother, I shall be delighted to show them every proper respect. They’ve been excellent neighbors to you in the country, I understand?”

“More than excellent,” Elinor assured him. “Their care for our comfort, their endless kindness, is more than I can ever properly thank them for.”

“I’m overjoyed to hear it, truly I am,” he nodded, satisfied. “And it’s only right, isn’t it? They’re people of great wealth, related to you through your father’s first family, so every civility that could make your life easier was only to be expected. Edward told us the loveliest stories of your little cottage, said it was the coziest, most perfect little place of its kind he’d ever seen, and that all of you seemed happier there than anywhere else. It was a great comfort to us to hear it, I assure you.”

Elinor felt a faint flush of embarrassment, and was relieved to be spared the need to reply when Mrs. Jennings’ servant appeared at the shop door to say his mistress was waiting for them by the carriage. John walked them all down the stairs, was formally introduced to Mrs. Jennings by the carriage step, repeated his promise to call on them the next day, and took his leave.

He arrived promptly the next day, bearing an apologetic excuse from Fanny, who could not join them: she was far too busy attending to her own mother’s affairs to step out, he said. Mrs. Jennings waved off all formality at once, insisting they were practically cousins, and she would certainly call on Mrs. John Dashwood very soon, bringing the Dashwood sisters with her. John’s manners to Elinor and Marianne were calm but perfectly kind; to Mrs. Jennings, they were unfailingly civil and attentive, and when Colonel Brandon stepped through the front door shortly after he did, John eyed the older man with such sharp curiosity it was obvious he was mentally tallying up his fortune, ready to match his civility to whatever sum he found.

After half an hour of chat, he asked Elinor to walk with him to Conduit Street, to introduce him properly to Sir John and Lady Middleton. The day was uncommonly fine, so she agreed readily. The moment they stepped out of the house, his questions spilled out.

“Who exactly is Colonel Brandon? Is he a man of good fortune?”

“Yes,” Elinor said. “He has a very fine estate in Dorsetshire, bringing in a very steady income.”

“I’m delighted to hear it! He seems the very picture of a gentleman, and Elinor — I must congratulate you on the prospect of a very respectable, comfortable establishment for life.”

“Me? Brother, whatever do you mean?”

“He likes you! I watched him very closely, and I’m entirely convinced of it. What did you say his total income was?”

“About two thousand a year, I believe.”

“Two thousand a year!” John’s face lit up with a performative, over-the-top air of generous delight. “Elinor, with all my heart I wish it was twice as much, for your sake.”

“Thank you, I truly believe you mean that,” Elinor replied, “but I am completely certain Colonel Brandon has not the slightest wish to marry me.”

“You’re mistaken, Elinor, very much mistaken. A very small amount of gentle encouragement on your part would secure him at once. He might be wavering right now, of course — the smallness of your fortune might make him hesitate, his friends might all advise against the match. But those little, soft attentions that ladies know so well how to give will fix him, no matter what reservations he has. There’s no reason at all you shouldn’t try. I don’t imagine you have any lingering prior attachment — in short, you know that kind of old, ill-fated infatuation is completely out of the question, the obstacles are insurmountable, and you have more than enough sense to see that. Colonel Brandon is the perfect match for you, and I will make every effort to be as civil to him as I can, to make him feel welcome to our family. It’s a match everyone will be overjoyed by. In fact —” he dropped his voice to an important, excited whisper “— it will be exceedingly welcome to all parties.” He paused, correcting himself quickly. “That is, your friends are all so anxious to see you well settled — Fanny most of all, she has your best interests entirely at heart, I promise you. And Mrs. Ferrars, Fanny’s mother, is such a kind woman, she said just the other day how happy that match would make her.”

Elinor refused to dignify that with a reply.

“It would be a most remarkable, droll little coincidence, wouldn’t it,” he went on, “if Fanny’s brother and my own sister were to settle and marry at exactly the same time. And it’s not even all that unlikely!”

“Is Mr. Edward Ferrars,” Elinor asked, steady and deliberate, “soon to be married?”

“It’s not formally settled yet, but it’s all well in hand. His mother is the most generous woman alive — Mrs. Ferrars has promised to settle a full thousand a year on him, if the match goes through. The lady is the Honourable Miss Morton, only daughter of the late Lord Morton, with thirty thousand pounds of her own. It’s a perfect, desirable connection for both sides, and I have no doubt at all it will happen soon. A thousand a year is no small sum for a mother to give away permanently, but Mrs. Ferrars has a most noble, generous spirit. Just think — the other day, the very moment we arrived in town, knowing we were sure to be a little short on funds after moving into Norland, she pressed two hundred pounds worth of bank notes straight into Fanny’s hands. It was such a welcome gift, for living in town is extraordinarily expensive for us right now.”

He paused, waiting for her agreement and sympathy, and Elinor forced herself to say, “Your expenses both in town and at Norland must be very considerable, but your income is a very large one, after all.”

“Not nearly as large as people assume,” he sighed. “I don’t mean to complain, of course — it’s a very comfortable income, and I hope it will grow better with time. But the enclosure of Norland Common that’s going on right now has been a massive drain on our funds. And then I made a small purchase earlier this year — East Kingham Farm, you must remember the place where old Gibson used to live. The land was so perfectly situated right next to our own property, I felt it was my duty to buy it, I couldn’t in good conscience let it fall into someone else’s hands. Convenience always costs a price, and that farm cost me a very great deal of money.”

“More than it was intrinsically worth, I expect,” Elinor said.

“Oh, I hope not! I could have turned around and sold it the very next day for more than I paid. But when it came to the purchase money, I was very nearly very unlucky — the stocks were so low at the time, if I hadn’t happened to have the full sum sitting with my banker already, I would have had to sell out my shares and take a terrible loss.”

Elinor could only smile faintly. “We had other great, unavoidable expenses right after moving into Norland, too,” he continued. Our respected father, as you well know, left all the remaining Stanhope household goods at Norland — and they were very valuable — to your mother. Far be it from me to complain about that, he had every right to dispose of his property as he chose. But because of that, we had to replace almost all the linen, the china, every last household item he gave away. You can imagine, after all these costs, how far we are from being well off, and how grateful we were for Mrs. Ferrars’s kindness.”

“Of course,” Elinor said. “With her generous support, I hope you will soon be comfortably well situated.”

“Another year or two will see to a great deal of that,” he said gravely. “But there is still so much more to do. We haven’t even laid a single stone for Fanny’s new greenhouse yet, and all we have of the new flower garden is a marked out plan.”

“Where is the greenhouse to be built?”

“On the knoll behind the house. We cut down all the old walnut trees there to make space for it. It will be a stunning feature to look at from all across the park, and the new flower garden will slope down right in front of it, so it will be exceedingly pretty. We cleared away all the ugly old thorn bushes that used to grow all over the brow of that hill.”

Elinor kept her dismay and her quiet censure to herself, and was very thankful Marianne was not there to share the provocation of hearing all their old Norland family trees cut down for a frivolous new garden.

Now that he had made very clear just how “strapped” he was for cash, and neatly eliminated any chance of him offering to buy his sisters a pair of ear-rings the next time he visited Gray’s, his mood brightened, and he began gushing over how lucky Elinor was to have a friend like Mrs. Jennings.

“She seems an incredibly valuable woman! Her house, her whole way of living, makes it obvious she has a very large fortune. This acquaintance has already been so useful to you, and I’m sure in the end it will prove even more materially advantageous. Her inviting you up to town was such a great favour to you, and she is so openly fond of you, it’s almost certain you and Marianne will not be forgotten in her will. She must have a great deal of money left to leave.”

“I should think not very much at all,” Elinor said. “All she has is her jointure, which is bound to go to her daughters.”

“But no woman of reasonable prudence spends every penny of her income, now, does she?” he argued. “Whatever she saves up over the years, she can dispose of however she likes.”

“Surely it’s far more likely she’ll leave those savings to her own daughters, rather than to us?”

“Her two daughters are already very comfortably married, there’s no need to leave them anything more. Whereas, in my opinion, she has treated you with such extraordinary kindness, she has given you a sort of claim on her future generosity that no conscientious woman could ignore. Her behaviour to you has been perfectly warm, and she can’t possibly be that kind without being aware of the hopes it raises.”

“But neither Marianne nor I hold any such hopes. Brother, your concern for our well being is making you see things that are not there.”

“Of course, well, people have very little power over who gets what, at the end of the day,” he said, as if recalling himself. “But my dear Elinor — what is the matter with Marianne? She looks very unwell, her colour is all gone, and she’s grown so thin. Is she ill?”

“She’s not well. She’s had a nervous complaint plaguing her for several weeks now.”

“I am sorry to hear that. At her age, any sort of lingering illness ruins a girl’s bloom forever! Hers was such a short one, too. Just last September she was the prettiest girl I ever saw, the kind that was sure to catch a gentleman’s eye. Fanny used to say, you know, that Marianne would marry sooner and better than you. Not that she isn’t very fond of you, of course, that’s just what she thought. But she’ll be wrong now. I doubt Marianne will ever marry a man with more than five or six hundred a year, at the very most, but I am very sure you will do far better. Dorsetshire! I don’t know much of Dorsetshire, but my dear Elinor, I will be so delighted to come visit you there, and I can promise Fanny and I will be the first and happiest of your guests.”

Elinor tried very seriously to convince him there was no chance at all of her marrying Colonel Brandon, but the fantasy was far too pleasing to John for him to let it go. He was already fully determined to cultivate a close friendship with the Colonel, and push the match forward with every show of attention. He had just enough leftover guilt for never doing a single thing to help his sisters himself, that he was desperate for everyone else to do the work for him — an offer from Colonel Brandon, or a nice legacy from Mrs. Jennings, was the easiest possible way to make up for his own neglect.

They were lucky enough to find Lady Middleton at home, and Sir John walked in before their visit was over. Courtesies were exchanged all around, Sir John was ready to like anyone, and even though John Dashwood knew next to nothing about horses, he quickly decided he was a very good natured fellow. Lady Middleton, meanwhile, saw more than enough fashionable polish in his appearance to decide his acquaintance was well worth having. John left the Middletons’ home absolutely delighted with both of them.

“I shall have the most charming report to carry back to Fanny,” he told Elinor as they walked home. “Lady Middleton is the very picture of elegant refinement! Fanny will be so glad to make her acquaintance. And Mrs. Jennings, too, she’s so perfectly well behaved, even if she is not quite as elegant as her daughter. Fanny had some small scruples about visiting her, you know, and very naturally — we only knew her as the widow of a man who made all his money in very low trade, and Fanny and Mrs. Ferrars were both fully convinced neither she nor her daughters were the sort of women Fanny would care to associate with. But now I can bring home a perfectly satisfactory account of both of them.”

Mrs. John Dashwood was so fully convinced of her husband’s sound judgment that she called on both Mrs. Jennings and Lady Middleton the very next day, and her confidence was richly rewarded. Even Mrs. Jennings – the lively older woman whose home housed Elinor and Marianne – proved far from beneath her notice, and as for Lady Middleton? Fanny declared her one of the most charming women alive.

The fondness was entirely mutual. Both women shared a core of cold, self-serving selfishness that drew them to each other like magnets, and they found perfect kinship in their bland, unyielding dedication to polite performance, and their mutual lack of curiosity for anything that fell outside the narrow bounds of their own small social circles. The stiff, distant reserve that made Fanny such a hit with Lady Middleton did not endear her to Mrs. Jennings, however. To the older woman, Fanny was nothing but a proud, chilly creature with no warm words for her husband’s sisters, who sat silent for at least seven and a half of the 15 minutes she spent in their Berkeley Street lodgings.

Elinor burned to know if Edward Ferrars had arrived in town, but she would never bring herself to ask Fanny outright – and Fanny would never have volunteered the information, not until she could proudly announce Edward’s engagement to the wealthy Miss Morton was finalized, or until her husband’s schemes to secure favors and money from Colonel Brandon came to fruition. She was convinced Elinor and Edward still pined hopelessly for each other, so every chance to separate them, in word or deed, felt like a necessary safeguard.

But the news Fanny would not share soon came from another quarter. Lucy Steele turned up not long after, all melodramatic distress, to beg Elinor’s sympathy: Edward had arrived in London with the Dashwoods, but he dared not sneak to Bartlett’s Buildings to see her, for fear their secret engagement would be discovered. The pair were wild to meet, but for now, letters were the only comfort they could share. Edward confirmed he was in town himself, twice calling on their Berkeley Street lodgings while the rest of the party was out on their morning walks, leaving his calling card on the dining table each time. Elinor was glad he had thought to stop by, but far more relieved that she had missed him.

The Dashwoods were so enraptured by the Middletons that, despite their habitual reluctance to spend a single unnecessary penny, they decided to host a grand dinner for them. Their acquaintance barely begun, they issued the invitation to their newly leased, very fine house on Harley Street, which they had taken for three months. Elinor, Marianne, and Mrs. Jennings were all invited too, and John Dashwood made a point of extending a warm welcome to Colonel Brandon, who was always eager to be wherever the Miss Dashwoods were, and accepted the invitation with far more pleasure than surprise. Mrs. Ferrars, Edward’s formidable mother, would be in attendance – though no one could tell Elinor if Edward or his brother Robert would be joining the party. The chance to finally meet the matriarch was enough to spark Elinor’s full curiosity: she no longer felt the sharp, panicky anxiety that would have plagued her months earlier at the thought of making the woman’s acquaintance, but her interest in seeing what Mrs. Ferrars was really like had never faded.

That quiet interest quickly curdled when she learned the Miss Steeles had also been added to the guest list. The two sisters had flattered Lady Middleton so relentlessly that even though Lucy lacked elegance and her sister Anne was downright unrefined, the Middletons had invited them to spend two weeks at their Conduit Street home – and the pair had timed their visit to start just a few days before the Dashwoods’ dinner. Their connection as nieces to the man who had cared for Fanny’s brother for years would never have earned them a spot at her table on their own, but as Lady Middleton’s guests, they could not be turned away. Lucy, who had long dreamed of getting close to Edward’s family to gauge their opinions and find a way to win their favor, could barely contain her joy when she received her invitation.

For Elinor, the news could not have been more different. She immediately assumed Edward, who was staying under his mother’s roof, would of course be invited to his own sister’s dinner – and the thought of seeing him for the first time after all their tangled history, face to face with Lucy standing beside him, felt almost unbearable. Those fears were unfounded, however, and it was Lucy herself who dispelled them, smug and triumphant, when she told Elinor that Edward would definitely not be at the Harley Street dinner. She even tried to twist the knife further, claiming he was staying away because his affection for her was so strong he could not hide it if they were in the same room.

The fated Tuesday arrived, and Lucy clutched Elinor’s arm as they climbed the stairs to the dining rooms together, the whole party trailing the servant in one unbroken line. “Pity me, dear Miss Dashwood!” Lucy whispered, her voice trembling for dramatic effect. “You are the only person here who can understand. I can barely stand. Good heavens – in a moment I will meet the woman who holds every bit of my future happiness in her hands, the woman who will one day be my mother!” Elinor almost teased her that they might be about to meet Miss Morton’s future mother-in-law, not hers, but she chose instead to assure Lucy sincerely that she did pity her – a response that left Lucy utterly bewildered, as she had fully expected Elinor to be overcome with unquenchable envy.

Mrs. Ferrars was a small, thin woman, ramrod straight to the point of stiffness, her face sharp and sour to the point of being openly unkind. Her complexion was sallow, her small features plain and lifeless, saved from total insipidity only by a permanent furrow between her brows that screamed of unyielding pride and ill temper. She was not a woman of many words, matching her speech exactly to the small number of ideas that occupied her mind, and not a single syllable she spoke was directed at Elinor. She eyed the older Dashwood sister with the clear, premeditated intent to dislike her no matter what happened.

Elinor could not be made unhappy by that treatment now. A few months prior, it would have broken her heart – but Mrs. Ferrars no longer had the power to distress her. The obvious difference between the matriarch’s coldness to her, and the warm favor she and Fanny lavished on Lucy, only made Elinor smile, even as it amused her. She could not help but find the whole scene ridiculous: the two women fawning over Lucy, the very person they would most want to humiliate in the world if they knew half of what Elinor did, while Elinor – who held no power to hurt them at all – was deliberately snubbed at every turn. She watched the Miss Steeles simper and flatter to hold onto that misplaced favor, and found herself thoroughly despising all four of them for their petty, foolish spite. Lucy was glowing from the special attention she received, and Anne Steele would have been perfectly happy if everyone spent the rest of the night teasing her about her beloved Dr. Davies.

The dinner was grand, the servants numerous, every detail arranged to showcase Fanny’s love of display and John’s ability to pay for it. No trace lingered of the supposed poverty John liked to complain about, even as he poured thousands into “improving” the Norland estate and had once come within a thousand pounds of being forced to sell his shares at a devastating loss. The only poverty on display was in the conversation, which was remarkably thin. John Dashwood had nothing worth saying, Fanny even less, and most of their guests shared the same flaw: almost everyone in attendance lacked either natural wit, polished grace, good humor, or basic warmth. When the ladies retired to the drawing room after dinner, that lack of substance was impossible to ignore. The men had at least livened the discussion with politics, enclosure laws, and horse training, but once they left, the ladies had no topic to occupy them until coffee was served beyond comparing the heights of two nearly same-aged little boys: Harry Dashwood, Fanny and John’s son, and Lady Middleton’s second son William. Since only Harry was present, no measurement could settle the argument, and everyone repeated their own firm, unproven opinion over and over, as if sheer repetition would make it true. The two mothers politely insisted each other’s son was the taller, the two grandmothers fiercely defended their own grandson’s height, Lucy claimed both boys were remarkably tall for their age with no difference at all, and Anne Steele switched sides every other sentence to compliment each mother in turn. Elinor once stated she thought William was taller, a comment that offended Fanny and Mrs. Ferrars so deeply she refused to add another word on the subject. Marianne, when called on for her opinion, offended everyone by saying she had no opinion at all, having never once thought about the matter.

Shortly after the gentlemen joined the ladies in the drawing room, John Dashwood spotted the beautifully painted pair of screens Elinor had made for Fanny back at Norland, newly framed and hung to decorate the room. He grabbed them eagerly, holding them out for Colonel Brandon to admire. “These are all my eldest sister’s work!” he said. “As a man of taste, I know you will love them. I do not know if you have ever seen her art before, but everyone agrees she paints exceptionally well.” The Colonel, who cared nothing for pretense of being an art connoisseur, praised the screens as effusively as he would have praised any piece of work Elinor had touched, and soon the whole room was crowding around to look. Mrs. Ferrars, who had no idea who had painted them, asked to see them, and Fanny handed them over, casually mentioning they were Elinor’s work.

“hmph. Very pretty,” Mrs. Ferrars said, barely glancing at them as she shoved them back to her daughter. Fanny flushed a little, for a moment thinking her mother had been unnecessarily rude. “They are very pretty, ma’am, are they not?” she said – then immediately panicked that she had been too kind to Elinor, and added, “Don’t you think the style is exactly like Miss Morton’s painting? She paints so delightfully – that last landscape of hers was absolutely stunning!”

“Beautiful indeed,” Mrs. Ferrars agreed. “Miss Morton does everything perfectly.”

Marianne could not hold her tongue. She had already been furious at the way Mrs. Ferrars had treated Elinor all night, and this petty, deliberate dig at her sister to praise a stranger they did not even know made her snap. “That is the strangest sort of admiration I have ever heard! What do we have to do with Miss Morton? Who knows her, who cares for her? We are talking about Elinor!” She snatched the screens out of Fanny’s hands to admire them the way they deserved to be admired.

Mrs. Ferrars drew herself up, face thunderous. “Miss Morton is Lord Morton’s daughter,” she said, her voice sharp with rebuke. Fanny was seething, and John Dashwood was white with panic at his younger sister’s audacity. Elinor was far more upset by Marianne’s outburst than she had been by Mrs. Ferrars’ rudeness – but Colonel Brandon’s eyes were fixed on Marianne, and all he saw was her loyal, tender heart, the fierce sisterly love that would not stand to see Elinor snubbed even in the smallest way.

Marianne’s emotions would not be contained. The cold cruelty of Mrs. Ferrars’ behavior toward Elinor all evening filled her with a premonition of all the heartbreak and pain Elinor might face at this woman’s hands, the same kind of wounded grief she herself had endured at Willoughby’s hands. Driven by that rush of affectionate feeling, she crossed to Elinor’s chair, wrapped her arms around her sister’s neck, pressed her cheek to Elinor’s, and whispered, “Dearest, dear Elinor – do not mind them. Do not let them make you unhappy.” She could not say another word, her spirits overwhelmed, and she buried her face in Elinor’s shoulder and burst into tears.

Every eye in the room turned to them, and almost everyone was concerned. Colonel Brandon was on his feet and moving toward them before he even realized what he was doing. Mrs. Jennings clucked sympathetically and pressed her smelling salts into Marianne’s hand, and Sir John flew into such a rage at Mrs. Ferrars for upsetting the girl that he immediately crossed the room to sit next to Lucy Steele and whispered the whole shocking story to her, complaining loudly about the old harridan’s behavior.

Within a few minutes, Marianne had composed herself enough to end the fuss and return to her seat, though her mood remained subdued for the rest of the night. “Poor Marianne,” John Dashwood whispered quietly to Colonel Brandon, as soon as he could get his attention. “She has never had as strong a constitution as her sister, she is very high-strung and nervous. And you have to pity a young woman who was once a great beauty, losing her looks like this. You would not guess it now, I expect, but Marianne was remarkably handsome just a few months ago – every bit as pretty as Elinor. Now you see how overset she gets.”

Elinor’s quiet, burning curiosity to meet the formidable Mrs. Ferrars had finally been sated, and the encounter left her with no small measure of relief. She had found in Edward’s mother every unlovely trait imaginable that could make a union between their families nothing short of a nightmare: brutal unyielding pride, petty, grasping meanness, and a fixed, irrational prejudice against Elinor that would have poisoned every step of a courtship between herself and Edward, had he been a free man. As she turned the visit over in her mind, she could clearly see every barrier that would have tangled their engagement, delayed their marriage, and made their lives miserable for years after. She found herself almost grateful, for her own sake, that the far greater, unassailable obstacle of Edward’s secret bond to Lucy Steele spared her ever having to grovel for Mrs. Ferrars’ approval, spared her from being at the mercy of the woman’s random, cruel whims. Even if she could not bring herself to rejoice at Edward being tied to Lucy, she had to admit that if Lucy were a kinder, more deserving girl, she ought to count that unexpected barrier a blessing.

She could only marvel that Lucy was so giddily, blindly elevated by the faint scraps of civility Mrs. Ferrars had shown her. Lucy’s vanity and desperate hunger for status had so thoroughly clouded her judgment that she mistook the politeness granted her only because she was not Elinor, not the woman Mrs. Ferrars and her daughter Fanny loathed, as genuine, personal favour. She thought that shallow preference was proof they adored her, all because no one knew the truth of her secret engagement. And that delusion was not just written all over Lucy’s face during the visit—it spilled over the next morning, when she badgered Lady Middleton into dropping her off in Berkeley Street, on the off chance she might find Elinor alone to gush over her newfound good fortune.

Luck was on her side: no sooner had she stepped through the door than a messenger arrived summoning Mrs. Jennings to attend Mrs. Palmer, leaving the two young women completely unobserved.
“My dearest friend!” Lucy cried, the second the last footstep faded down the hall, grabbing Elinor’s hands in hers. “I simply had to come tell you how perfectly happy I am. Could anything in the world be more flattering than the way Mrs. Ferrars treated me yesterday? She was so impossibly affable! You know how terrified I was at the thought of meeting her, but the very moment they introduced us, she acted as if she’d taken an immediate, wholehearted fancy to me. Wasn’t that so? You were there, you saw it all—weren’t you completely struck by how warm she was?”
“She was certainly very civil to you,” Elinor allowed gently.
“Civil? That’s not half of it! I saw far more than mere civility. That was kindness no one else got at that party, no one but me! No pride, no icy hauteur, and Fanny Dashwood was exactly the same—all sweetness, all soft good humour!”
Elinor tried to steer the conversation elsewhere, but Lucy would not be deterred, pressing her again and again to admit that her happiness was well founded, until Elinor had no choice but to respond.
“Undoubtedly, if they knew of your engagement,” she said, “nothing could be more flattering than the way they welcomed you. But as matters stand, they are entirely unaware—”
“I knew you would say that!” Lucy cut in quickly. “But there is no reason on earth for Mrs. Ferrars to pretend to like me if she did not, and her liking me is everything. You cannot talk me out of my satisfaction. I am certain everything will end perfectly well, no of the terrible hurdles I used to fear. Mrs. Ferrars is a charming woman, and so is Fanny Dashwood! They are both perfectly delightful. I cannot believe I never heard you say before how agreeable your sister by marriage is!”
Elinor had no reply to that, and did not even attempt to make one.
“Are you unwell, Miss Dashwood?” Lucy pressed, tilting her head. “You look so low, so quiet—surely you are not ill?”
“I was never in better health,” Elinor said, forcing a small smile.
“I am so glad to hear it! I should be heartbroken if you fell ill, you who have been my greatest comfort in all the world. Heaven knows what I should ever have done without your friendship.”
Elinor struggled to make a civil response, doubting she succeeded, but it was enough to satisfy Lucy, who prattled on immediately.
“I know you truly care for me. Next to Edward’s love, your regard is the greatest comfort I have. Poor Edward! But the best of this is, we will get to meet constantly now. Lady Middleton adores Fanny Dashwood, so we will be visiting Harley Street all the time, and Edward spends half his days at his sister’s house. Mrs. Ferrars and Fanny both said more than once yesterday that they will always be delighted to see me. They are such wonderful women! If you ever mention me to your sister, you cannot praise her highly enough, you simply cannot.”
Elinor had no intention of doing any such thing, and made no promise to do so. Lucy continued, carried away by her own good fortune.
“I would have known in an instant if Mrs. Ferrars disliked me. If she had only given me a stiff, formal curtsy, never spoken a word to me, never looked at me with a hint of warmth—you know exactly the sort of forbidding treatment I mean—I would have given up all hope then and there. I could never have endured it. I know how violent her dislikes can be, once they take root.”
Elinor never got the chance to reply to that giddy, unthinking boast. The drawing room door was thrown open, the servant announced Mr. Ferrars, and Edward stepped across the threshold.
It was an excruciating, awkward moment. All three faces went bright with embarrassment, every one of them looking foolish and flustered. Edward looked half-ready to turn right around and walk back out the door instead of coming further in. The exact situation all three had dreaded more than anything had come to pass: they were all three together, with not a single other person present to soften the tension.
The two women recovered first. Lucy knew better than to draw attention to herself, to risk revealing their secret engagement, so she only looked at him with soft, demure tenderness, spoke a single, brief greeting, and fell entirely silent. Elinor, for Edward’s sake as much as her own, forced herself to pull herself together, pushing past the sting of old hurt to greet him with a manner almost easy, almost open. Another small, brave effort smoothed away the last of the awkwardness. She would not let Lucy’s presence, nor the quiet knowledge of all Edward had cost her, stop her from telling him how glad she was to see him, how sorry she had been to be out of the house the last time he had called at Berkeley Street. She would not let the sharp, observant gaze of Lucy, fixed firmly on every move she made, stop her from showing him the gentle attentions due an old, dear friend, almost family. Her warmth calmed Edward, and he found the courage to sit down—though his embarrassment still far outstripped theirs. Lucy had no stake in the tension, and Elinor had long since steeled her heart for pain, but Edward’s torn, conflicted heart could not find the same ease.
Lucy said nothing at all, determined not to make the situation any easier for the other two, so almost every word that was spoken had to come from Elinor. She volunteered all the small, obvious details Edward should have thought to ask for: news of Mrs. Dashwood’s health, the comfort of their lodgings in town, the slow progress of Marianne’s recovery. She did not stop there, either: soon after, she made a heroically selfless decision, announcing that she would go fetch Marianne to join them, giving the two secret fiancés a few minutes alone. She did it with perfect grace, loitering on the landing for several long minutes, steeling herself to the quiet hurt of it, before she went to find her sister.
Her small sacrifice was cut short almost immediately. Marianne, wild with joy at the news Edward was in the house, came bounding into the drawing room the very second Elinor opened her bedroom door. Her pleasure at seeing him was as loud, as unfiltered, as every other feeling she had ever known. She pressed his hand in hers, her voice thick with sisterly affection.
“Dear Edward!” she cried. “This is such a happy moment! It almost makes up for every last bit of misery we have endured lately.”
Edward tried to return her warmth as it deserved, but with Lucy sitting right there, silent and watchful, he dared not say half of what he truly felt. They all sat down again, and for a long heavy moment, no one spoke. Marianne’s soft, tender gaze bounced between Elinor and Edward again and again, frustrated that Lucy’s unwelcome, uninvited presence was putting a damper on the joy they should all be sharing. Edward was the first to break the silence, commenting on Marianne’s paler looks, expressing his fear that London did not agree with her.
“Oh, never mind me!” Marianne replied, earnest and spirited even as tears welled up in her eyes. “Do not worry about my health. Elinor is well, you see. That is enough for both of us.”
The remark did nothing to ease Edward or Elinor’s discomfort, and it did not endear Marianne to Lucy, who looked up at her with a sharp, unkind glare.
“Do you enjoy London?” Edward asked, grasping for any new, safe topic to discuss.
“Not at all. I came here expecting so much joy, but I have found none. The only comfort this place has ever afforded me is seeing you, Edward. Thank heaven, you are exactly the same as you always were.”
She paused, and no one spoke.
“Elinor,” she added after a moment, “we must ask Edward to escort us back to Barton when we leave, in a week or two, I think. I am sure he will not mind taking charge of our journey home.”
Poor Edward mumbled some indistinct reply that none of them, not even he himself, could make sense of. Marianne mistook his agitation for shy delight at the thought of more time with Elinor, and perfectly satisfied, she turned to other topics.
“We had such a dreadful day at Harley Street yesterday, Edward! So dull, so wretchedly dull! But I have so much to tell you about it later, things I cannot say right now.” She held her tongue about how much she had hated his mother and Fanny Dashwood, wisely saving that rant for when they could speak privately. “But why were you not there? Why did you not come see us?”
“I was otherwise engaged,” Edward said.
“Engaged? But what trivial engagement could possibly matter more than being with us, your dearest friends?”
“Perhaps, Miss Marianne,” Lucy cut in sharply, eager to get a little revenge for Marianne’s obvious favouritism, “you imagine young men never bother keeping their engagements, no matter how small, if they do not feel like honouring them.”
Elinor burned with anger, but Marianne did not even feel the barb.
“Not at all,” she said, calmly earnest. “I am very sure Edward’s conscience is the most delicate, the most scrupulous I have ever known. He will never break a promise, no matter how small, no matter how much it goes against his own interest or pleasure. He would never hurt anyone’s feelings, never act selfishly for even a moment. That is the truth, Edward, and I will say it out loud. Why should you never get to hear how wonderful you are? If you will not accept my praise, then you are no friend of mine—anyone who wants my love and esteem must put up with me saying so out loud.”
Her glowing, unreserved praise landed horribly for two-thirds of the room. Lucy resented every word, and Edward was so overwhelmed by embarrassment, he jumped straight to his feet to leave.
“Going so soon?” Marianne protested. “Dear Edward, you cannot go yet!” She pulled him a little to one side, whispering that Lucy could not possibly stay much longer, that he had only to wait a few minutes and they would be free to talk. But even that encouragement was not enough. Edward was determined to leave, and no one could persuade him to stay. Lucy, who would happily have lingered for two more hours if he had, left the house not long after he did.
“The audacity of her, coming here so often!” Marianne huffed, the second the front door clicked shut behind Lucy. “Could she not see we wanted her gone? How cruel of her, to torment Edward like that!”
“Why cruel?” Elinor said softly. “We are all his friends, and Lucy has known him longer than any of us. It is only natural he should enjoy seeing her just as much as he enjoys seeing us.”
Marianne stared at her long and steadily.
“Elinor, that is the sort of evasive, false talk I cannot bear. If you are saying that only to make me contradict you, then you ought to remember I am the last person in the world to play that game. I will not trick myself into saying things I do not truly mean, just to humour you.” She turned and left the room before Elinor could say another word.
Elinor did not dare follow her. She was still bound by her solemn promise to Lucy to keep their engagement secret, so she could say nothing that would shatter Marianne’s happy, mistaken illusion that Edward was still free, still devoted to Elinor. Painful as it was to let her sister remain in the dark, she had no choice but to bear it. All she could hope was that Edward would not keep putting all three of them through the agony of such awkward, half-spoken meetings again soon—and there was every reason to believe that shared misery would be enough to keep them far apart for a long time to come.

Within days of their last chance encounter with Willoughby, the London newspapers carried a small, triumphant announcement: Thomas Palmer, Esq.’s wife had safely given birth to a son and heir, a tidbit that was only truly thrilling to the handful of close connections who had known of the coming birth for weeks.

The event sent Mrs. Jennings into peals of delighted excitement, reshaping her whole schedule at once. Eager to dote on Charlotte and the new baby every waking hour, she left for the Palmers’ house first thing each morning after dressing, and rarely returned home until well after dark. At the Middletons’ earnest request, Elinor and Marianne found themselves spending every full day at their Conduit Street townhouse as well. Left to their own devices, the sisters would have far rather lingered in the quiet of Mrs. Jennings’ empty home, at least for the mornings—but it was impossible to object to the combined wishes of their hosts.

Their company was scarcely more welcome to Lady Middleton and the Steele sisters than the arrangement was welcome to them. Elinor and Marianne had far too much good sense to fawn over Lady Middleton’s every trivial whim or coo over her spoilt children, so Lady Middleton decided they must be ill-natured; their habit of curling up with books for quiet reading struck her as vaguely satirical, a trait she disliked without ever bothering to define it. Lucy and her sister Anne viewed the Dashwoods as interlopers, trespassing on the ground they had worked so hard to claim as their own, stealing the favour of the Middletons they meant to keep all to themselves.
Their presence was a constant irritation: Lady Middleton felt ashamed to laze about doing nothing in front of the sharp-eyed Dashwood girls, and Lucy dared not lay on her usual thick, cloying flattery for fear Elinor and Marianne would see through it and despise her for it. Only Anne Steele saw a path to turn the situation to her favour. If either Dashwood girl would just spill every last, salacious detail of Marianne’s heartbreak over Mr. Willoughby, she would happily give up her favourite spot by the after-dinner fire to make it worth their while. But the sisters never indulged her: every pointed, pitying remark Anne tossed Elinor’s way about “poor broken Marianne” only got a blank look in response, and every careless jab about how flighty beaux could be earned Marianne’s full, unmasked disgust. Even a far smaller favour would have won her over—all she wanted was for them to laugh with her about the silly doctor who was rumoured to be courting her, but they never did, leaving her to make all her own jokes on the topic on the days Sir John was out at dinner.

None of this quiet jealousy was visible to the ever-cheerful Mrs. Jennings, who insisted the girls getting to spend so much time together was a perfect delight, and teased the Dashwoods every night for getting a break from her “stupid old woman” company. She would pop in to join the group sometimes at the Middletons, sometimes back at her own house, brimming over with stories of Charlotte’s recovery, convinced the new mother’s good health was entirely down to her own careful tending. The only thing that soured her mood was Mr. Palmer’s stubborn refusal to admit his son was the most beautiful baby who had ever lived. No matter how many times she pointed out the baby’s exact resemblance to every relation on both sides of the family, he only shrugged and said all infants looked identical to him, a very unfatherly opinion that she complained of daily.

It was around this time that poor Mrs. John Dashwood suffered her own small, ridiculous misfortune. She was paying a call with Elinor and Marianne at her Harley Street home when a mutual acquaintance dropped by, and on hearing the two Miss Dashwoods introduced as Mr. John Dashwood’s sisters, the woman immediately assumed the girls were permanent guests in Harley Street. Within two days, she sent out musical party invitations addressed to Elinor and Marianne alongside John and Fanny Dashwood’s, a misstep that threw Fanny into a quiet fury. She was now forced to go to the enormous inconvenience of sending her carriage to collect her sisters-in-law and escort them to the party, and worse still, had to pretend to treat them with public kindness—who was to say they would not now expect to be invited out with her again? She could always snub them later, of course, but that was little comfort: when you are determined to act unkindly, nothing stings worse than other people expecting you to be generous.

Marianne by this point had grown so used to going out every evening to fill the empty, aching hours after Willoughby’s rejection that she no longer cared where she was taken. She would dress quietly and mechanically for every engagement, never expecting even a scrap of amusement, and often did not even ask where the night’s event was until the carriage pulled up at the door. She cared so little for her appearance these days that she spent less than half the time adjusting her hair and gown than Anne Steele did staring at her, the moment she was done dressing. Anne missed nothing: she memorised the cost of every ribbon, the number of gowns Marianne owned, and made constant, nosy attempts to find out how much Marianne spent on laundry every week, and what her annual allowance was. Every one of these rude little inspections ended with the same aggravating compliment: “Upon my word, you look vastly smart tonight, I do declare you’ll make a dozen new conquests!”

That very evening, after enduring that exact remark, the sisters climbed into John Dashwood’s carriage five minutes after it arrived at the door, a punctuality that left Fanny fuming—she had reached the party first, and been hoping they would be late enough to inconvenience her coachman, or give her an excuse not to save them seats.

The musical party itself was unremarkable, like all parties of its kind: full of a small handful of guests who actually loved music, and a far larger crowd who could not tell a pianoforte from a hurdy-gurdy, while the performers all considered themselves the finest amateur musicians in the whole of England. Elinor, who had no taste for performative displays of skill, did not bother to pretend to care about the grand piano, and let her eyes wander freely around the room. It was then she spotted a familiar face: the young man who had lectured them at length about fancy toothpick cases at Gray’s the stationer’s shop, months before. He soon caught her staring, said a few words to John Dashwood, and the two of them walked over to her, as John introduced him as Mr. Robert Ferrars.

He bowed so preeningly, twisting his head at an absurd angle, that Elinor knew at once he was exactly the vain, silly coxcomb Lucy had described him to be. How lucky it would have been, she thought ruefully, if her love for Edward Ferrars depended on the merits of his family! His mother’s sharp temper and his sister’s cruelty would have done half the work of turning her off him, and this brother’s ridiculous vanity would have finished it entirely. But Robert’s empty conceit did nothing to dim her affection for the quiet, gentle Edward at all, as he proved over the next quarter hour of conversation. He lamented Edward’s awkwardness, his inability to mix in high society, and insisted it was all their mother’s fault for sending him to a private tutor as a boy instead of Westminster School like he had been. “It’s all irremediable now, of course,” he said, puffing out his chest. “I tell my mother that all the time. If she’d only ignored my uncle Sir Robert’s bad advice and sent Edward to public school, he’d be as polished a man as I am today.”

He then prattled on, learning they lived in Devonshire, expressing shock that anyone could live there without being near Dawlish, and gushing endlessly about how much he adored small cottages. He told her he had once thrown three expensive architect’s plans straight into the fire for his friend Lord Courtland, and told him to build a cottage instead, and had rearranged an entire cottage for a dance for Lady Elliott once, fitting eighteen couples in the dining room, card tables in the drawing room, tea in the library, and supper in the small saloon. Elinor agreed with every word he said; he was too ridiculous to deserve the effort of a rational argument.

On the ride home, John Dashwood, who cared as little for music as Elinor did, had a sudden thought inspired by the evening. Remembering how Mrs. Dennison had mistaken his sisters for his permanent guests, he suggested that they invite Elinor and Marianne to stay in Harley Street for a few days while Mrs. Jennings was busy tending to Charlotte. The cost would be negligible, the inconvenience almost nothing—and it would finally lay his father’s deathbed promise to rest, and clear his conscience entirely.

Fanny was horrified. “I can’t possibly do that without offending Lady Middleton,” she said quickly. “The girls are her guests every single day. Otherwise I would be only too happy to have them, you know I am—look how I took them out this very evening! But they’re Lady Middleton’s visitors, I can’t very well steal them away.”

John Dashwood meekly pointed out the sisters had already spent a full week at Conduit Street, and Lady Middleton would hardly begrudge them a few days with their own brother.

Fanny paused, then hit on the perfect excuse. “My love, I would ask them at once, truly I would, but I just this very day decided to invite the Miss Steeles to stay with us for a few days. They are such well-behaved, sweet girls, and their uncle did so very much to help Edward get his start in life. We owe them that kindness. The Dashwood girls can come visit us next year, you know, but the Steeles might leave town at any moment. You already like them, don’t you? My mother adores them, and little Harry is completely besotted with them.”

John Dashwood was fully convinced. He agreed the Steeles needed to be invited right away, and his conscience was soothed by the plan to invite his own sisters the following year—though he secretly suspected that by next year, Elinor would be married to Colonel Brandon, and Marianne would be visiting her sister at the Colonel’s estate, making the invitation entirely unnecessary.

Fanny, delighted to have escaped the burden of hosting her sisters-in-law with such a clever little lie, wrote to Lucy first thing the next morning, inviting her and Anne to stay in Harley Street as soon as Lady Middleton could spare them. The note made Lucy giddy with joy: it felt as if Fanny herself was now championing her secret engagement to Edward, clearing the path straight to the Ferrars family’s good graces. Such a chance to be under the same roof as Edward and win over his mother and sister was the greatest possible advantage she could have hoped for. She at once declared that their visit to the Middletons, which had never had a set end date, was always meant to wrap up in exactly two days’ time.

When Elinor saw Fanny’s note, she realised for the first time that Lucy’s carefully laid plans might actually succeed. Such unheard-of kindness, extended to her after so short an acquaintance, could only mean Fanny’s favour was shifting to Lucy entirely, driven by far more than petty spite towards the Dashwoods. Lucy’s flattery had already softened Lady Middleton’s cold pride, and wormed its way into Fanny’s narrow, selfish heart—and there was no telling what greater favours she would win next.

Sir John called on the Steeles at Harley Street a few days later, and came home full of wild stories of how beloved they were. Fanny Dashwood swore she had never met two more charming young women in her life. She had given each of them a hand-sewn needle book made by a displaced French emigrant, called Lucy by her first name, and announced that she did not think she could ever bear to let them leave.

Two full weeks had barely slipped by before Charlotte Palmer was well enough that her mother, Mrs. Jennings, no longer felt the need to spend every waking hour at her son-in-law’s house. Content to drop by once or twice a day to coo over the new baby and fuss over Charlotte’s rest, she returned at last to her own home on Berkeley Street and her familiar, comfortable routines – much to the quiet delight of Elinor and Marianne Dashwood, who were glad to slip back into their easy, shared days with her.

It was on the third or fourth morning after this quiet return to normal that Mrs. Jennings came trundling back from her daily visit to the Palmers, her bonnet askew, her cheeks flushed with breathless excitement, and an air of bustling importance that could only mean she’d stumbled on the most extraordinary piece of gossip Elinor had ever heard. She barely paused to hang her pelisse by the door before she cried out, “Lord, my dear Miss Dashwood! Have you heard the news at all?”

“No ma’am,” Elinor said, looking up from her embroidery. “What has happened?”

“It’s so strange I can scarce credit it myself! Wait till I tell you every last bit. When I got to Mr. Palmer’s today, Charlotte was in a perfect fuss over the baby – certain he was deathly ill, he’d cried for an hour straight, and was covered head to toe in tiny pimples. I looked at him straight away and said ‘Lord, my dear, that’s nothing in the world but red gum,’ and the nurse said the very same. But Charlotte wouldn’t be satisfied, so they sent for Mr. Donavan the apothecary. Luckily he was just stopping by from Harley Street, so he popped right in, took one look at the baby, and confirmed we were all right – no harm done at all. The minute he was turning to leave, though, I don’t know what possessed me, but I asked him if there was any other news about. He smirked, and simpered, and suddenly looked all serious, like he was holding a great secret, and at last he leaned in and whispered, ‘Lest any unpleasant report about your young ladies’ acquaintance reach you unawares, I thought it best to say there is no great cause for alarm – Mrs. Dashwood is expected to do perfectly well.’”

“What?” Elinor sat bolt upright. “Is Fanny Dashwood ill?”

“That’s exactly what I said!” Mrs. Jennings exclaimed. “‘Lord, is Mrs. Dashwood ill?’ I cried, and that’s how it all came pouring out. The long and short of it is this: that Mr. Edward Ferrars, the young man I used to tease you about so often – and thank heavens nothing ever came of that, now it turns out – has been secretly engaged to my cousin Lucy for more than a twelvemonth! There now! Can you believe it? No one on earth knew a syllable of it but her sister Nancy! It’s not so odd they fell for each other, but to carry on for a whole year without a single soul suspecting? That’s the strangest part of all. I never once caught them together, else I’d have spotted it in a second.

They kept it all quiet, you see, for fear of Edward’s mother Mrs. Ferrars, who’s set on him making a grand, wealthy match. Not a word of it reached your brother or sister, not a hint, until this very morning. Poor Nancy, bless her, she’s good-hearted but as daft as a brush, and she thought to herself ‘Everyone is so fond of Lucy, they’ll never object to the match,’ so she trots straight over to Fanny, who was sitting alone at her carpet-work, not suspecting a thing – why, only five minutes before, she’d been telling your brother she planned to marry Edward off to some lord’s daughter or other, I forget her name. You can imagine what a blow it was to her pride! She fell into violent hysterics at once, screaming loud enough for your brother to hear all the way down in his dressing room, where he was planning a letter to his steward. He flew up the stairs, and what a terrible scene followed. Lucy turned up at the house that very minute, all unsuspecting, and Fanny flew at her like a fury, scolding her so harshly the poor girl fainted dead away. Nancy dropped to her knees sobbing, your brother paced the room in a panic, Fanny swore they’d not stay another minute under her roof, and your brother had to go down on his own knees to beg her let the girls stay long enough to pack their trunks. Then Fanny had another fit of hysterics, he was so frightened he sent for Mr. Donavan, and that’s when he walked in on a whole house in an uproar. The carriage was already at the door to take my poor cousins away, and they were just stepping in when he left – he says Lucy was so distraught she could barely walk, and Nancy was little better. I declare I have no patience for Fanny Dashwood! I hope with all my heart the two of them marry in spite of all her fuss. Lord, how wretched poor Edward will be when he hears of this! To have the woman he loves treated so scornfully! Everyone says he’s monstrous fond of her, and Mr. Donavan agrees.
