He’s gone back to Harley Street now to be on hand when they break the news to Mrs. Ferrars, who was sent for the second the girls left the house. She’ll no doubt have hysterics too, and I don’t pity her one bit. I can’t stand all this nonsense about money and grand connections. There’s no reason at all Edward and Lucy shouldn’t marry! Mrs. Ferrars can easily afford to settle enough on him, and Lucy, bless her, knows better than anyone how to stretch a penny. I swear, if she gave them five hundred a year, Lucy would make them look as smart as any couple on eight hundred a year. They could live so snug in a little cottage just like yours in Barton, maybe a touch bigger, with two maids and two menservants – why, I can even get them a housemaid, my Betty’s sister is out of work and she’d be perfect for them.”

When Mrs. Jennings finally paused for breath, Elinor had had time to steady her racing heart. She was relieved to see no one suspected the sharp, quiet ache under her skin – Mrs. Jennings had long stopped imagining Elinor was attached to Edward, and best of all, Marianne was out on a walk, so there was no need to hide her distress in front of her sister just yet. She answered calmly, as impartial as she could be, commenting on the absurdity of the scene the apothecary had described, while her mind whirled. She could barely tell what she truly expected of the situation, save that it would almost certainly end with Edward keeping his promise and marrying Lucy. She felt deep compassion for Edward, very little for Lucy even when she tried to summon it, and not an ounce of sympathy for Fanny or Mrs. Ferrars.

She knew immediately she must tell Marianne the truth at once, before anyone else brought the subject up to her unawares. It was a painful, heavy task: she would have to shatter what she knew Marianne still believed, that Edward might somehow one day be free for her, and risk making Marianne relive all the sharp agony of Willoughby’s betrayal, thinking the two men’s situations were the same. But there was no putting it off, so she went to find her sister at once.

She spoke gently, plainly, no dramatic flourishes, no dwelling on her own pain – only mentioning the months of quiet self-command she had practiced since Lucy first told her the secret, as an example Marianne might follow. But Marianne broke down the second she understood, crying so hard she could barely breathe, horrified that Elinor had carried this secret for four whole months, all while she was tending to Marianne through the worst of her grief for Willoughby, while Marianne had even snapped at her for seeming calm and happy.

“Four months?” Marianne gasped, clutching her hands. “You knew all that time? And you never said a word? While I moaned and wailed and made a whole household fuss over my broken heart, you carried this all alone?”

“I promised Lucy I would not tell a soul,” Elinor said softly. “I could not break that trust. And I could not bear to make you and Mama unhappy for no reason, when there was nothing any of you could do to help.”

Marianne was utterly humbled. She begged Elinor’s forgiveness, sobbing for being so selfish, so wrapped up in her own sorrows she never saw her sister was suffering too. She promised Elinor anything she asked – never to speak of the engagement with bitterness, never to snub Lucy when they met, to greet Edward with perfect kindness if chance ever brought them face to face, no matter how sharp the pain was. And she kept that promise beautifully: when Mrs. Jennings chattered on and on about the news for the rest of the afternoon, Marianne sat perfectly still, her face steady, agreeing politely every time she was spoken to, even when the older woman gushed about how fond Edward was of Lucy, and the pain in her throat felt like it might choke her.

The next morning brought a new trial. Their brother John Dashwood arrived, his face grave and solemn, as if the world had nearly ended.

“I suppose you have all heard,” he said, sitting down carefully, “of the shocking discovery that took place under our roof yesterday.”

They all nodded, silent.

“Your poor sister Fanny has suffered dreadfully,” he went on. “And Mrs. Ferrars too – it has been a scene of most complicated distress. But I am hopeful we may all weather the storm. Poor Fanny was in hysterics all day yesterday, but Mr. Donavan says there is no real danger, her constitution is strong. She says she shall never think well of anyone again, and who can blame her, after showing those Steele girls nothing but kindness and hospitality, to be rewarded with such secret deception? She swears now she wishes she had invited you two to stay with us instead of them.”

He paused to accept their quiet thanks, then continued. “Mrs. Ferrars was even more distraught when Fanny broke the news to her. She had been planning such an excellent, eligible match for Edward, settling him comfortably with Miss Morton, who has thirty thousand pounds of her own – the thought that he was secretly engaged to a girl with no fortune at all never once crossed her mind. She sent for Edward at once, and I was there to help reason with him. We told him if he would only break the engagement and marry Miss Morton, his mother would settle the Norfolk estate on him, clear of all land tax, bringing in a full thousand a year – and she offered to raise it to twelve hundred a year, when he would not bend. When he still refused, she swore she would give him nothing at all but the two thousand pounds he already had of his own, she would never see him again, she would block every possible path to advancement in any profession he chose to enter.”

Marianne gasped, clapping her hands together in indignation. “Gracious God! Can such cruelty be real?”

“Your surprise is most natural, Marianne,” her brother said, nodding. “Who could imagine such stubbornness, to turn down such generosity?”

Marianne bit her lip, remembering her promise, and held her tongue.

“All of our arguments did nothing,” John continued. “Edward said very little, but what he said was unshakable. He would not break his engagement, no matter the cost.”

“Why, then he’s acted like a perfectly honest man!” Mrs. Jennings blurted out, unable to stay quiet a moment longer. “Begging your pardon, Mr. Dashwood, but if he’d done any different, I’d think him a right scoundrel. Lucy is my cousin, and there’s not a sweeter, more deserving girl walking this earth.”

John Dashwood was surprised, but too polite and too keen to keep on the good side of a woman with Mrs. Jennings’s fortune to take offense. “I would never speak ill of any relation of yours, ma’am. Lucy Steele is no doubt a very deserving young woman, but this connection is simply impossible. We all wish her very happy. Mrs. Ferrars’s behavior, in this situation, is only what any conscientious, wealthy mother would do. Edward has chosen his own path, and I fear it will be a very hard one.”

“Where has he gone, the poor young man?” Mrs. Jennings asked.

“I am sorry to say, there was a complete, permanent rupture between him and his mother. He left her house yesterday, and we do not know where he has gone in town, nor would we dream of making inquiries. Only two thousand pounds to live on – imagine how dreadful that prospect is, for a man born to expect thousands a year! If not for his own folly, he would have had two thousand five hundred a year within three months, married to Miss Morton. We all feel for him, but there is nothing we can do to help.”

“Poor dear boy!” Mrs. Jennings cried. “He is more than welcome to a bed and every meal at my house, any time he likes. He has no business wasting his money on tavern dinners and rented lodgings.”

Elinor’s heart ached so sharply for Edward she could barely breathe, and she smiled faintly at Mrs. Jennings’s kindness, grateful for it even as John Dashwood went on.

“His mother has already decided to settle that Norfolk estate on his younger brother Robert, instead of Edward,” he said. “I left her this morning with her lawyer, sorting out the paperwork. Can there be anything more galling for a man, than to see his own younger brother given the property that should have been his, all because he chose to be stubborn? Poor Edward. I truly feel for him.”

Mrs. Jennings could not stop singing Edward Ferrars’ praises for his honorable choice, but only Elinor and Marianne truly understood the full weight of his virtue. No one else knew how little reward there had ever been for choosing to disobey his family, how no comfort awaited him beyond the quiet, unshakable knowledge that he had done right, after he was cut off from all his fortune and cast out of his mother’s good graces. Elinor’s chest swelled with quiet pride at his unbreakable integrity; Marianne, soft with pity for the hardship he had borne, forgave every past slight he had seemed to inflict on her sister, at once.

Yet even as their full trust in each other was restored by the public reveal of Edward’s secret engagement, neither sister cared to dwell on the subject when they were alone. Elinor avoided it on principle: Marianne’s too-warm, too-certain insistence that Edward still carried a torch for her only rooted that forbidden hope deeper in her mind, when she was trying with all her might to let it fade. Marianne, for her part, soon lost the courage to speak of it at all. Every comparison it forced between Elinor’s steady, selfless conduct and her own wild, impulsive recklessness over Willoughby left her writhing in self-reproach. She felt every sting of that contrast, but not to spur her to better behavior now, as her sister had hoped: she only sank deeper into the torture of unending penitence, bitter that she had never exerted self-control before, with no hope of amending her ways now. Her spirit was so worn thin that present effort felt impossible, leaving her only more dispirited than before.

For two full days, no new news reached them of the goings-on in Harley Street or the Steeles’ lodgings on Bartlett’s Buildings. Though Mrs. Jennings already had more than enough juicy gossip to spread far and wide without seeking out more, she had resolved from the first moment of the reveal to pay her cousins a comforting, curious visit the second she could, and only the unexpected rush of extra callers had kept her away. The third day after they learned the full particulars was a bright, glorious Sunday in early March, warm enough to draw crowds out to Kensington Gardens even in the second week of March. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor joined the throng, but Marianne, who knew the Willoughbys had returned to town and lived in constant dread of encountering them, chose to stay safely home rather than brave such a public space.

An old close friend of Mrs. Jennings joined them almost as soon as they stepped through the garden gates, and Elinor was grateful for the distraction: the woman monopolized all of Mrs. Jennings’ attention, leaving Elinor free to wander and sink into quiet reflection. She spotted no sign of the Willoughbys, no sign of Edward, and for a long stretch no one at all who could spark her interest, grave or light. Until, to her quiet surprise, she was approached by Miss Anne Steele, who looked a little flustered but lit up at the sight of them. She gushed with unfeigned delight to meet them, and after Mrs. Jennings greeted her with especial warmth, Anne left her own party to linger with them for a little while.

Mrs. Jennings leaned in to whisper to Elinor at once: “Fish all the news out of her, my dear. She’ll tell you anything you ask, you see I can’t tear myself away from Mrs. Clarke right now.”
Luckily for both their curiosities, Anne would have spilled every secret unprompted anyway.
“I’m so glad I ran into you!” she chirped, looping her arm familiarly through Elinor’s. “You’re the very person I’ve been dying to see.” She dropped her voice to a stage whisper. “I suppose Mrs. Jennings has heard all about our little drama? Is she angry with us?”
“Not at all, I am sure,” Elinor assured her.
“Oh that’s such a relief! And Lady Middleton? She isn’t cross either?”
“I cannot imagine she would be.”
“Thank goodness, I’ve had such a dreadful time of it these past few days! I’ve never seen Lucy in such a fury. She swore at first she’d never sew me a new bonnet, never do a single thing for me for as long as she lived—but now she’s quite over it, and we’re thick as thieves again. Look, she made this little pink bow for my hat, and stuck the feather in it last night. I know you’re going to laugh at me for wearing pink ribbons, but why shouldn’t I? Everyone teases me that it’s the doctor’s favorite color, but I never would have known he liked it best if he hadn’t said it out loud! My cousins plague me about it nonstop, I declare sometimes I don’t know where to look.”
She wandered off that tangent for a minute, then quickly circled back to the news everyone was desperate to hear.
“Well, but Miss Dashwood, people can say whatever they like about Mr. Ferrars deciding he’d never marry Lucy, but that’s all nonsense, I can tell you! It’s a downright shame those ill-natured rumors are flying around. Whatever Lucy might have feared, no one else had any right to say for certain he’d back out.”
“I never heard any such rumor hinted at before, I promise you,” Elinor said.
“Oh no? Well it was being spread all over town, more than once! Miss Godby told Miss Sparks that no sensible person would ever expect Mr. Ferrars to give up a girl like Miss Morton with thirty thousand pounds, for Lucy Steele who has nothing at all. And my cousin Richard swore to me that when push came to shove, he was sure Mr. Ferrars would run. Then Edward didn’t come near our rooms for three whole days, I didn’t know what to think! Lucy was all ready to give up hope entirely—we left your brother’s house on Wednesday, and never saw hide nor hair of him Thursday, Friday, Saturday. She almost wrote to him, but then her pride wouldn’t let her. But this very morning, he turned up just as we got home from church, and he told us everything. His mother summoned him to Harley Street on Wednesday, yelled at him for hours, the whole family threatened to disown him, and he stood right there and swore he loved no one but Lucy, and he’d marry no one but her. He was so upset by the whole row he jumped on his horse right after he left his mother’s house and rode off into the country, hid out at some random inn all Thursday and Friday just to calm his head. He thought it all over, you see, and realized he had nothing but two thousand pounds to his name, no other income, and if he took orders like he’s been planning, the only post he’d get would be a tiny curacy that barely paid enough to feed one person. He thought it would be cruel to trap Lucy in a life of poverty, so he begged her, if she had the slightest wish to be free of the engagement, to end it right then and there, no hard feelings. But he never once said he was tired of her, or wanted to marry Miss Morton, or anything like that! I’d swear to it on my life. Of course Lucy wouldn’t hear a word of breaking it off. She told him straight, all soft and sweet, that she didn’t care about being poor, she’d happily live on nothing if she got to be his wife. Then they were so happy, they started planning everything: he’ll go get ordained as soon as he can, and they’ll wait to marry until he can secure a proper church living.”
Anne paused, and Elinor blinked. “Wait—you were in the room with them the whole time to hear all this?”
Anne snickered, as if Elinor had said something terribly silly. “La! Miss Dashwood, you don’t think people make love with other folks standing right there? For shame, you know better than that! No, no, they were shut all alone in the drawing room together. I just stood outside the door and listened to what I could hear.”
Elinor froze, horrified. “You listened at the door? I wish you’d told me that before, I never would have let you tell me details of a conversation you had no right to overhear. How could you be so unfair to your own sister?”
Anne waved the protest away. “Oh that’s nothing! Lucy would do the exact same to me. A year or two back, when Martha Sharpe and I had all sorts of secrets, she’d hide in closets or behind chimney boards all the time just to listen to what we were saying.”
Elinor tried to steer the conversation to another topic, but Anne could not stay away from the subject preying on her mind for more than a couple of minutes.
“Edward says he’s going up to Oxford soon,” she babbled. “Right now he’s lodging on Pall Mall. His mother is such a horrid ill-tempered woman, isn’t she? And your brother and sister-in-law weren’t very kind to them either! But I won’t say a word against them to you, and I will say they did send us home in their own carriage, which was more than I expected. I was half terrified they’d ask for those little sewing kits they gave us a few days before, but no one said a word, and I made sure to hide mine where no one would see it. My cousins are going to tease me something dreadful when they hear he’s going to be ordained, you mark my words. They’ll tell me I should write to the doctor to get Edward the curacy of his new living. But I’d never do that for all the world! I’ll just say ‘La! How could you ever think I’d bother the doctor with such a thing?’”
Before Elinor could reply, Anne’s friends the Richardsons came into view. “Oh, there they are! I had loads more to tell you, but I can’t stay away any longer. They’re such genteel people, he makes a fortune and they keep their own private coach. Tell Mrs. Jennings please that I’m so glad she isn’t angry with us, and Lady Middleton isn’t either! And if you and your sister ever leave town, and Mrs. Jennings is lonely, us Steeles would be more than happy to come stay with her as long as she likes. I don’t think Lady Middleton will invite us back again any time soon, though. Goodbye! I’m so sad Miss Marianne missed seeing me. Give her my love. Oh, you aren’t wearing your spotted muslin today! I’d have been scared it would tear walking all over the grass.”
She rushed off to greet the Richardsons, leaving Elinor to her thoughts. Almost nothing Anne had told her was new; every detail lined up exactly with what she had already guessed would happen. Edward and Lucy’s engagement was as firm as ever, their wedding date as uncertain as could be, hanging entirely on the off chance Edward would ever be gifted a proper church living that could support a wife.
When they climbed back into their carriage to head home, Mrs. Jennings begged for every scrap of news, but Elinor chose only to share the simple, public details Lucy would want spread, leaving out the unfortunate eavesdropping anecdote. She told her that the engagement was still fully on, and they were making plans for Edward to take orders as soon as possible.
“Wait for a living, indeed!” Mrs. Jennings huffed, shaking her head. “We all know how that goes! They’ll wait a year, no living will turn up, and they’ll end up living on fifty pounds a year from that tiny curacy, plus the interest on his two thousand pounds, plus whatever pennies Mr. Steele and Mr. Pratt can scrape together for Lucy. Then they’ll have a baby every year, and Lord help them, they’ll be so poor! I’m going to see what I can scrape together to help furnish their little cottage, that’s for sure. Two maids and two footmen, like I was joking about the other day? No no, they’ll be lucky to afford one hardworking maid-of-all-work.”
The next morning, a twopenny post letter arrived for Elinor, postmarked from Bartlett’s Buildings, in Lucy’s own neat handwriting:
> Bartlett’s Buildings, March
> My dearest Miss Dashwood,
> I hope you will excuse the liberty I take in writing to you, but I know your kindness to me will make you happy to hear such good news of me and my dear Edward, after all the dreadful troubles we have suffered these past weeks. Thank God, though we bore so much persecution, we are both well now, and as happy as two people deeply in love can be. We owe so much gratitude to our friends, none more than you, whose great kindness I will never forget, nor Edward, whom I have told every bit of it.
> I am sure you and dear Mrs. Jennings will be glad to hear that I spent two perfect happy hours with him yesterday afternoon. He would not hear a word of us parting, even when I begged him, as I thought was my duty for the sake of prudence, to set me free. I would have ended our engagement that very second if he had asked, but he swore it would never happen. He does not care a fig for his mother’s anger, as long as he has my love. Our prospects are not bright, it is true, but we must wait, and hope for the best. He will be ordained very shortly, and if it ever falls in your power to recommend him to anyone who has a living to bestow, I know you will not forget us. I trust dear Mrs. Jennings will speak a good word for us to Sir John, or Mr. Palmer, or any friend who might be able to help us.
> Poor Anne was very much to blame for blabbing our secret, but she meant well, so I say nothing of it. I hope Mrs. Jennings will not think it too much trouble to call on us any morning she is passing this way, it would be such a great kindness, and my cousins would be so proud to receive her.
> My paper is almost full, so I will end here, begging you to pass my most grateful, respectful regards to Mrs. Jennings, Sir John, Lady Middleton, and all the dear children the next time you see them, and give my love to Miss Marianne.
> I am, your most obliged and affectionate friend,
> Lucy Steele.

Elinor handed the letter straight to Mrs. Jennings, who read it aloud, gushing over every line.
“How prettily she writes! That bit where she offered to let him break the engagement? That’s just like Lucy, sweet sensible girl. Poor soul, I wish I could get him a good living tomorrow, with all my heart! She calls me ‘dear Mrs. Jennings’, did you see that? She’s the sweetest good-hearted girl that ever lived. I will absolutely go call on her, no question. So thoughtful of her to remember everyone! This is the prettiest letter I’ve ever read, it does her heart and head all the credit in the world.”

More than two months had slipped by since the Dashwood sisters first arrived in London, and Marianne’s longing to flee the smoky, crowded city sharpened with every passing hour. She pined for the sharp, sweet air of Devonshire lanes, for the quiet of Barton Cottage’s orchard, for the unhurried liberty of days that didn’t revolve around stiff drawing-room calls and hollow society small talk. She was certain no place on earth could soothe her still raw, unhealed grief half so well as their little home beside the sea.

Elinor was nearly as eager to leave, too — the only difference between them was that she had the good sense to anticipate how grueling a full day’s cross-country journey would be for Marianne in her fragile state, and refused to rush their departure recklessly. She had only just broached the subject of their return with their warm, generous hostess Mrs. Jennings, who protested the plan with every bit of affectionate eloquence she possessed, when a far better arrangement presented itself. The Palmers planned to relocate to their Somerset estate of Cleveland for the Easter holidays, and Charlotte had sent a gushingly enthusiastic note begging Mrs. Jennings and both the Dashwood sisters to come stay with them. Even the usually gruff, uninterested Mr. Palmer had added his own sincere welcome to the invitation, and his manners toward the sisters had softened considerably ever since he learned of Marianne’s heartbreak, so Elinor accepted at once, despite her initial hesitation to impose.

When she told Marianne the news, the girl’s reaction was far from hopeful. “Cleveland?” she cried, pale and agitated. “No — I cannot go there.”
“You forget,” Elinor soothed, “it is nowhere near the place you once thought you would move to —”
“But it is in Somersetshire,” Marianne gasped. “That is the county I dreamed I would settle in, with — no, Elinor, you cannot ask me to set foot there.”

Elinor did not argue that she ought to simply overcome those old, painful feelings. Instead she appealed to the love Marianne held dearest: their mother. This route, she explained, would make the rest of their journey home far softer and easier. Cleveland was only a few miles outside Bristol, and from there the ride to Barton would take a single long, leisurely day — their mother could even send their trusted old family servant to meet them at Cleveland, so they would not have to travel the full distance alone. They would not need to stay at Cleveland for more than a week, at most, and they could be home hugging their mother again in barely three weeks’ time. Marianne’s love for Mrs. Dashwood outstripped every lingering, imaginary sting of old heartbreak, and she soon agreed. She spent the rest of the night scribbling out an hour-by-hour countdown to the moment the doors of Barton Cottage would swing open for them.

Mrs. Jennings, who had grown far too fond of the girls to see them go, begged them to come back and stay with her in London after their visit to Cleveland, but Elinor gently declined. Once Mrs. Dashwood sent back her eager agreement to their travel plans, every last detail of their journey home was settled.

“Ah, Colonel!” Mrs. Jennings cried to Brandon, the very first time he called after their departure plans were finalized, “I don’t know what on earth you and I are going to do without the Miss Dashwoods! They’re determined to head straight home from the Palmers’ estate, and when I come back to London after Easter, the two of us will be sat here gawping at each other as dull as a pair of napping cats.” She hoped, of course, that this dramatic vision of their future boredom would push the quiet colonel to propose to Elinor, so they would all avoid that tedious fate — and it seemed her little scheme had worked. Elinor had stepped over to the window to measure a print she planned to copy for a friend, and Brandon followed her, his face soft with unspoken meaning, and talked to her there for several minutes.

Mrs. Jennings was far too honorable to eavesdrop openly, so she deliberately moved her chair right next to the piano forte where Marianne was playing, but she could not help but notice Elinor’s cheeks flush bright pink, that she grew visibly flustered, and that she was so focused on whatever Brandon was saying that she completely forgot to keep measuring her print. When Marianne paused between two pieces, Mrs. Jennings caught a snippet of Brandon’s low voice, where he was apologizing for how small and plain his house was at Delaford. That settled it for her — of course that was the proper etiquette for a man on the verge of proposing, to warn his future wife his home was not grand. She could see Elinor’s lips form the shape of words that said the size of the house did not matter at all, and she mentally cheered for her sensible, unpretentious friend.

A second lull in Marianne’s playing let her catch another line of Brandon’s calm, steady voice: “I am afraid it cannot take place very soon.”
Mrs. Jennings nearly jumped out of her skin. What a ridiculous, unromantic thing to say to a woman you are proposing to! “Good Lord, what could possibly stop it?” she thought to herself, holding back the exclamation. “Surely he doesn’t think he needs to wait to grow older before marrying?”

But Elinor did not look offended or upset in the slightest. When their conversation ended, and they stepped apart, Mrs. Jennings heard her say, soft and full of heartfelt sincerity, “I shall always think myself very much obliged to you.” Mrs. Jennings was delighted at her obvious happiness, and very confused when the colonel took his leave a minute later, perfectly calm and collected, no grand romantic gesture in sight, as if he had not just got engaged! She could not believe her old quiet friend made such an unenthusiastic suitor.

What they had actually discussed was nothing of the sort.
“I have heard,” Brandon said to Elinor, his voice full of gentle compassion, “of the terrible injustice Mr. Ferrars has suffered at the hands of his family. If I have the story right, he has been cast off entirely by his mother for refusing to break his engagement to a young woman who is perfectly worthy of him. Is that true?”
Elinor confirmed that it was.
“The cruelty of tearing apart two people who have loved each other for years is unforgivable,” he said, shaking his head. “Mrs. Ferrars has no idea what harm she might do, what desperate measures she might drive her son to. I met Mr. Ferrars a few times in Harley Street, and I like him very much. He is not the sort of man to open up quickly to strangers, but I have seen enough of him to wish him well for his own sake — and as a friend of yours, I wish him well far more. I hear he intends to take orders as a clergyman. Would you be so kind as to tell him that the rectory of Delaford, my living, arrived vacant in this morning’s post? It is his, if he will accept it. I wish it paid more than the late incumbent’s two hundred pounds a year — it is a small enough income, and while it can be improved, it will never make him rich. But it will keep him comfortable as a bachelor, and nothing would make me happier than to give it to him.”

Elinor was so shocked she could not speak for a full minute. Barely 48 hours earlier, she had thought Edward’s fate was utterly hopeless, that he would never have the means to make a life for himself — and now this good, kind man was handing him a lifeline, just out of the goodness of his heart. The rush of gratitude she felt was so strong it brought tears to her eyes. She thanked him over and over, sang Edward’s praises, and promised to deliver the message that very day — she knew Edward was still in town, and had his exact address from Miss Steele. She tried to convince Brandon to tell Edward the news himself, but he gently refused, saying he knew Edward would feel awkward accepting such a favor from a relative stranger, and would be far more comfortable hearing it from her.

That was when Brandon mentioned, apologetically, that his house at Delaford was small and plain — the line that had made Mrs. Jennings assume he was talking about a shared marital home. Elinor waved the concern away at once. “The size of the house will not inconvenience them at all, it will be perfectly suited to their small household and their income.”
Brandon blinked, surprised she assumed Edward would marry right away. “This little rectory can only keep him comfortable as a single man,” he explained. “It is nowhere near enough of an income to support a wife. My patronage does not extend any further than this, and I do not have enough influence to secure him more. If I ever am able to do more to help him in future, you may rest assured I will. But as things stand now, this barely brings him any closer to the happiness he wants most. His marriage must still be a long way off — I am afraid it cannot take place very soon.”

That was the line that Mrs. Jennings had so wildly misinterpreted. When the two of them parted, and Elinor told him she would always be deeply obliged to him, it was not because a marriage proposal had been accepted — it was because the quietest, most generous man she knew had just handed the man she loved more hope than she had dared to dream of for months.

The door had barely clicked shut behind Colonel Brandon before Mrs. Jennings rounded on Elinor, her round face alight with a knowing, playful smirk, like she’d just caught a secret no one else had thought to share.
“I won’t even pretend to ask what the Colonel just said to you, my dear,” she trilled, settling into the nearest armchair with a smug little bounce. “I tried to duck out of earshot, honest I did, but the bits I caught left no doubt what his business was. I could not be happier for you if I tried—every last inch of me wishes you all the joy in the world.”

Elinor’s chest felt soft and tight all at once, warmth spreading through her limbs that she’d not expected to feel so soon. “Thank you, ma’am. It is the most wonderful, surprising news. Colonel Brandon’s kindness is more than I ever dared hope for—there are so few men in the world with a heart so open, so quick to step in where others would turn away. I have not been so stunned in all my life.”
“Stunned? Pish, my love, I’m not the least bit shocked!” Mrs. Jennings waved a hand dismissively, her lace cap bobbing. “I’ve suspected for months he had it in him. No man looks at you the way he does and doesn’t end up acting on it, sooner or later.”
“You judged his character rightly, of course, you’ve known him for years,” Elinor said, a faint, sad little smile tugging at her mouth. “But no one could have foreseen an opportunity like this presenting itself quite so quickly.”
“Opportunity, bah!” Mrs. Jennings laughed. “When a man sets his heart on doing something good? He’ll carve out an opportunity faster than a needle through linen. Mark my words, soon you’ll be the happiest bride in all of Devonshire, and I’ll be trotting up to Delaford to visit the two of you before the autumn rains hit.”
“You intend to come calling at Delaford, then?” Elinor said, her smile growing a little more real.
“Of course I do! And don’t let the Colonel make out that the house is falling apart, either. I’ve seen it—great sprawling place, gardens full of roses, better than half the gentry’s homes in the shire. Why he’d claim it needed fixing I can’t imagine; if it’s broken, he’s the man rich enough to fix it, isn’t he?”

A servant popped in then to announce the carriage was waiting at the door, and Mrs. Jennings scrambled to her feet, already reaching for her bonnet. “Oh drat, I’ve not even told you half of what I meant to say! But we’ll hash the rest out tonight, all by our lonesomes, no callers to interrupt us. I won’t drag you out with me—your head’s far too full of this lovely news to care about paying visits, and besides, I know you’re dying to run find Marianne and tell her every last detail.”
Marianne had slipped out of the parlour before Colonel Brandon had even finished speaking, so Elinor nodded. “I will tell Marianne, certainly. But I would ask you not to mention it to anyone else just yet, if you please.”
Mrs. Jennings’ face fell a little. “Oh, very well—but I was planning to stop in and see Lucy Steele up in Holborn today, you know.”
“Not even Lucy, if you can keep it, ma’am. A day’s delay will do no harm. I must write to Mr. Ferrars first, to let him know what has happened, and until that is done, no one else ought to hear of it. There is no time to waste, not when he means to take his orders so soon.”

Mrs. Jennings blinked, thoroughly confused for a long moment, then her face lit up as a spark of understanding hit. “Oh! Oh, I see now! Mr. Ferrars is to officiate the ceremony, isn’t he? That’s a lovely touch, so thoughtful of you both! But my dear, isn’t that a touch odd? Shouldn’t the Colonel be the one writing to him, not you?”
Elinor hadn’t the faintest idea what she was on about, and didn’t care enough to ask, so she just replied to the last part. “Colonel Brandon is far too delicate, far too considerate of Edward’s feelings, to bring the news himself. He wanted it conveyed gently, by someone Edward already counts as a friend.”
“Ah, that’s very strange sort of delicacy if you ask me, but I won’t pester you,” Mrs. Jennings said, seeing Elinor reach for her pen and paper. “You know your own affairs best. Goodbye, my sweet—haven’t had news that made me this happy since my Charlotte had her first baby.”
She was halfway out the door when she popped back in, cheeks flushed. “Wait! I just thought of Betty’s sister—she’s an excellent housemaid, sews like an angel, I’d love to get her a place as your lady’s maid down at Delaford. You can think on it later, no rush.”

Elinor nodded, barely hearing a word. She was far too wrapped up in the letter to Edward, wondering how on earth to phrase the news—too much or too little, and she risked hurting his pride, or overstepping, when he was still bound to Lucy, when every kind word she wrote felt like a secret little ache in her own chest. She sat there, pen hovering over the page, staring at the blank paper, when the parlour door creaked open.
It was Edward.
He’d run straight into Mrs. Jennings on the steps of the house, on his way out to her carriage, and she’d practically shoved him inside, babbling that Elinor was waiting for him, that she had terribly important business to discuss, before he could protest. The two of them stood there staring at each other for a long, flustered moment—they hadn’t spoken a word since his engagement to Lucy had been dragged out into public, since he knew Elinor was fully aware of how his family had cast him off, left him penniless, with no way to support a wife. They both fumbled through their greetings, red-faced, awkward, Edward apologizing for intruding even though he’d been practically forced through the door.
“Mrs. Jennings said you wished to see me,” he said, sinking into a chair across from her, his hands twisted tight in his lap. “I would never have presumed to drop by unannounced, but I could not bear to leave London without seeing you and Marianne one last time. I leave for Oxford tomorrow, you see. It will likely be months before we cross paths again.”
“You would never have left without our well wishes, even if we could not give them to you in person,” Elinor said, steadying her voice, determined to get the news out as fast as she could to spare them both more awkwardness. “Mrs. Jennings was quite right. I do have something very happy to tell you—something Colonel Brandon charged me with, just ten minutes ago. He knows you intend to take orders, and he is delighted to offer you the living at Delaford, which has just fallen vacant. He only wishes the income was larger than the two hundred a year it brings in.”

Edward stared at her, completely stunned, his mouth hanging half open. All he could get out was, “Colonel Brandon?”
“Yes,” Elinor said, her courage growing as the first hard part was over. “It is his way of showing how deeply he sympathizes with all that your family has put you through lately—with that terrible unfair situation you were left in. All of us, Marianne, me, all your friends share that sympathy. It is also a tribute to how highly he thinks of your character, how much he admires the honourable way you have behaved through every hardship.”
“Colonel Brandon is giving me a living? That cannot possibly be real,” Edward whispered.
“Your own family has been so cruel to you, it is no wonder you struggle to believe anyone would be so kind.”
“No,” he said, his eyes locking on hers, his voice thick with emotion. “I never doubted *your* kindness. I know full well I owe this all to you. I cannot find the words to thank you—you know I was never any good at speeches.”
“Oh no, you are quite wrong,” Elinor hurried to say. “You owe this to no one but your own good character, and Colonel Brandon’s wisdom in seeing it. I had no hand in it, not at all. I did not even know the living was vacant, not five minutes before the Colonel walked in. He is a friend to our whole family, so it gives him extra joy to do this for someone we care for—but I never once asked him for a single thing on your behalf.”
She hesitated to admit she might have had a tiny, tiny influence, and that hesitation only made Edward more certain she was the one who had arranged it all. He sat quiet for a long minute, thinking, before he spoke again. “Colonel Brandon seems like a man of great worth. I have always heard nothing but good of him, and your brother John thinks the world of him. He is an honourable gentleman, no doubt.”
“You will grow to like him even more, once you get to know him,” Elinor said. “You will be very near neighbours, after all—the parsonage is barely a stone’s throw from the big house at Delaford.”
Edward did not answer. He looked away from her, his expression dark, quiet, almost sad, as if he was already dreading the thought of living so close to a man he was certain was his rival for Elinor’s heart.
“I know the Colonel lodges on St. James Street,” he said, standing up abruptly. Elinor told him the number. “I must go to him at once, to thank him. He has made me the happiest man alive.”

Elinor did not try to stop him. They parted, her voice soft as she told him she would always wish him every possible happiness, and he stammered out a half-formed reply that could not quite capture all the feeling he was holding back.
As the door clicked shut behind him, Elinor sank back into her chair, breathing out slow. When I see him again, she thought to herself, he will be Lucy’s husband. The thought ached, sharp and quiet, but there was no use fighting it.

When Mrs. Jennings came home later that afternoon, she was so bursting with curiosity about her secret that she barely even mentioned the new people she’d gone to call on. She pounced on Elinor the second she walked through the door.
“Well, my dear! I sent that young Mr. Ferrars up to you, didn’t I do a splendid job? You didn’t have any trouble popping the question to the Colonel, did you? He didn’t drag his feet, I hope?”
“Nothing of the sort happened, no,” Elinor said, biting back a laugh.
“Good! How soon will the wedding be? I thought you said he needs to get ordained first?”
Elinor blinked. “Oh, no, that’s for Edward. It takes two or three months, usually, to finish ordination, as far as I know.”
“Two or three months?” Mrs. Jennings squawked. “Good heavens, my dear! The Colonel would never wait that long! What a waste of time! Surely you could find someone else who’s already ordained to marry you lot, no need to hang about for poor Mr. Ferrars!”
“Ma’am,” Elinor said, bewildered, before the full force of the misunderstanding hit her. “Colonel Brandon is not marrying me. He is giving the living to Edward Ferrars, so that Edward will have an income to marry Lucy Steele.”

For a second Mrs. Jennings just stared, then she burst out laughing so hard she had to sit down, clutching her sides. She’d been completely under the wrong impression the whole time! Instead of being disappointed, though, she was even more delighted.
“Oh! Oh my goodness, I see now! I’ve made a perfect fool of myself! Well, that parsonage down at Delaford, it’s got five rooms on the ground floor, fifteen beds total, you know! Far nicer than your little cottage at Barton! We’ll nag the Colonel to fix it all up nice and cosy for Edward and Lucy before they move in, you’ll see. I promise you, I’ll be down there visiting them before Michaelmas, and Lucy will be right there in that parsonage, settled happy as can be. You mark my words.”
Elinor smiled, a soft, quiet thing. She thought, deep down, that Edward and Lucy would not need to wait two or three months at all. Mrs. Jennings was almost certainly right.

Edward barely remembered the walk from Colonel Brandon’s lodgings to Bartlett’s Buildings, his chest so swollen with unaccustomed joy it felt as if he might float off the pavement entirely. When he burst through Lucy’s door, his grin so wide it ached, she could tell at a glance his dearest hopes were finally realized. The next day, when Mrs. Jennings trundled round with her congratulations, Lucy was still so giddy she swore she had never seen Edward in such high spirits in all the years of their secret, hard-won engagement.

Her own happiness was no less unshakable. She laughed and nodded along to Mrs. Jennings’ giddy plans, already certain the two of them would be settled cozily in Delaford Parsonage long before Michaelmas came. Far from holding back her gratitude to Elinor, she sang her praises with unreserved warmth, declaring she believed Miss Dashwood would move heaven and earth for anyone she truly cared for, and no kindness from her now or in future would ever surprise her. As for Colonel Brandon? She already worshipped him like a living saint, and resolved he should be treated as one in all worldly matters too, his tithes raised to the very highest possible sum, and that at Delaford she would make free use of his carriage, his best dairy cows, and his plumpest, finest poultry whenever she pleased.

A full week had passed since John Dashwood called at Berkeley Street, and in all that time neither he nor Fanny had sent more than one passing verbal enquiry after her “indisposition” following the discovery of Edward’s secret engagement. Elinor knew social courtesy demanded she pay Fanny a formal visit. It was an obligation she loathed, and none of her companions offered her any encouragement to go: Marianne not only refused to come along herself, she begged Elinor not to go at all, too proud to put on polite airs for the woman who had so cruelly slighted them. Mrs. Jennings, who would have placed her own carriage at Elinor’s disposal at a moment’s notice, disliked Fanny so heartily that not even her curiosity to see Fanny’s mortified face, nor her giddy urge to side openly with Edward and affront her, could overcome her reluctance to share a room with her. So Elinor set out alone, dreading every step, at risk of being left tête-à-tête with a woman her friends had far less cause to despise than she.

The maid told her Mrs. Dashwood was not at home, but before the carriage could turn away from the house, John Dashwood happened to step out the front door. He lit up with feigned delight at the sight of her, declaring he had been just on his way to call at Berkeley Street, and insisted on leading her back upstairs to the drawing room, promising Fanny would be overjoyed to see her.

The room was empty.
“Fanny must be resting in her chamber,” he said. “I’ll fetch her in a moment. She will not be the least bit displeased to see you, far from it—you and Marianne have always been such favourites of hers. Why could Marianne not come today?”
Elinor offered a polite excuse for her sister.
“I am glad to see you alone,” he replied, lowering himself into an armchair. “I have so much to ask you. This living of Colonel Brandon’s—can it possibly be true? Has he really given it to Edward? I heard of it only yesterday by chance, and was coming straight to you to confirm the story.”
“It is perfectly true. Colonel Brandon has presented Edward to the Delaford living.”
“Good heavens, that is astonishing! No relation between them, no connection at all! And livings fetch such a price these days—what is the value of this one, exactly?”
“About two hundred pounds a year.”
“Two hundred a year! Why, if the late incumbent had been old and frail, likely to die any day, he could have sold the next presentation for fourteen hundred pounds at the very least! What a fool he was not to sell it before the man passed! A man of his sense, too! I cannot fathom such improvidence. Though, come to think of it, I am sure the truth is this: Edward is only holding the living temporarily, until the young man the Colonel really sold the presentation to comes of age. Aye, that is it, you may depend on it.”
Elinor contradicted him flatly, explaining she had personally carried the Colonel’s offer to Edward, and knew the full terms of the gift. He was forced to concede she could not be mistaken.
“It is truly astonishing,” he cried, when she finished. “What on earth could the Colonel’s motive possibly be?”
“A very simple one: to be of use to Mr. Ferrars.”
“Well, well. Edward is the luckiest man alive, no doubt of it. But you must not mention this to Fanny, will you? I have broken the news to her already, and she bears it very well, but she would hate for it to be gossiped about beyond the family.”
Elinor bit her tongue to keep from pointing out that a secure income for her own brother, which would never take a single penny from her or her little boy Harry, ought to be a thing Fanny could bear with perfect composure.
“Mrs. Ferrars does not know a word of this yet,” John went on, dropping his voice to a sombre, confidential hush, “and I think we ought to keep it hidden from her for as long as we possibly can. When the marriage takes place, I fear she must hear the whole truth.”
“But why such caution?” Elinor asked. “It is not as if Mrs. Ferrars could ever take pleasure in knowing her son finally has enough money to live on, of course—no one could expect that. But after the way she behaved, after she cast him off for ever and bade everyone around her shun him too, how can she possibly care anything that happens to him? She cannot be so weak as to throw away the comfort of a child, yet still cling to the anxieties of a parent.”
“Ah, Elinor, your reasoning is sound enough, but it ignores human nature,” John said, shaking his head. “When Edward’s wretched match finally happens, his mother will grieve for him just as much as if she never discarded him at all. We must hide any news that might speed that dreadful day. Mrs. Ferrars is one of the most affectionate mothers who ever lived.”
Elinor said no more, and fell silent.
“We are thinking now,” John said after a short pause, his tone grave and deliberate, “of arranging for Robert to marry Miss Morton instead.”
Elinor smiled faintly at his self-important tone, and replied calmly, “I suppose the lady herself has little choice in the matter.”
“Choice? What can you possibly mean by that?”
“Only that from your manner of speaking, it seems it makes no difference to Miss Morton whether she marries Edward or Robert.”
“Of course there is no difference. Robert will be treated as the eldest son in every practical respect now, and both are perfectly agreeable young men. I do not know that one is superior to the other by any measure.”
John fell quiet for a moment, then leaned over to take Elinor’s hand, his voice dropping to a dramatic whisper.
“One thing I may assure you, my dear sister, and it will gratify you, I know. I have it on the best authority, straight from Fanny, who heard it from Mrs. Ferrars herself, that of the two possible engagements Edward might have made—you understand me—the one to you would have been infinitely preferable. It would have given her less than half the vexation Lucy’s attachment has caused. She said it would have been the least of two evils, and she would gladly settle for nothing worse than that now. But all that is quite out of the question, of course, not to be mentioned or thought of. But I wanted to tell you, because I knew it would please you. Not that you have any reason to regret, mind you. You will do exceedingly well, no doubt of it. Colonel Brandon has been calling on you a great deal lately, has he not?”
Elinor’s head was spinning, her nerves taut enough to snap, and she was more than relieved when the door opened and Robert Ferrars sauntered into the room. After a few idle moments of small talk, John Dashwood remembered he had not yet told Fanny Elinor was there, and slipped out of the room to fetch her. Elinor was left alone with Robert, whose bright, careless self-complacency as he reaped all the benefits of his mother’s favour at his disinherited brother’s expense, earned only her deepest disdain.
Barely two minutes had passed when Robert began to speak of Edward. He had heard of the living too, and was bursting with curiosity. Elinor repeated the details just as she had told them to John, and the effect on Robert, while very different, was no less striking. He laughed immoderately, the idea of the stiff, awkward Edward being a clergyman and living in a tiny drafty parsonage diverting him past all bounds. When he added the silly image of Edward in a white surplice, reading banns for a pair of random village common folk, he thought there could be nothing more ridiculous on earth.
Elinor sat perfectly still, her face grave and unsmiling, staring at him with a look of quiet contempt he was far too vain to notice. When he finally wiped the tears of laughter from his eyes, he grew serious.
“We may treat it as a joke, but upon my soul it is a most grave business. Poor Edward! He is ruined for ever. I am very sorry for him, he is a good-hearted fellow, as well-meaning as any man alive. Poor Edward, his manners never were the best. But we are not all born with the same ease and address, are we? I was absolutely thunderstruck when the engagement came out. I told my mother straight away, ‘Madam, if Edward marries that girl, I shall never see him again.’ That is exactly what I said. Poor Edward! He has shut himself out of all decent society for ever. If I had known of the attachment a few hours sooner, I could have talked him out of it easily, told him exactly what a disgrace he was bringing on himself. But it was too late, I was not in the way to interfere. Now it is all over. He will be half-starved in that little parsonage for the rest of his days, you may be sure of it.”
The door opened before Elinor could reply, and Fanny Dashwood stepped into the room. Though she never spoke of Edward’s misfortune outside her immediate family, Elinor could see the embarrassment on her face, and the forced, stilted cordiality of her greeting. She even went so far as to feign sorrow that Elinor and her sister would be leaving town so soon, that she had hoped to spend more time with them. John Dashwood followed her in, gazing at her with adoring pride as if every fake, gracious word she spoke to Elinor was the height of kindness and grace.

Their final brief stop in Harley Street passed uneventfully: Elinor accepted her brother John’s over-cheerful congratulations that the sisters had made such good progress toward Barton without spending a penny of their own money, as well as his news that Colonel Brandon would be following them to Cleveland in a day or two’s time. That last visit drew their town-based family interactions to a close entirely. Fanny offered a faint, half-hearted invitation for them to stop by Norland any time they happened to be passing by—an event more unlikely than any other fate that could befall them, as both parties well knew—while John took Elinor aside to make a warmer, quieter promise that he would ride out to visit her the very second she was settled at Delaford. Elinor could not help but smile at the odd collective joke their social circle had fallen into: everyone seemed determined to cast Delaford as her inevitable future home. It was the very last place on earth she wished to live or even visit, and the absurdity of it felt all the sharper when Lucy, too, pressed her for a visit to the Delaford parsonage the moment the two of them parted in London.

The first days of April had barely broken pale dawn when two carriages set out from Hanover Square and Berkeley Street, prearranged to meet at a designated stretch of the high road. They would take two full days to make the journey, to spare Charlotte Palmer and her small baby too much jostling, while Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon rode ahead at a faster pace, planning to join the rest of the party at Cleveland shortly after their arrival. Marianne, who had ached for weeks to escape London, found she could not bring herself to leave without sobbing, once the final moment came. This was the very house where she had last let herself believe Willoughby loved her, where every bright, giddy, private plan they had woven together had lived before it burned to ash forever. To drive away from a city where he still walked its streets, chasing new acquaintances and new schemes that she would never be part of, felt like a small second goodbye to the ghost of the life she had almost had.

Elinor’s relief, by contrast, was unshakable. There was no one left in London she would miss, no more awkward forced displays of Lucy’s false friendship to endure, no risk of Marianne crossing paths with Willoughby on some accidental street stroll while he paraded his new wife. She looked ahead with quiet, soft hope to the quiet of Barton, where the slow, gentle air would stitch Marianne’s frayed peace of mind back together, and let her own quiet, tender hopes for the future settle steady.

The journey passed without a single mishap. On the second day, they crossed the border into Somerset, the county Marianne’s imagination had flipped between reverent longing and sharp, painful avoidance for months, and by mid-morning of the third day, their carriage rolled up the drive to Cleveland. The large, modern home sat nestled on a sloping green lawn, its sprawling pleasure grounds dotted with winding gravel paths, old fir trees, blooming mountain ashes, and drooping acacias that formed a thick, leafy screen to hide the service buildings from view. No grand formal park surrounded it, but the grounds stretched for acres, shrubberies newly green with spring leaves, wooded walks shaded and quiet.

Marianne’s chest tightened the second she stepped over the threshold. They were only eighty miles from Barton, and barely thirty miles from Combe Magna, Willoughby’s estate. Before any of her companions noticed she had slipped away—while the rest crowded around Charlotte to show her baby off to the housekeeper—she stole out through the winding, budding shrubberies, climbing all the way to the Grecian temple perched on the highest rise of the property. She leaned against its marble rail, staring out over the rolling green hills stretching southeast until her eyes burned, convinced if she squinted hard enough she could just catch a glint of Combe Magna’s roofs far off on the farthest ridge of the horizon. That sharp, precious ache, the misery that felt almost like joy, wrapped around her as she wept. When she wandered back to the house by a different path, grass damp under her boots, she resolved to spend every spare hour of her stay wandering these grounds alone, no one to pity her, no one to pester her to distract herself from the soft, lingering grief she cherished.

She returned just in time to join the rest of the party as they set out to explore the immediate grounds. The rest of the morning drifted slow and soft: they wandered the walled kitchen garden, admiring the pink buds on the fruit trees while the gardener grumbled about late spring blights, dawdled through the greenhouse where Charlotte laughed at Marianne’s pout when she spotted her favorite potted flowers nipped black by lingering frost, and visited the poultry yard to listen to the dairy maid bemoan hens abandoning their nests, foxes snatching chicks, and the entire promising new brood of vanishing. Marianne had planned nothing but long, sunlit walks for her entire stay, so she was completely caught off guard when a heavy, steady rain began pouring the second they finished dinner. She had been counting on a twilight walk back to the Grecian temple, and even a light damp chill would not have deterred her, but the rain fell so thick she could barely see ten feet from the front door.

The small party settled in for a quiet evening. Mrs. Palmer doted on her baby, Mrs. Jennings worked at her carpet stitching, they chattered about the friends they had left behind in London, sorted through Lady Middleton’s upcoming social engagements, and wondered if Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon had even made it as far as Reading that night. Elinor joined their conversation gently, while Marianne, as was her habit, quickly tracked down the tucked-away library no one else in the Palmer family ever used, and disappeared into the pages of a book. Charlotte Palmer was the warmest of hosts, open and unreserved, if a little scatterbrained and short on formal polite manners—her sweet, pretty face and genuine kindness made every small flaw endearing, her folly never cruel or conceited, though Elinor could not help but find her constant, bubbling laughter a faint, faint irritant.

The two gentlemen arrived late the following day, just in time for a very delayed dinner, and their arrival breathed new life into the party after an entire rain-soaked morning that had left them nearly out of things to discuss. Elinor had never been sure what to make of Mr. Palmer before, but in his own home he proved perfectly courteous to all his guests, only occasionally dry and sarcastic to his wife and mother-in-law, and capable of being perfectly pleasant company. He was only ever unkind when he put on airs of being far more clever and refined than everyone around him, fussy about his meals, inconsistent with his hours, pretending to disdain his baby while doting on it in private, and wasting half every morning at billiards instead of tending to his estates. All in all, Elinor liked him far better than she had expected, but she found she did not mind liking him no more than that: his selfishness, vanity, and love of luxury only made her think all the more fondly of Edward Ferrars, with his generous heart, simple tastes, and soft, quiet shyness.

Colonel Brandon, who had just returned from a trip to Dorsetshire, soon pulled Elinor aside to speak of Edward. He treated her like an old, trusted confidante, describing the small Delaford parsonage in great detail, listing all its flaws, and laying out the repairs he planned to oversee himself before Edward took up the post. He lit up at the sight of Elinor, even after only ten days apart, hung on every word she said, and deferred to her every opinion so openly that Mrs. Jennings’s long-held conviction that he was hopelessly in love with Elinor almost felt plausible. But Elinor knew better: she watched his face when Marianne complained of a scratchy throat and aching head, the faint, unspoken flash of alarm that no one else noticed, the way he tensed like he would run for a doctor before anyone else had even registered she was unwell. It was Marianne he loved, quiet and loyal as always, and Elinor smiled to herself that she was the only one who could see it.

On the third and fourth evenings of their stay, the rain cleared, leaving the air soft and cool at twilight. Marianne went out for long, wandering walks, not just on the dry gravel paths, but all the way out to the wildest, oldest parts of the grounds, where the grass grew long and soaked from the earlier downpour, the ancient trees twisting their branches overhead like a shaded canopy. She stayed out long after the sun set, tramping around in wet shoes and stockings, and by the time she came back, she was burning up with a terrible cold. For a day or two she brushed it off, insisting a single good night’s rest would cure her completely, but soon her fever spiked, her cough grew harsh, and her limbs ached too badly for her to deny how unwell she was. Everyone offered their favorite home remedies, all of which she stubbornly turned down, until Elinor gently persuaded her to take a single, mild, simple remedy before she finally climbed into bed for the night.

Marianne dragged herself out of bed at her usual early hour the next morning, her cheeks flushed and her hands ice-cold, and insisted to every concerned inquiry that she was perfectly well. She even forced herself to pick up her usual embroidery hoop and the volume of poetry she’d been halfway through, determined to prove her words true—but the day that followed did little to back her claims. She sat shivering by the parlour fire for hours, eyes glazing over on lines she could not parse, then dragged herself to the sofa to lie languid and listless, too worn out even to sigh. When she finally stumbled off to bed long before her usual time, clearly growing sicker by the minute, Colonel Brandon could only stare in bewilderment at Elinor’s apparent calm. She had tended Marianne all day against her sister’s grumpy protests, forced her to take the cooling tonics the apothecary had recommended, and clung, just as Marianne did, to the hope that a full night of sleep would set everything right.

That hope shattered entirely when Marianne passed a feverish, restless night, tossing and turning and murmuring incoherently. When she tried to drag herself out of bed at dawn, she had to admit she had not the strength to sit up, and crawled back under the covers of her own accord. Elinor did not hesitate to take Mrs. Jennings’ advice and send for Mr. Harris, the local apothecary the Palmers swore by.

When he arrived, he examined Marianne carefully, and told Elinor he was confident she would recover in a few days—but his offhand mention that her fever trended putrid, and that the disorder was contagious, sent Mrs. Palmer into immediate hysterics for the sake of her infant son. Mrs. Jennings, who had suspected Marianne’s illness was far more serious than Elinor would admit from the very start, confirmed her daughter’s fears at once, and insisted Charlotte leave the house with the baby that very instant. Mr. Palmer teased his wife for being foolish at first, but her anxious pestering wore him down, and within an hour of Mr. Harris’ arrival, Charlotte, her boy, and his nurse were on the road to the home of a Palmer relation a few miles outside Bath. Mr. Palmer promised he would join them in a day or two, at her desperate pleading, and even tried to persuade his own mother to come with him—but Mrs. Jennings would not budge. With a warmth that made Elinor’s throat ache with gratitude, she declared she would not leave Cleveland for as long as Marianne was unwell, determined to step in and care for the girl as if she were the Dashwood sisters’ own mother, far away in Barton. She proved a tireless, helpful nurse, happy to split every late shift with Elinor, and her decades of experience tending to sick household members pulled Elinor through more than one exhausting hour.

Marianne, meanwhile, was too weak and ill to feel anything but dull, universal ache. She had clung to the hope that she would be well enough to leave for Barton at the end of the week, the trip where she and Elinor were meant to surprise their mother at the cottage door the next morning—and that lost prospect made every twinge of her fever feel sharper. She lamented the delay in every quiet, ragged sentence she spoke, even as Elinor assured her their journey home would only be pushed back a few short days, a belief Elinor truly held at that point.

For two days, Marianne’s state barely shifted. She was no worse, but she was no better. Mr. Palmer finally left to join his wife, persuaded at last by Colonel Brandon to keep his promise to her—and the Colonel, who had been on the verge of leaving too to give the two sisters privacy, was stopped in his tracks by Mrs. Jennings’ quick wit. She told him outright that she needed him to stay: every evening, when Elinor was upstairs with Marianne, she would have no one to play piquet with, and she would be bored out of her mind. He did not pretend to demur for long. He had no wish to leave Marianne, ill and unknowing of how deeply he cared for her, and Mr. Palmer was relieved to leave the house knowing there was a steady, level-headed man on the grounds to help Elinor in an emergency.

Marianne never learned that the owners of Cleveland had all but abandoned their home seven days after their arrival for her sake. She never asked after Mrs. Palmer, and her complete indifference to the subject saved Elinor from having to explain it all.

Mr. Harris still insisted Marianne would be well soon, but everyone else’s hope frayed thin fast. Mrs. Jennings declared early on that she was sure Marianne would not recover, and Colonel Brandon, who spent most of his days wandering the grounds alone with his thoughts, could not fight the dark, sinking fear that he would never see Marianne well, never get the chance to tell her how he felt.

For a bright moment on the third day, that fear lifted. Mr. Harris arrived that morning and declared her markedly improved: her pulse was stronger, every symptom trending in the right direction. Elinor’s spirits soared, and she fired off a letter to her mother playing down the severity of the illness, certain Marianne would be well enough to travel home in a matter of days.

But the day did not end as brightly as it began. As dusk fell, Marianne’s fever spiked again. She grew restless, heavy, and flushed far worse than she had been all week. Elinor told herself it was just the exhaustion of sitting up while her bed was remade, gave her the prescribed cordial, and watched her sink into what she thought would be a restorative sleep. She refused to leave her side, determined to watch over her through the whole rest. Mrs. Jennings went to bed unusually early, the house maid who had been helping nurse wandered off to the housekeeper’s room for a snack, and Elinor was entirely alone with her sister.

Marianne’s rest grew more and more disturbed. She tossed from side to side, letting out soft, incoherent little moans, and Elinor was just about to wake her from the clearly painful slumber when a random creak from the hallway made Marianne jolt upright, eyes wild and glassy with fever.

“Is mama coming?” she shrieked.

“Not yet,” Elinor said, tucking the blankets around her and hiding how her own hands were shaking. “But she will be soon, I promise. Barton is a long way away, remember?”

“She must not go through London,” Marianne babbled, thrashing. “I will never see her again if she goes through London.”

Panic coiled tight in Elinor’s chest. She pressed her fingers to Marianne’s pulse, and it was fluttering far too fast, far too faint. She knew instantly she had to send for Mr. Harris at once, and send a rider to Barton to fetch their mother. The first person she thought to turn to for help was Colonel Brandon. She rang for the maid to sit with Marianne, then ran down to the drawing room, where she knew he would still be sitting, long after everyone else had gone to bed.

There was no time for politeness. She laid out all her fears, all her plans, and to her overwhelming relief, he did not waste a single minute. Before she could even finish suggesting he find a reliable man to ride to Barton, he offered to go himself. No persuasion was needed. Elinor thanked him through burning, grateful tears, and while he sent his own servant racing to wake the stables for fresh post horses and send a messenger for Mr. Harris, she scribbled a short, urgent note to her mother.

There could not have been a better person to fetch her. His steady judgement would guide her frantic mother the whole ride back, his quiet kindness would soothe her when she got the terrible news, and Elinor could not imagine a safer, more caring companion for her on that desperate journey. He did not waste a single second. The horses were harnessed even faster than he had expected, and he pressed Elinor’s hand one solemn, wordless time before climbing into the carriage, and disappearing down the drive into the dark night.

It was almost midnight when Elinor went back up to Marianne’s room to wait for the apothecary. The night stretched on for what felt like an eternity: Marianne raved in delirium, calling her mother’s name over and over, and Elinor was torn apart by the thought that she had wasted days playing down Marianne’s illness, that they had waited too long, that her mother would arrive only to find her youngest daughter gone. Mr. Harris did not show up until after five in the morning. Though he was shocked by the sudden turn for the worse, he insisted the danger was not yet fatal, adjusted her treatment, and left Elinor just enough fragile hope to keep her standing.

When Mrs. Jennings woke up and learned what had happened, her old, grim fears came flooding back, and she could not even pretend to offer Elinor hope that Marianne would pull through. She grieved openly for the kind, bright young girl she had grown so fond of over the past three months, and Elinor’s heart ached all the more for it.

Mr. Harris returned that afternoon, but his new medicines had done little to break the fever. Marianne was quieter now, but not lucid, trapped in a heavy, unresponsive stupor. Elinor begged to call in another doctor, but he insisted there was no need, that the next new treatment he planned would work. It was almost noon when Elinor, holding her sister’s wrist, thought she felt a faint, steady thrum in Marianne’s pulse that had not been there an hour before. She waited, watching, checking again and again, too scared to say a word to Mrs. Jennings for fear she would be mistaken. Soon enough, the signs of improvement multiplied: Marianne’s breath slowed and evened out, the flush faded from her cheeks, and she opened her eyes to look directly at Elinor, clear and lucid, though still terribly weak.

By the time Mr. Harris returned at four o’clock, he could do nothing but congratulate her, and declare Marianne entirely out of danger.

Elinor did not laugh, or cheer, or make any loud show of joy. Her relief was quiet, heavy, brimming over with gratitude. She sat by Marianne’s bed the rest of the afternoon, tending to her every small need, watching her sleep peacefully, counting the minutes until her mother and Colonel Brandon would arrive. The night was cold and stormy: wind howled around the eaves, rain lashed against the windows, but Elinor barely noticed. She knew the two travelers would be pushing their horses as hard as they could, and every second dragged by too slow.

At eight o’clock, she heard the distant clatter of hooves on the drive. She ran to the window, and there was the carriage, its lamps flaring bright in the rain, pulled by four fresh, panting horses—they had changed teams again and again, rushing the whole way from Barton, her mother no doubt beside herself with fear.

Elinor did not wait. She left the maid sitting by Marianne, and hurried down the stairs, her legs shaking, desperate to tell her mother that Marianne was safe, that everything would be alright. She burst through the drawing room door, breathless, ready to fling herself into her mother’s arms—

And saw only Willoughby, standing by the fire.

Elinor flinched back so hard her breath caught, horror coiling tight in her chest the second her eyes landed on him. Her first instinct was to flee the room entirely, her fingers already curled around the doorknob to twist it open, before he lunged forward, his tone sharper with urgency than entreaty.
“Miss Dashwood—half an hour. Ten minutes. I beg you, stay.”
“No, sir,” she replied, steady as a rock. “I will not. Your business cannot be with me. The servants must have forgotten to tell you Mr. Palmer is not at home.”
“Had they told me Mr. Palmer and every last one of his relations was at the devil, it would not have turned me from this door,” he burst out, voice raw with vehemence. “My business is with you. Only you.”
“With me?” Elinor stared, utterly bewildered. “Well then—speak quickly, if you can. And do not be so violent.”
“Sit down, and I will be both.”
She hesitated, uncertain of what to do. The sharp fear pricked her that Colonel Brandon might walk in and find her shut alone with Willoughby, but she had long promised herself she would hear whatever explanation he might one day offer Marianne, and curiosity warred with her sense of honor to hold her still. After a breath’s pause, she reasoned the faster she complied, the faster this strange, tense encounter would end, and she walked silently to the table and sat. He dropped into the chair opposite, and for thirty long seconds, neither of them spoke a word.
“Please be quick, sir,” Elinor said, impatience fringing her tone. “I have no time to spare.”
He sat hunched, lost in deep meditation, and seemed not to hear her at all.
“Your sister,” he said, abruptly, a moment later. “The servants told me she’s out of danger. God be praised. But is it true? Is it really true?”
Elinor could not bring herself to speak, and he repeated the question, more desperate than before.
“For God’s sake, tell me—is she out of danger, or is she not?”
“We hope she is.”
He shot to his feet and paced the length of the room, boots thudding on the floorboards.
“Had I known that half an hour ago—but since I am here,” he said, forcing bright, hollow cheer as he dropped back into his chair, “what does it matter? Just once, Miss Dashwood—and it will surely be the last time—let us speak honestly. I am in no mood for games. Tell me,” he added, a hot flush creeping up his cheeks, “do you think I am more of a knave, or a fool?”
Elinor stared at him, more stunned than ever. She began to suspect he was drunk—there was no other way to explain this unannounced, uninvited visit, this wild, unhinged demeanor. She stood at once.
“Mr. Willoughby, I advise you to return to Combe at once. I have no leisure to stay with you any longer. Whatever business you have with me will be far better recollected and explained tomorrow, when you are of sound mind.”
“I understand you,” he replied, with a quiet, knowing smile, his voice perfectly even. “Yes, everyone would assume I am very drunk. A pint of porter with my cold beef at Marlborough was enough to throw me off my wits, according to that logic.”
“At Marlborough?” Elinor cried, more lost than ever trying to parse his purpose.
“Yes. I left London at eight this morning, and the only ten minutes I spent outside my chaise the whole day was to stop for that very nuncheon.”
His calm bearing, the sharp, clear intelligence in his eyes, convinced Elinor that whatever unpardonable folly had driven him all the way to Cleveland, it was not intoxication. She paused to collect her thoughts, then spoke.
“Mr. Willoughby, you must know—certainly I do—that after all that has passed between us, you forcing yourself into my presence like this demands an extraordinary excuse. What do you mean by it?”
“I mean,” he said, fierce and sincere, “if I can, to make you hate me just one degree less than you do right now. I mean to offer you some kind of explanation, some kind of apology for the past. I want to lay my whole heart bare to you, prove that while I have always been a blockhead, I have not always been a deliberate, unthinking rascal—so that Marianne can forgive me, properly, on fair terms, not out of soft pity alone.”
“Is that truly why you came?”
“On my soul, it is,” he said, and the warmth in his tone brought back every trace of the charming, lighthearted Willoughby they had all loved in Devonshire, and against her better judgment, Elinor believed he was sincere.
“If that is all, you may rest easy already. Marianne does forgive you. She forgave you long ago.”
“Has she?” he exclaimed, that same hungry, eager tone in his voice. “Then she forgave me far too soon, before she knew the full extent of my cowardice. But she will forgive me again, for the right reasons. Will you listen to me now?”
Elinor nodded, assenting.
“I do not know,” he said, after a long pause where Elinor waited and he turned his thoughts over one by one, “what story you have heard of my behavior toward your sister, what cruel, selfish motive you have ascribed to me. You may not think better of me when I am done, but it is worth trying. I will tell you everything. When I first grew intimate with your family in Devonshire, I had no hidden agenda, no grand plan—only to pass my time there more pleasantly than I ever had before. Your sister’s lovely face, her wild, passionate, generous manners—they could not help but please me. And from almost the first day, she cared for me so openly, so unreservedly that it staggers me now to think I was ever so vain and shallow as to take that devotion for granted. At first, I will own, her affection only fed my ego. I did not care for her happiness, only my own amusement. I gave in to every selfish impulse I had always been too indulgent of, and set out to win her regard without ever meaning to return it.”
Elinor’s eyes flashed hot, angry contempt, and she cut him off. “This is hardly worth telling, Mr. Willoughby, neither for you to speak nor for me to listen. A beginning as base as that can lead to nothing but pain. Do not make me suffer through any more of this.”
